    - Not taken: (2) a `DELETE /dns/key-record` route, a route, an authz row and a second write a rotation; (3) the server retiring a receiver's older keys 2 TTLs after a newer one publishes, which strands a receiver whose successor never went live and has the server choose which key it serves.
    - Raised by the build (MAIL-M7-13; the build's ledger, QD-868).

444. **A search takes spam and trash only when asked, by an explicit include: a `SearchRequest` field, the MCP tool's `include` and `ur search --include` (option 1, the recommendation).**
    - Question: §15.7 has a member's mail search leave out spam and trash unless asked, and the device-side join that does so is built, but §5.4's `SearchRequest`, §16.10's `search` arguments and `ur search`'s flags named no way to ask, and IMAP SEARCH inside a Trash or Spam folder needs the engine to search it.
    - Answer: §5.4's `SearchRequest` gains an explicit include of the mail folders the default leaves out (`IncludeSpam` and `IncludeTrash`, or one folder list), which the engine passes to the device-side join; the MCP `search` tool gains `include: [spam, trash]`, its outputSchema unchanged, and `ur search` gains `--include spam,trash`, both mapping onto that field; IMAP SEARCH sets it from the selected folder; without it every search keeps §15.7's default (§5.4, §15.7, §16.10).
    - Cost: one request field, one tool argument and one flag.
    - Not taken: (2) a filter, `fields{folder: spam|trash}`, a field no index row holds; (3) only IMAP SEARCH asking, so no agent could find a message spam filtering misplaced.
    - Raised by the build (SEARCH-M10-28; the build's ledger, QD-898).

445. **The mail formats' bytes are as `protocol/mail.proto` and `protocol/MAIL-FORMAT.md` landed them, where QD-432's rows and its accepted readings part (option 1, the recommendation).**
    - Question: QD-432's fill answered three question files in one entry: plan-MAIL-format-bytes' rows, written against a mail.proto with no `MailDeposit` yet, plan-SMTP-wire-gaps' outbox, and mail-proto-format-readings' sixteen items accepted as landed; the rows and the landed bytes differed in places no item named, and in a few a row and an accepted item spoke to the same point.
    - Answer: §15 takes the formats as they stand. From the rows: `MailHint.host = 2`; a same-network deposit sets `version`, `mail_from` and `recipients` only; `MailIndexSegment{entries = 1}` and `MailFlagSegment{ops = 1}`, with no version; `MailFlagOp`'s `op 2, set 3, label 4, sidecar_id 7`; `r/`'s `MailGroupRef`; `MailForward.verified_time_ms = 3`; `MailProverRecord.measure_time_ms = 3` and its rate `data_bytes_per_s`; `MailOutcome`'s `byte_count 3` to `update_time_ms 8`; `MailIngress`'s drop order, listings held to the first 16 of at most 12 B, then `ptr_name`, then listings from the end; and `o/` as `u32be(length) || Signed || message` around `SmtpOutboxEntry`. Where a row and an accepted item part, the item's point stands and the row's numbers fill the rest: `MailOutcomeRecord` holds one `MailOutcome` a domain, with `tls_level` at 9; `MailFlagOp`'s clock is one `hlc` at 5, with 6 reserved; `SmtpOutboxEntry.approval_not_after` is 8; `MailDeposit.mail_from` is bytes. `IndexEntry.time`, `MailBurner.created` and `MailProverRecord.smtp_data_proof_bytes` keep §15.7's names, a field no design list names taking the row's spelling; `MailHint.kind` is the enum `MailHintKind` (0 unspecified, 1 DEPOSIT, 2 RECEIVER_NEEDED); `SmtpOutboxEntry` is declared in mail.proto, and SMTP-M9-09 registers only `SIGNED_SMTP_OUTBOX` and its purpose; a listing over 12 B is left out, never cut (§15.5, §15.7, §15.8, §15.15, §14.5, §6.11).
    - Cost: none now: nothing is released, and only the formats' own tests read these bytes before M7's exit review freezes them.
    - Not taken: (2) correcting any of them by number before M7's exit, a regeneration and a vector rewrite each.
    - Raised by the build (MAIL-M7-01; the build's ledger, QD-912, which settles QD-432's fill).

446. **go-smtp's in-tree fork carries a second small change, so every session accepts RFC 5321's bare `RCPT TO:<Postmaster>` (option 1, the recommendation).**
    - Question: RFC 5321 §4.5.1 says the bare `RCPT TO:<Postmaster>` MUST be supported, but go-smtp's path parser requires an `@` and answers `501` before a session sees the recipient, on every receiver and on the platform listener, and the in-tree fork (§4.7) had been kept to D-MAIL-3's `ServeConn`.
    - Answer: the fork carries a second change beside `ServeConn`: for RCPT, `parsePath` returns `postmaster` for a bare `<Postmaster>` with no domain, matched without regard to case, where upstream answers 501, every other path keeping upstream's rules; each session maps the bare form to its own domain's postmaster before its recipient rules run, the platform listener to `postmaster@<root>` and a receiver's session to postmaster at the domain it serves; URNETWORK.md names the change beside `ServeConn`, about ten lines and a test, upstreamable on its own (§15.3, §15.4, §15.5, D-MAIL-3).
    - Cost: one more change to carry across go-smtp updates.
    - Not taken: (2) leaving it, a MUST of RFC 5321 unmet on every receiver and the platform listener, though report senders write full addresses.
    - Raised by the build (MAIL-M5-03; the build's ledger, QD-921).

## The build's owner questions on the MCP server, harness integration, records and the team directory (2026-11-06 to 2026-11-16)

The design-records track for §15 and §16 folded the answers below on 2026-11-16, each citing its ledger id: the owner-class items the owner accepted or chose on 2026-11-06, the answers the owner decided one at a time from 2026-11-10 to 2026-11-14, and the recommendations of 2026-11-15 and 2026-11-16 that the owner's instruction of 2026-11-16 accepted. A decision that groups several answers carries its latest answer's date. §16's other items, the defect fixes the owner accepted on 2026-11-06 and the build's readings, fills and settled items, went into §16 as text with no number.

447. **A decision that no vault carries back reaches its requester as an `ApprovalDelivery`, sealed to the requester's ReaderKey (option 1, the recommendation, which the owner chose on 2026-11-06).**
    - Question: §16.8 returned a member's decision through a vault and activated the rest as `GrantActivate`, whose quorum needs a requests rule, so `mail_send`, `dns_set`, O-tier `evs_write`, `mail_alias_*` and §16.11's endorsement had no route back to a sidecar that reads neither a vault nor the team record, nor an admin's decision to a member.
    - Answer: `ApprovalDelivery{header, requester_sidecar_id, sealed}`, a §7.9 delta with SignedType 0x1110 that the approver's device files after it signs: `sealed` is the Signed `ApprovalDecision` or `ApprovalCancel`, HPKE-XWing to the requester's current ReaderKey (`gkm/rk/<sidecar_id>`, §10.5), so only the requester opens it, tighter than a seal to the config generation; it counts against `config_statements_per_day`, adds one api-7 clause (signer, `requester_sidecar_id`, size) and authorizes nothing, the requester still running §16.6's spend check; it serves tagged and ephemeral requesters, admins' approvals of other actions and §16.11's endorsement, so `filed_by` 3 keeps its meaning, and grants keep `GrantActivate` (§16.6, §16.8, §16.11, §7.9, §8.8, §6.11, §2.8's api-7).
    - Cost: one statement type, reserved in signed.proto before §7.9's records froze; api learns that a decision reached that sidecar; one log entry a decision, inside the daily statement limit.
    - Not taken: (2) a new entry kind in the approver's directory stream, team networks only, where every admitted sidecar, agents included, reads approver, requester and hashes; (3) grant-shaped decisions only, so cloud agents lose per-action approval and, once a run is tainted, send no mail and set no DNS at all.
    - Raised by the build (plan-RECORD; the build's ledger, QD-013).

448. **Every run of a harness process takes the union of that process's runs' taint, and the residual is stated (option 1, the recommendation, accepted with the build's owner items in bulk on 2026-11-06).**
    - Question: run identity rests on hook events any same-uid process can forge (§16.6), so a tainted agent could open a fresh run by a forged SessionStart and then forge the PermissionRequest owner decision 142 exists to refuse, and no FS row tried it.
    - Answer: the run file keeps the harness process's identity (pid and start time; local, never shown); every session or event under a harness process binds to the union of that process's runs, ended ones included, so a new run there starts with their taint, fsynced, until the process exits; only a SessionStart reporting a fresh start opens a new run, a session with no live harness ancestor binds nothing and reads tainted, and SessionEnd clears nothing; §16.6's monotone row reads "a new run starts untainted unless a run of its harness process is tainted", FS-27 gains forged SessionStart and SessionEnd steps, and §2.10 and §16.17 state the residual: a process that leaves the harness's tree (setsid, a launchd or systemd job, a second harness started detached) inherits nothing and can use what §5.5 leaves to any local process, so I-9 binds runs, not all of a user's processes (owner decision 47) (§16.6, D-MCP-16, §16.17, §2.10, FS-27).
    - Cost: `/clear` or a new chat in a long-lived window stays tainted until the harness restarts, so one bad read costs that process harness evidence, standing grants and approved recipients.
    - Not taken: (2) the same, but a new `ur mcp` relay starting clean, which an agent can spawn from its shell; (3) disclosing only, so any agent with a shell walks around owner decision 142's tainted-run rule.
    - Raised by the build (plan-MCP; the build's ledger, QD-018).

449. **`ur` in a cloud VM takes its auth key from a file the setup writes and its first hook unlinks, never from an environment variable (option 1, the recommendation, accepted with the build's owner items in bulk on 2026-11-06).**
    - Question: a cloud environment runs setup before a session's variables are set, skips it for cached sessions, loses everything only running when paused, and gives every command the agent runs the session's variables, so nothing enrolled in setup survives, a single-use key serves one session, §5.6's memory-only keychain dies at each pause, and a key in a variable reaches the agent.
    - Answer: the environment's setup step writes the whole key string to a mode 0600 file, which the platform's snapshot keeps; the `--project` cloud profile's committed SessionStart hook runs `ur connect --token-file <path> --ephemeral` before any other `ur` step, and `ur` reads the file, enrolls, unlinks it and erases its copy, so the key is gone from the running VM before the agent's first turn; `ur` reads no key from any environment variable and refuses to enroll if `ur-authkey-` appears in its own environment; the key is a Delegation key with `max_admits` above 1 and §7.8's 30-day cloud `not_after`, a single-use key serving where a platform runs setup per session; a cloud sidecar keeps its keychain in a 0600 file, so a restored VM rejoins until its client is reaped, after which `ur` stays Unenrolled, capture spools and a new session enrolls; §16.9 states the residual: anyone who uses the environment can read the key in its setup text, every snapshot holds it, and a copy admits fresh agents until the Delegation ends (§16.9, §5.6, §16.16, §16.17).
    - Cost: one flag, one hook step and a file keychain for cloud sidecars; the key sits in setup text everyone on that environment can read, and a session idle past the reap loses `ur` until a new session.
    - Not taken: (2) the key file kept for the session's life, which a same-user agent can copy whole; (3) an environment variable with cloud VMs exempt from I-7; (4) a single-use key or a member's approval per session, which stops unattended agents.
    - Raised by the build (plan-MCP; the build's ledger, QD-041).

450. **A search never fetches for itself: hints coalesce on the leading edge, and a hinted version not yet fetched counts as uncovered (option 1, the recommendation, accepted with the build's owner items in bulk on 2026-11-06).**
    - Question: §16.10's sync and Targets rows had a search bring a hinted repo current before phase 1, a fetch the search causes, while D-EVS-50, §11.14's trigger row and §2.8 say a search triggers none and desktops show evs neither, and FS-83 refuses the same fetch for indexers.
    - Answer: the first hint for a repo in a quiet `Search.HintCoalesce` interval fetches at once, and later hints in it wait for its end; a search reads only the cache; a version hinted but not fetched when a search starts counts as uncovered, so coverage reports it and it is never silently absent (FI-18); §16.10's sync row drops "brought current before phase 1", and its Targets row reads "a commit hinted before a search starts, unless inside a burst already coalescing, in that search's phase 1" (§16.10, §5.4's `HintCoalesce`).
    - Cost: a commit hinted inside a burst can miss a search by up to a minute, which coverage reports.
    - Not taken: (2) a search flushing pending hints on daemon hosts, so evs could time desktop searches from off-grid reads, against §2.8, D-EVS-50 and FI-23.
    - Raised by the build (plan-SEARCH; the build's ledger, QD-048).

451. **A tagged sidecar's worker announcement is set by whoever controls its deployment, with no record and no reader check (option 1, deployment policy, the recommendation, which the owner chose on 2026-11-06).**
    - Question: §16.11's announcements table had a tagged sidecar's worker item "set by its sponsor or an admin", but §7.9 has no per-sidecar field, and `SidecarAdmit`, `SponsorSet` and `Delegation` carry none, so the table read as a remote control no carrier delivers.
    - Answer: the clause reads as whoever controls the deployment: `ur directory worker on` or `off` on the sidecar's own host, by hand or from the script that provisions it, stored in that sidecar's local settings over `ServiceSettings.directory.workers`, with no record and no reader check, and the table says so; to stop a running agent's item from elsewhere a sponsor uses `Suspend` or a revocation (§8.8) (§16.11's announcements table).
    - Cost: a sponsor quiets a running cloud agent only by redeploying, suspending or revoking it; worker items hold no secret, so this is noise control only.
    - Not taken: (2) a signed per-sidecar setting, a config record, a §2.8 row and a reservation before M2's Gn-in; (3) dropping the clause, so a sponsor cannot quiet one agent without stopping it.
    - Raised by the build (plan-RECORD; the build's ledger, QD-115).

452. **Two harness config keys act, PII hashing from M3 and `claims` from M6, and the mock's other keys wait (option 1, the recommendation, accepted with the build's owner items in bulk on 2026-11-06).**
    - Question: the mock was the harness config's only key list, PII had neither a default nor a rule list, and §8.5 named a harness `reach:` block as a filer of requests, so which keys act, when, and what PII hashing means changed what readers see and what is in scope.
    - Answer: `record.redact.pii`, `off` or `hash`, default `hash`, from M3: email addresses and E.164 phone numbers become §16.7's marker with rule `pii-email` or `pii-phone`, each a redaction in the run's Coverage; `claims`, `{"default": auto|prompt|off, "projects": {<project key>: value}}`, from M6, read by CORE-M6-02 under D-CORE-18's rule; `agents.<label>` overrides those two keys only; `record.capture` is kept and ignored, capture staying §16.7's fixed set, since a narrowing control weakens R-MCP-4 and needs a person-only surface first; `reach`, `models` and `identity` are kept, ignored and warned about, and §8.5's harness `reach:` block files no request until a later design, as §8.5 and §1's row say (§16.7, §8.5, §1).
    - Cost: a small format; by default stored transcripts lose emails and phone numbers for good.
    - Not taken: (2) the same with `pii` off by default, PII unhashed in the vault for `EvsShare` subjects and the audit reader; (3) letting `record.capture` narrow too, which needs a person-only path and a Team refusal; (4) no vault config in v0, local flags only.
    - Raised by the build (plan-MCP; the build's ledger, QD-116).

453. **A person's statement about a change is an `attestation` entry, never an approval (option 1, the recommendation, accepted with the build's owner items in bulk on 2026-11-06).**
    - Question: product copy (P5:728) promised that "a person approved a change", but §16.8's approvals bind outward actions (§5.4), a commit is none, and no D- item amended the promise, so it stood with nothing behind it.
    - Answer: an `attestation` entry (RECORD.md's kind 29, section 11): a person's signed statement, reviewed, tested or approved, about named commits, filed by `ur record attest` on `/dev/tty` or in an app's UI, with an SSHSIG where the member holds a key; it sits outside the I-8 gate, is never called an approval, and P5:728's copy says what it is (§16.8's approvals; RECORD.md sections 6.3, 11 and 17).
    - Cost: one kind and one command; it shows who said so, not that a review happened, and it binds no merge.
    - Not taken: (2) a `SidecarActionKind` for changes, an approval with no effect to spend; (3) narrowing the copy to outward actions, so the client packet loses its main story.
    - Raised by the build (RECORD.md's review; the build's ledger, QD-127).

454. **A log admin's `ledger_scope` entry decides what a `record` repo copies, and no agent chooses a log (option 1, the recommendation, accepted with the build's owner items in bulk on 2026-11-06).**
    - Question: §16.8 gave `record` repos writers, but `record_append` names no log and §16.5's catalog is static, so nothing wrote a client ledger's entries, and who may write one, and whose entries reach readers wider than the team record's admins, was scope and trust.
    - Answer: a log admin's `ledger_scope` entry (RECORD.md's kind 43, section 6.5) names the repos and projects a ledger covers, and a sidecar with write on that log copies there each of those runs' entries bound for the team record, the team record's projection, never a vault body; no tool changes and no agent chooses a log, and `ur status` names each `record` log a daemon copies to, so a member can see it (§16.8's logs table; RECORD.md sections 6.3 and 6.5).
    - Cost: one new kind; each daemon follows every such log it can write; a log admin decides whose run projections reach that log's readers, and members see it only in `ur status`.
    - Not taken: (2) a log argument on `record_append`, so a prompt-injected agent could write into a client's ledger; (3) only log admins writing in v1, with no automatic partition.
    - Raised by the build (RECORD.md's review; the build's ledger, QD-138).

455. **Presence attestations verify over the create ceremony's client data against vendor roots a release pins, and a valid statement chaining to no listed root labels asserted (the recommendations: QD-137's, accepted with the build's owner items in bulk on 2026-11-06, QD-730's of 2026-11-12 and QD-885's of 2026-11-15).**
    - Question: §16.6 pinned FIDO MDS roots, whose terms bar copying or distributing metadata without a written agreement, while the pinned file would ship in every release of a public repo; `KeyRegister.attestation` held the bare attestation object, though every format but `none` signs the create ceremony's client data, which the object does not carry; and the drafted verifier refused a valid statement chaining to no pinned root, where QD-137 labeled it asserted.
    - Answer: a release pins only roots their vendors publish, and no MDS data: Apple's WebAuthn root for `apple`, Google's hardware attestation roots for `android-key`, and a short hand-kept list of vendor roots for `packed` and `tpm`, in one data file a release step refreshes. `KeyRegister.attestation` holds a canonical CBOR map of two byte strings, `{"attObj": the attestation object, "clientDataJSON": the create ceremony's client data}`, a CTAP2 client writing the JSON it hashed; the verifier checks that the client data's type is `webauthn.create` and its origin is on §16.6's table, checks each of the four formats over SHA-256 of the client data, and leaves the challenge unchecked, since the credential key in authData, which must equal `public_key`, binds the attestation to the registration. A statement that does not verify is refused; for `apple` and `android-key`, one vendor's root each, a chain failure once the root is pinned refuses, so a software key cannot pass as `apple`; for `packed` and `tpm` a statement that verifies but chains to no listed root, and any format whose pool is empty, labels asserted, as `fmt none` and an empty attestation do. The label changes no authorization, so a key outside the list loses only the word attested, and it rises to attested when a release pins its root, with no re-registration; api-7's words for a key registration name the origin and the challenge (§16.6's registration and labels rows, §2.8's api-7, §23.7).
    - Cost: fewer keys show attested, and a short list needs hand upkeep.
    - Not taken: for QD-137, (2) MDS-derived roots once cleared and (3) no roots in v0; for QD-730, (2) a proto message in the field and (3) the raw object, verifying `none` alone; for QD-885, (2) refusing every chain failure and (3) refusing only where the format's pool is non-empty.
    - Raised by the build (plan-MCP, MCP-M2-01 and MCP-M3-08; the build's ledger, QD-137, QD-730, QD-885).

456. **The file `ur record open` writes, and `ur record disclose --content` reads, is a new `DisclosureOpenings` message that no reader can take for a packet (option 2, the recommendation, decided on 2026-11-10).**
    - Question: RECORD-M6-06's `Open` wrote, and its `Disclose` read, a file whose format no section gave; the draft used a `DisclosurePacket` with no checkpoints, which `ur record verify` fails on its own and which invites a wrong verify.
    - Answer: a new record.proto message, `DisclosureOpenings {version, log_id, repeated Opening openings, repeated DisclosedKey keys, purpose}`, which cannot be mistaken for a `DisclosurePacket`; the vault's disclosure entry of act 2 records its SHA-256 as `packet_sha256`; adopted at M6's entry review, `Open` writing it and `Disclose` reading it, with a section 20 vector, and RECORD.md section 11 names the message (§16.8's disclosure row).
    - Cost: one message more in record.proto at M6's entry review.
    - Not taken: (1) a checkpoint-less `DisclosurePacket`; (3) a `CopyBody`, which carries no keys and is capped at 64 entries.
    - Raised by the build (RECORD-M6-05; the build's ledger, QD-651).

457. **A dial or route grant's prefixes and ports, and a version-3 access request's, ride `CanonicalAction.sealed_detail`, bound to the action's clear fields, while the request carries a local port, weight, ALPNs and a listen's socket name (the recommendations, 2026-11-12).**
    - Question: QD-064 moved `local_port`, `weight` and `alpn` out of `CanonicalAction` to sealed `ApprovalRequest` fields, or to `sealed_detail` should that option be taken, and owner decision 220 took it for a dial or route grant's prefixes and ports, raising the version; that left unsaid where the three fields go, which version `sealed_detail` takes, version 3's clear set, the detail's message, what its seal binds to, an action having no `StatementHeader`, and where a `socket_listen`'s name goes, since QD-064's server column lacked field 23.
    - Answer: split by what each field is: `local_port`, `weight` and `alpn` leave `CanonicalAction` (24, 25 and 34 reserved) for sealed `ApprovalRequest` fields 14, 15 and 16, daemon-built and rendered by approvers, for every kind that has them, and a `socket_listen`'s name moves from field 23, reserved, to `ApprovalRequest` field 17, so the action carries only a grant's fields and a server sees only `grant_dst`'s `sock:` entry; `sealed_detail = 38` is a dial or route grant's, at `CanonicalAction` version 4, holding `CanonicalActionDetail { repeated string cidrs = 1; uint32 target_port = 2; repeated string grant_dst = 3; }` in §7.9's sealed-body format, those grants' clear fields then holding no prefix or port, and a version-3 access request to a worker's ports carries its port and `sidecar:<id>:tcp:<port>` text the same way, so version 3's clear set leaves 27 and port text out; the detail's seal context is SignedType `SIGNED_GRANT_ACTIVATE`, the network id, and in a header nonce's place the first 16 bytes of SHA-256 over the action's clear fields as stored with field 38 left out, so a detail opens only beside the clear fields it was sealed with, in that network, with no new field and no new server-visible value (§16.8, §16.6, §16.11, §2.8's api-7).
    - Cost: two field moves and one message before record.proto froze at M3; a column in MCP-M3-02's kind table; one more derivation to specify and vector, sealed after the clear fields are final.
    - Not taken: for QD-740, (2) every such field in `sealed_detail`, so publishes carry a body no gate opens, and (3) version 3 as written, an access request's port in the clear; for QD-771, (2) the subject in the nonce's place, (3) a fresh clear nonce and (4) no binding; for QD-772, (1) field 23 on a server path for public names only, (3) for every listen, showing an application's socket name to api, and (4) a listen kept local.
    - Raised by the build (RECORD-M2-01; the build's ledger, QD-740, QD-771, QD-772).

458. **`--private` in a team network with no directory refuses before filing the RepoCreate, so every private repo has its marker (option 1, the recommendation, 2026-11-12).**
    - Question: a repo created with `--private` while its team network had no directory had nowhere for its private marker, nothing kept the person's choice where another device could read it, and once the directory existed every other repo admin's device would announce it by §16.11's predating rule.
    - Answer: a repo-creating command run with `--private` in a team network that has no directory refuses before filing the RepoCreate, naming the missing directory; where the device is a directory admin it files the directory first and then creates the repo with its private marker; the predating rule stays as written, a general or record repo that predates the directory being announced by the first of its admins' devices to sync it (owner decision 181's every repo by default) (§16.11's private row).
    - Cost: a member's `--private` create fails until an admin's device files the directory, a window that normally lasts seconds.
    - Not taken: (2) the predating rule writing a private marker, never a full announcement, narrowing owner decision 181 for older repos; (3) keeping the choice locally until the directory appears, a race with no bound.
    - Raised by the build (RECORD-M6-14; the build's ledger, QD-748).

459. **A run card is one per run and stream, versioned by the index epoch above the stream version, and a CUT lists it through its epoch (the recommendations, 2026-11-13).**
    - Question: RECORD.md 7.4 versioned a run card by the highest seq folded, which neither grows with every entry a stream gains for the run nor separates the projections an erasure's CUT must list from those made after it, and §16.8 had one card a log, which needs an elected writer and a reader of every stream.
    - Answer: a run card's version is E * 2^32 + c, E the index_epoch its push names, which only a CUT moves, and c the stream version at which the run last gained a source entry in that stream (`IndexFormatSettings.CardVersionBitCount`, 32); a CUT lists a card through its epoch, so every projection made before the CUT is listed and every one made after it is above the list; a card is one per run per stream, card (R, r) the fold of what stream R holds of run r, under R's ref_id, written by R's writer in its companion push, so one push carries it and one coverage row counts it, and §16.10's group row joins a run's cards by run_id, started the earliest, last activity the latest, title and fields from the card whose row time is newer (§16.8, §16.10, §11.12, §11.14; DESIGN-DETAILS.md section 15).
    - Cost: a few bytes more a card in an entry and a row, and 26 to 28 B a card in an erasure set; a run ingested twice is one hit built from two rows.
    - Not taken: for the version, (2) the seq with an erasure counter and (3) a CLOSURE writing the re-projection in place; for the identity, (2) one card a log by an elected device and (3) one card a log on a ref of its own.
    - Raised by DESIGN-DETAILS.md's design agent (section 15; the build's ledger, QD-779, QD-780).

460. **A record erasure destroys the bodies before its CUT, and an erased context entry's label and session ids stay in the run's earlier entry rows (the recommendations, 2026-11-13).**
    - Question: the plan had every erasure CUT first, which left a window in which a builder still folds the erased entry into a card at a new epoch; and an erased context entry had given its agent label and session ids to the rows of the run's other entry documents, which the erasure does not list.
    - Answer: a record erasure ERASEs the entry bodies, re-reads the stream heads, then CUTs; a card builder reads the log's erasure ref at the first build it makes at a new epoch and applies every ERASE on it before it folds, so no card built after the CUT holds an erased entry, and a builder that holds the ERASE before the CUT builds no card for that run and holds that stream's coverage pin; mail keeps the CUT first; an eraser that dies between the two is resumed with the CUT by another device (§11.12's reconcile row). An erasure of a context entry lists that entry and the run's card; index rows of the run's other entry documents written before the erasure keep fields 9 (agent label) and 11 (session ids); from the CUT on a builder reads those fields from the first context entry of the run its stream holds that no erasure names, and between the ERASE and the CUT a builder that holds the ERASE builds no entry document of that run; `ur record erase` says so when it lists what it will destroy, and taking fields 9 and 11 out of entry rows stays the sound change should this be revisited (§16.8, §11.12).
    - Cost: for one push's time a run's card is not rebuilt by devices that hold the ERASE; a person who wants a label or session id gone erases the run's other indexed entries too.
    - Not taken: for the order, (2) a CUT naming the erased entries and (3) a second CUT; for the fields, (2) listing and re-entering every entry document of the run and (3) entry rows without fields 9 and 11.
    - Raised by DESIGN-DETAILS.md's design agent (section 15; the build's ledger, QD-781, QD-782).

461. **DESIGN-DETAILS.md section 15's nine small readings for index erasure and run cards stand as built (the recommendation, 2026-11-13).**
    - Question: section 15 took nine readings an implementer would otherwise decide, from how a reader masks a listed key to how much an eraser fetches for a card.
    - Answer: a reader masks a listed key at or below its listed version and applies an ERASE's whole-key mask only while no set version it holds lists the key; a record log's companion push runs on `RecordSettings.CardInterval` (5 min), with a build at a run's first source entry and at the coverage entry that ends it; source entries fold in (seq in their writer's chain, entry_id bytes) order; a run whose every source entry is erased has no card; a stream version or index epoch at or past 2^32 writes no card (`IndexFormatSettings.CardVersionBitCount`); each device that held the stream's entries when it applied the ERASE folds the card again `BackfillAge` after the CUT unless the index already holds it above the list; the daemon that ERASEs a copy ingested after the erasure calls the CUT again for it; the weekly BASE removes only entries whose erasure is complete; an eraser that does not mirror the log fetches at most `IndexEraseSettings.CardFetchByteCount` (16 MiB) a card (§16.8, §16.10).
    - Cost: none beyond section 15's rules as built.
    - Not taken: each reading's other ways, which section 15 records.
    - Raised by DESIGN-DETAILS.md's design agent (section 15; the build's ledger, QD-783).

462. **An `egress_change` action carries its subject, a new `egress_mode = 39` and, under exits, its exits in `grant_dst` (option 1, the recommendation, 2026-11-13).**
    - Question: §17.9 reads an egress change as an outward action under `Sidecar.Authorize` (I-8), but kind 5 had no fields, so `NewCanonicalAction` refused it and `ur bridge egress` could not pass §5.4's gate.
    - Answer: an `egress_change` action (SidecarActionKind 5) carries `subject` (field 29) required, a new `uint32 egress_mode = 39` required, holding `BridgeEgressMode`'s values, and under `exits` the exits in `grant_dst` (field 10) as `sidecar:<id>` entries or one tag entry; version 1; no server column, since no GrantActivate or gate carries it; the approver renders the subject, the mode and the exits; §17.9's reading stands, and `ur bridge egress` without an active grant prompts and waits (§16.8).
    - Cost: one CanonicalAction field, a kind row and an approver's render line.
    - Not taken: (2) a person's confirmed command and the verifier's signer class alone, striking egress from §17.9's outward list; (3) refusing it until M8's app work.
    - Raised by the build (BRIDGE-M4-19; the build's ledger, QD-799).

463. **Model calls come from Codex's PostModelRequest hook and Claude Code's OpenTelemetry export, under a token sealed at install and scoped to model-call entries (the recommendations, 2026-11-14 and 2026-11-15; the later narrows the earlier's list of harnesses).**
    - Question: a harness's OpenTelemetry exporter needs a credential the daemon's loopback receiver checks (I-17), while I-7 keeps every credential out of an agent's environment; and Codex has both a PostModelRequest hook and an export, which would count its calls twice.
    - Answer: `ur install-harness` draws 32 random bytes per install and seals them under `<state>/harness/` beside the template's seal, writes them into the exporter's headers (Claude Code's `env` block `OTEL_EXPORTER_OTLP_HEADERS`) with the endpoint naming the loopback port it chose and sealed, and a re-install restores the same token and port; the daemon's receiver (`mcp.IngestTelemetry`) accepts the token, compared in constant time, for `model_call` entries alone, labeled source hook, and refuses anything else 401 with nothing recorded; I-7 is read as covering credentials that administer, read or sign, and this token only adds the harness's own reports, which any same-uid process can already add through `ur hook` (owner decisions 47, 75). A Codex run's model calls come from its PostModelRequest hook alone: `ur install-harness` adds PostModelRequest to Codex's hooks.json and writes no `[otel]` table for Codex, removing one an earlier install wrote, one `model_call` entry a call with its request id and token counts, and the receiver passes over any record whose resource names Codex; Claude Code keeps OpenTelemetry as its only source, and Cursor documents no exporter (§16.7, §16.18, §2.9's I-7).
    - Cost: a token in an agent's environment, which I-7's letter forbade, whose residual is forged cost and token counts, never an action; Codex's calls rest on its hook carrying the payload conformance pins.
    - Not taken: for QD-822, (2) no exporter settings, so Claude Code and Cursor runs record no model calls, and (3) a loopback listener with no caller check, against I-17; for QD-835, (2) Codex's export alone, (3) both, deduplicated by request id, and (4) the table written and every batch refused.
    - Raised by the build (MCP-M3-26 and the harness-telemetry-token track; the build's ledger, QD-822, QD-835).

464. **A directory card holds at most 16 items in at most 16,188 B, so every Signed card fits the 16 KiB card file whatever its varints (option 2, then option 1, the recommendations, 2026-11-15; the later amends the earlier's number).**
    - Question: §16.11 gave a card 16 items in 16,208 B and `directory_card_bytes` 16 KiB, but a card's and its Signed's own fields add about 180 B, so a maximal card passed the file bound; QD-834 lowered the cap to 16,192 B, and a card at that cap with seq, its position and `written_millis` at their widest varints still came to 16,388 B.
    - Answer: a directory card holds at most 16 items in at most 16,188 B, the largest cap at which every Signed card fits the 16,384 B card file, counted exactly on the wire (the card's and the Signed's own fields take up to 196 B at their widest), so it stores in at most eight 2 KiB steps, as `directory_card_bytes` 16 KiB says; `DefaultDirectoryCardSettings`' `ItemsByteCount` defaults to 16,188 and `CardFileByteCount` stays 16 KiB; the change lands before cards freeze at M10's Gn-in, and nothing that writes cards has shipped (§16.11's d/ and caps rows and directory.proto's comment).
    - Cost: one number before M10's Gn-in.
    - Not taken: for QD-834, (1) the items' 16,208 B kept, a maximal card storing at nine steps, and (3) `directory_card_bytes` raised to 18 KiB; for QD-858, (2) 16,192 B kept, a card fitting only while its seq and position stay below 2^56.
    - Raised by the build (RECORD-M6-03 and the card-items-cap track; the build's ledger, QD-834, QD-858).

465. **`ReachReceipt` gains `server_name = 11`, the name a visitor asked for, and the TLS front's publish receipts coalesce per server name (option 1, the recommendation, 2026-11-15).**
    - Question: §13.7's reach receipts row has the front record the visitor's SNI, but `ReachReceipt` had no field for it, and writing it into `relays` would read a name as a relay.
    - Answer: §16.8's `ReachReceipt` (protocol/record.proto) gains `string server_name = 11`, the name the visitor asked for (the TLS SNI), which only the TLS front sets; publish receipts coalesce per (grant, peer, server name, 5-minute window), so a visitor who reaches two aliases in one window gets two receipts; it is sealed in the record and needs no §2.8 row, and `relays` keeps the meaning R-MCP-7 and the exports give it (§16.8, §13.7).
    - Cost: one additive field.
    - Not taken: (2) the SNI as the edge's host in `relays`; (3) leaving the SNI out.
    - Raised by the build (TLS-M6-06; the build's ledger, QD-850).

466. **An `Opening` names the version of the entry it was made from, `uint32 version = 4`, absent reading 1 (option 1, the recommendation, 2026-11-15).**
    - Question: a reader of an Opening, a CopyBody, a disclosure packet's entries or an export line could not tell whether the bytes that open the commitment are the kind's own message or a version-2 `AgentSignedBody` holding it (RECORD.md 5.3), save by guessing from how the bytes parse.
    - Answer: §16.8's `Opening {header, salt, body}` gains `uint32 version = 4`, absent reading 1, set from the `RecordEntry` the opening was made from, so a reader parses body as the kind's own message (1) or the `AgentSignedBody` holding it (2), and nothing is inferred from how bytes happen to parse; old readers skip the field and still verify the commitment; the version is the opener's word, not signed, and a wrong one only makes the body fail to parse as its kind or its agent signature fail; the jsonl export's `entry_version` key is the same answer, written into RECORD.md 9 (§16.8's disclosure row; RECORD.md 5.5, 9 and 11).
    - Cost: one optional field.
    - Not taken: (2) the kind's message in `body` with the AgentSignedBody's other fields beside it, the commitment then resting on a re-encoding; (3) every consumer trying both parses.
    - Raised by the build (RECORD-M6-03; the build's ledger, QD-864).

467. **A vault card's sources add coverage and, in personal mode, claims and handoffs, and ingest writes a run's redacted prompts into its context entries (the recommendations, 2026-11-15).**
    - Question: RECORD.md 7.4 read a vault card's claim fields and `active` from claims, coverage and approval decisions that were not among its sources, so a card was no function of its stream's entries; and a vault card's prompt text and title fallback needed the run's user prompts, which only a harness's transcript held, in three harnesses' formats.
    - Answer: a vault card's sources gain coverage and, in personal mode, claim and handoff, so a card stays a function of its stream's source entries: field 14 stores the claim's own `claim_state`, or handed_off, as of the fold, never expired, which reads compute; field 13 the newest claim's expiry; and its row's expires the newest coverage's time once the run has ended, which §16.10's group row reads for active on vault and team cards alike; the team card's line counts each action kind asked for approval, approval_decision staying outside its sources. MCP ingest puts the redacted user prompts it already collects into the record: `ContextBody` gains a repeated prompts field, written in a stage-2 context entry as prompts arrive (or a new raw kind, should a context entry not carry them), so a vault card's prompts, cut at 2 KiB, and its title fallback are a function of its stream's entries, every device rebuilding them alike, and no harness's transcript format enters record extractor 1; the vault then holds prompts twice (§16.7, §16.10; RECORD.md 5.x, 7.3 and 7.4).
    - Cost: a card's version moves on those entries; a record.proto change before M10's freeze; prompts stored twice.
    - Not taken: for QD-899, (2) the sources as written, so a vault card cannot show a run as ended or its claim, and (3) approval decisions among the team card's sources; for QD-900, (1) each harness's prompt rule in extractor 1 and (3) no prompts in the card.
    - Raised by the build (SEARCH-M10-28; the build's ledger, QD-899, QD-900).

468. **`AnnounceWhere.platform` numbers its six platforms 1 to 6 in §16.11's order, 0 reading unknown (option 1, the recommendation, 2026-11-15).**
    - Question: §16.11 named six platforms for a worker's `platform`, a uint32, without their numbers.
    - Answer: directory.proto's `AnnounceWhere.platform` (`uint32 platform = 6`) takes 1 darwin, 2 linux, 3 windows, 4 android, 5 ios, 6 wasm, in the order §16.11 writes them; 0 is never written and reads as unknown, as RECORD.md section 6.2's zero row reads every enumeration; new platforms are appended; a reader shows a number it does not know as unknown and refuses nothing; the field stays a uint32 (§16.11's comment).
    - Cost: none.
    - Not taken: (2) a string identifier, a wire change to a declared field before M6's Gn-in.
    - Raised by the build (RECORD-M3-01; the build's ledger, QD-925).

469. **The directory's path-class identifiers and branches also refuse invisible format characters and line separators (option 2, as built, the recommendation, 2026-11-15).**
    - Question: §16.11's classes row refused C0 and C1 controls, bidi overrides and whitespace runs in identifiers, which let zero-width characters, tag characters and line separators reach agents as stub text no person reviewing an item could see.
    - Answer: path-class identifier fields (project, model, claim targets and the like) and branches, which also pass git check-ref-format, take NFC with no C0 or C1 control, no whitespace run and no invisible code point: none of Unicode category Cf (the bidi controls, the zero-width characters, U+FEFF and the tag characters), none with Variation_Selector or Other_Default_Ignorable_Code_Point, and no U+2028 or U+2029; labels and service names keep their ASCII grammars, and prose keeps the literal rule, controls and bidi overrides dropped; the daemon drops such characters when it normalizes a project, model or claim target, a writer whose branch holds one announces no branch, and a reader refuses an item whose identifier holds one (`directoryIdentifierTextValid`) (§16.11's classes and residual rows).
    - Cost: an emoji ZWJ sequence or a ZWNJ in a branch, project or path cannot be announced as written.
    - Not taken: (1) the literal rule, so invisible characters and a bidi override in a branch reach agents.
    - Raised by the build (RECORD-M6-11; the build's ledger, QD-936).

470. **An evs write seals its run's tainted bit and its session's durable agent id in the push's provenance, and reads return each version's provenance (the recommendations, 2026-11-15 and 2026-11-16).**
    - Question: owner decision 245 gave a format-1 push the writing run's tainted bit from M3, but no task built the evs half, the writes setting it and the reads returning it, and §16.5's `evs_write` sealed an `agent_id` an MCP session, holding only an agent label, did not have.
    - Answer: an EVS1 task at M3 builds QD-042's evs half: `EvsWritePathsArgs`, `EvsWriteAtTipArgs` and `EvsWriteEffect` take the writing run's tainted bit, or its run id read through `Sidecar.RunTainted`, into `EvsPlanArgs.Tainted`, and the session's agent id into the sealed provenance's `agent_id`; `ReadHead`, `History` and `Read` return each version's sealed provenance (tainted, agent_id and EvsImport's label) in `EvsFileInfo`, opening each version's push's sealed metadata where a format-1 read did not fetch it; MCP-M3-13 passes `McpCall.Tainted()` and honors the bit, so a version a tainted run wrote reads `trust: untrusted` and this sidecar's own versions an untainted run wrote read as own, the closed reading standing until then. An in-daemon agent gets a durable `agent_id`, one per (sidecar, label): a random Id minted at the label's first session and kept in the network's keychain.ur beside that agent's subkey record, restored with keychain.ur and unguessable from the label; once `AgentRevoke` tombstones it, the label's next session mints a new one; the daemon resolves a session's label to its id, `McpSession` exposes it, and every MCP tool passes it, into evs writes and `NewSidecarAction`, so §16.8's records, §16.10's grouping and the approval binding use one id; until it lands MCP seals no `agent_id`, which changes no trust decision (§16.5, §16.6, §5.5).
    - Cost: one EVS1 task and one registry task; the read half opens each version's sealed metadata.
    - Not taken: for QD-851, (2) MCP-M3-13 building both halves in the data lane's files and (3) every version untrusted until M10; for QD-981, (2) `agent_id` sealed only for an isolated agent and (3) the label sealed beside the id, a format change.
    - Raised by the build (MCP-M3-16 and the evs-provenance-taint track; the build's ledger, QD-851, QD-981).

471. **§16.6's spend check counts toward a rule's k only the approvers the rule names, as the authorization log's quorum does (option 1, the recommendation, 2026-11-16; it follows owner decision 349).**
    - Question: owner decision 349 had the log count a member's decision toward a rule's k only where the rule names it, but §16.6's spend check, which the daemon and every gate run, still counted any Owner's or Admin's decision, so a gate and the log could give different verdicts on one decision set, against D-MCP-12's one verdict.
    - Answer: the spend check follows the log: a member's decision counts toward a rule's k only where the rule's approvers name it (autogroup:admin, a group the member is in, or autogroup:dst-owner resolving to an admin because no owner or sponsor stands, D-ACL-23); an Owner's or Admin's decision the rule does not name only meets §8.6's admin clause where the clause applies and is never one of the k; `SpendRule` tells the two apart, `spendableSet` counts only named approvers toward k, the single-decision `Spendable` keeps taking an admin's decision where the clause applies, and `ConfigApprovalStates.Approvers` lists the named approvers, an admin only where the clause needs one and no named approver is an admin; so a set holding only an Admin's decision on a grant action under the directory's default rule is spendable at no gate, and §16.6's approval flow no longer asks Admins for approvals the log would refuse (§16.6's spendable and gate step 1).
    - Cost: an interface change to `SpendRule`, its implementations and its fakes.
    - Not taken: (2) leaving the spend check as it was, a gate and the log answering differently on one decision set.
    - Raised by the build (the acl-admin-quorum-follows-rule track; the build's ledger, QD-972).

472. **An erasure request that lapsed when its repo was announced again stays lapsed through a later revocation (option 1, the recommendation, 2026-11-16).**
    - Question: the erasure runner judged a request lapsed by comparing its marker_version with the repo's standing marker, so after a revoke, an announce and a second revoke, the second revocation's marker revived the first request, and an ERASE could name a request the person had undone.
    - Answer: an erasure request that lapsed because its repo was announced again stays lapsed for good: the runner compares the request's marker_version with the version of the announcement that replaced its marker, not only with the standing marker, so a later revocation's marker, a higher version, does not revive it; the lapse is derived from the log and kept per request in the fold; a runner that reaches a lapsed request refuses it and raises bad_erasure naming the admin, and no ERASE names it, so every ERASE names the revocation a person just made; FS-99's M6 case gains the three-step sequence in one repo (§16.11's revoke row, FS-99).
    - Cost: a remembered lapsed fact a request in the fold.
    - Not taken: (2) a later revocation's request completing every pending one of the repo; (3) documenting that revoking twice may erase under the older request.
    - Raised by the build (RECORD-M6-24; the build's ledger, QD-984).

## The build's owner questions on IP addresses, subnet routing and port forwarding, and on the SDK and apps (2026-11-11 to 2026-11-16)

The design-records track for §17 and §18 folded the answers below on 2026-11-16, each citing its ledger id: the answers the owner decided one at a time from 2026-11-11 to 2026-11-14, and the recommendations of 2026-11-15 and 2026-11-16 that the owner's instruction of 2026-11-16 accepted. A decision that groups several answers carries its latest answer's date. The sections' other items, the share pins' and the sign-out edge's wire, which the owner accepted on 2026-11-06 as defect fixes (QD-077, QD-211), went into §17 as text and fences with no number. Two of the SDK's answers carry no number: QD-732, which the owner withdrew on 2026-11-12, since DESIGN-DETAILS.md section 4 had answered it before it was asked, and QD-988, which keeps index code off the `ios_extension` leg as §22.5's §18 row and §18.10 already say, the split being a code track's.

473. **The config head carries a bridge ledger, the body-level state of every folded bridge statement, judged on the state its entry lands on and checked by every full reader (option 1, the recommendation, in each, 2026-11-11).**
    - Question: a bridge statement's rule (who holds which overlay address, whether a route's or a map's approval held, which alias took which block) is in its sealed body, which api cannot judge and section 5's `tail_verdicts` carry no bit for, so as landed a reader opened every sealed body the log ever took at each compile and judged an approval on the state at its entry's position, which a reader that started at a later base does not hold: two readers could hold different ledgers with nothing forking, and a compile grew with the log. And what judges a filing's approval after a base that did not fold it?
    - Answer: the config head carries a bridge ledger: files of rows by kind (`bridge/ledger/addresses` with their tombstones, `bridge/ledger/rules/<xx>` shards by rule id with each rule's body and disposition, `bridge/ledger/egress`, `bridge/ledger/aliases`) and `bridge/ledger/manifest` with their hashes. A writer writes them at each fold from the verdicts it holds; every full reader standing under the previous base computes the same files and forks on a difference; a device that starts at the base, and a VPN component, take them. A filing in a base's tail is judged by every reader that holds its body's key on the new base's rows with the tail re-applied, as a signer already is; the writer freezes the verdict into the ledger when it folds the entry, and writers fold through their tip whenever a bridge statement is unfolded. The one case that differs from a verdict at the entry's own position, a head landing within seconds of an approved filing whose policy disqualifies that approval, turns the verdict at every reader alike and fails closed, and the requester files again under a new decision. The files are ciphertext at evs, so no §2.8 row (§17.8's rule id row and Ledger, D-BRIDGE-6, §17.16, §17.17; §7.9's paths, BASE and fold rows).
    - Cost: six kinds of file in the config head, about 600 KB at 5,000 devices; a fold step in the config writer and a comparison in its head check; a filing in the seconds before a base's acceptance can turn void, and its requester files again.
    - Not taken: for QD-667, (2) no ledger, every compile opening every bridge statement the log ever took, so compiles grow with the log and ledgers differ by how long a device has run, and (3) approvals in the clear statement with their prefixes in `sealed_detail`, so api sees who approved which route and map; for QD-668, (2) a body-verdict bit per entry in `ProjectionHeader`, a server-visible field and a §2.8 row, and (3) a writer pushing a new head whenever its base's acceptance raced a filing, a retry loop in the writer's most contended path.
    - Raised by the design details (DESIGN-DETAILS.md section 12; the build's ledger, QD-667, QD-668).

474. **A daemon builds its bridge provider only after it has replayed the denials it had applied (option 1, the recommendation, 2026-11-11).**
    - Question: a daemon applies a revocation the posture can check by signature with the posture, which restores it before the first packet, but one only the root's judge decides (a k-of-n `MemberRevoke`, a proposal's activation at a tick, the compromise cascade) narrows the bridge in memory until the next compile confirms it, so a restart between the two let a flow in under a rule the device had judged revoked, until the first compile after the start.
    - Answer: at a daemon's start, and at each new client generation, the bridge provider is built only after every entry above the restored state that the `ConfigClient` holds has been replayed to the bridge, and a compile at the tip has run if one can, from local stores alone, since the `ConfigClient` stores an entry before any entry callback runs. If its store is behind the posture's pin, the provider waits for the follow to reach it, with `bridge_alarm` after 30 s. Nothing a restart does lowers enforcement. An app's VPN component, which has no resident judge, is unchanged (§17.8's Engine, D-BRIDGE-18, §17.16).
    - Cost: a host whose config store lagged its posture at a crash serves no bridge flow until it reaches api again.
    - Not taken: (2) no gate, a judged revocation out of force until the first compile after the start, a window an operator withholding entries can stretch; (3) the gate with a 30 s timeout, the same hole for a host that restarts with no network.
    - Raised by the design details (DESIGN-DETAILS.md section 12; the build's ledger, QD-669).

475. **The sharing network's approving admin activates an accepted share with `ur bridge activate`, pinning both tables, the accepting side's one sealed container among them (the recommendations, 2026-11-11 and 2026-11-15).**
    - Question: X's host maps a foreign flow's alias to its target by Y's alias table, sealed under the invite code's key K, which every holder of a multi-use code has, so a second holder with the operator could swap which alias reaches which target, and the table named aliases in Y's block, which Y may cycle; `network_bridge_share` has one sealed column a side, with nowhere for the accept's sealed ShareKey or its shortfall; and §17.11 named no command by which X's approving admin sees and activates an accepted share.
    - Answer: X's approving admin pins the alias table as it pins the target table: `BridgeShareGrant` gains the accepting network's id and each table's hash and version (fields 6 to 9), written in the act that files the share's `GrantActivate`, and a host compiles a share only from tables that hash to the pins. The alias table holds each alias's host bits (the low 16 bits) and length, and X's host matches a destination's low 16 bits, so a block change in Y changes nothing X pinned; if Y ever re-aliases a share, X's admin activates again. The accepting side's sealed column holds a `BridgeShareAccept{aliases, share_key, shortfall}`, each field one seal under its own label; an active share carries aliases and share_key, either refusal the shortfall alone, and `alias_table_sha256` pins the whole container, so the ShareKey the approving admin saw is pinned with the aliases, while the server still sees one opaque value. The admin activates with `ur bridge activate <share_id>`, a Confirm daemon method `bridge.activate` beside `bridge.invite` and `bridge.accept_invite`, which shows Y's network, each target with its alias, the ShareKey's fingerprint and both tables' hashes, asks on /dev/tty and activates on that preview, refusing anything that changed since; the activation stays apart from the invite, since another admin may approve (§17.9's shares, share seal and share pins, §17.11, §17.16).
    - Cost: four fields in a config file no server reads; X's admin activates again if Y re-aliases a share; one command and one control method.
    - Not taken: for QD-670, (2) the seal alone, so a second code holder with the operator can redirect aliases among the share's targets, and (3) Y's block in the table, which X's admin re-pins at each block change of Y; for QD-865, (2) columns of their own for the ShareKey and the shortfall, a server change and a §2.8 amendment; for QD-831, (2) the activation folded into `ur bridge invite`, though the inviting admin need not be the approving one, and (3) the app's UI alone, so a network run from `ur daemon` could never activate a share.
    - Raised by the design details and the build (DESIGN-DETAILS.md section 12, BRIDGE-M6-03; the build's ledger, QD-670, QD-831, QD-865).

476. **A host commits a share's raised config generation before it admits the contract that carried it (option 1, the recommendation, 2026-11-11).**
    - Question: X's host admits a share-link delegation only if its ShareCert names a generation at or above the newest it has verified for the share, which it raises and keeps in §17.8's persisted state, the ratchet that refuses a sidecar Y removed; stored after the admission, a crash between the two restored the lower generation and admitted the removed sidecar again until a current one presented the newer cert.
    - Answer: when a share-link contract's ShareCert names a generation above the host's ratchet for that share, the host commits the raise to §17.8's persisted posture store and then admits the contract: one store commit per share per rotation of Y, a few milliseconds, in that contract's `VerifyPrincipal` sequence before the dial, never a packet's, a flow's or a later contract's, so R-BRIDGE-9's no-I/O-per-packet rule is untouched, and a removed sidecar stays refused after a host restart, as FS-38 asks (§17.9's admit row, D-BRIDGE-17).
    - Cost: one store commit on the first contract after each rotation of Y.
    - Not taken: (2) admit, then commit off the sequence, a crash in the gap losing the raise inside the cert's 7 days; (3) refuse that contract, commit and admit the next, so the first contract after every rotation fails and its link is marked down for 30 s.
    - Raised by the design details (DESIGN-DETAILS.md section 12; the build's ledger, QD-671).

477. **`BridgeRules` carries the rows `autogroup:untagged` holds at a host as `untagged = 10` (option 1, the recommendation, 2026-11-12).**
    - Question: an sdk provider before C16 gives a sealed subject that no row binds the rows of an other client, untagged, which under an ACL document hold only `autogroup:untagged`'s rows, but `BridgeRules` held rows per subject, each keyed by a `sidecar_id`, and the compiler cannot enumerate app clients, so under a document an app device had no rows to give.
    - Answer: `BridgeRules` gains `BridgeRuleSubject untagged = 10`: the dial, route and exit rows `autogroup:untagged` holds at this host, with no `sidecar_id`, `member_id` or `client_ids`, which the compiler fills from §8.5's expansion and the parser checks as a subject's rows; an sdk provider before C16 gives them to any sealed subject no row binds, so under an ACL document an app device keeps the exits its policy grants the group. Per-subject rows stay as they are. `BridgeRules` is in process only, so no §2.8 row (§17.6's subject note).
    - Cost: one field in a message no server sees.
    - Not taken: (2) a reserved `sidecar_id` value standing for the group, a magic value inside a format; (3) other clients rowless under a document, so app devices lose the public exit their policy grants through an sdk provider whenever the network writes one.
    - Raised by the build (BRIDGE-M4-09; the build's ledger, QD-733).

478. **A bridge body sealed to a generation that never forms is void after a wait, at the fold and at every reader, and a filer that keeps filing such bodies is revoked (the recommendations, 2026-11-12).**
    - Question: any standing sidecar could file a bridge statement whose sealed body names a generation that never forms; no writer could open it, every later fold stopped below it, and once 4,096 entries followed no base could land. What ends the stop, what does a reader that later comes to hold the generation make of the voided body, and is it enough that a sidecar that keeps filing such bodies holds the fold until it is revoked?
    - Answer: a config writer whose whole read of gkm's generation listing, made inside the plan, names G as the newest while it holds keys 1 to G freezes a bridge body sealed above G as void (Refused, no such generation) once the entry at its tip is `UnformedVoidWait` (24 h) past the filing's own entry in log time, and folds past it; every reader that holds keys refuses, void and with no alarm, a body whose generation's GenRotate is signed more than `GenerationFormGrace` (1 h) after the entry that carries it, so a generation claimed after the void changes no reader's verdict and no device forks; a checker never voids and, lacking the generation, takes the head's rows unchecked; the void at the fold and the readers' rule land together. Both waits are `BridgeLedgerSettings` fields, the wait raised to a floor of the grace plus 30 min. A member device that keeps filing such bodies can hold the fold while it files, an accepted liveness bound: the writer's `config_key_waiting` line names the filer on every plan, revoking the filer, a statement that needs no base, ends it one wait after its last filing, and a network can lower the wait. The id level and the rows are unchanged, and no server sees anything new (§17.8's Unformed generations, §7.9's fold row, D-BRIDGE-6, §17.17).
    - Cost: a timer in the writer's fold and a judgment on gkm's listing, which a withholding server can delay but not forge; a rule every reader of a network holds alike, resting on gkm holding a claim's time within 10 min of its clock; an admin's revocation to end a filer's campaign, bases past the tail cap waiting up to 24 h at a time until then.
    - Not taken: for QD-761, (1) bounding the generation at id level, a rows field and a §2.8 line, and (3) leaving the stop unbounded; for QD-773, (2) no reader rule, the check accepting a head's void, and (3) a reader that gains such a generation forking; for QD-774, (2) the wait lowered to its 90 min floor, (3) the id-level bound as well, and (4) a fold stopping inside the previous base's tail.
    - Raised by the build and the design details (BRIDGE-M2-02, DESIGN-DETAILS.md 12.2.3's amendment; the build's ledger, QD-761, QD-773, QD-774).

479. **Under `exits` on Linux, `ur tun` keeps the daemon's own transport off the tun by connect's egress mark and wg-quick's rules, and holds the exits on a host whose reverse path filter is strict (the recommendations, 2026-11-13 and 2026-11-14).**
    - Question: under `exits` the daemon's own connect sockets must not enter the tun, but connect's egress binding does nothing on Linux, and carving the platform's addresses cannot cover extenders and peers; two sockets, the extender carrier's and the provider's ICMP egress off Windows, were outside connect's egress machinery on every platform; and on a strict reverse path filter the transport's replies would be dropped.
    - Answer: a default-off connect change adds `egress_linux.go`, whose `applyEgressInterface` sets SO_MARK from a new `SetEgressMark` at every dial site connect's egress machinery covers (the sites `egress_dial_sites_test.go` audits); `ur tun` puts the default route in its own table T with wg-quick's rules (`not fwmark M lookup T`, `lookup main suppress_prefixlength 0`) and carves nothing by the platform's addresses. SO_MARK needs CAP_NET_ADMIN, which `ur tun` already requires. Until it lands, `ur tun` on Linux installs no default route under `exits` and says so. As a connect task of its own, `openExtenderPacketConn` applies egress to the UDP socket it makes, covering the alt api path and the extender carrier's QUIC dial, which the DNS carrier rides, and the provider's ICMP egress off Windows is made through the machinery too, so the mark, and on Windows the interface pin, reach them; net_extender.go's limits move into settings with it (owner decision 297). Where rp_filter is strict (1) on all or on the interface of the main table's default route, `ur tun` holds the exits, putting nothing on, says so with the fix (rp_filter 2, systemd's default) and tries again at each sync; `ur` changes no host security setting, firewall rule or global sysctl a person did not choose, and wg-quick's `src_valid_mark` and connmark recipe is a later task of its own if strict hosts come to matter (§17.10, §17.11, §17.17, §17.18).
    - Cost: two connect tasks and their audit of the sites; on Windows the carrier socket pinned, a behavior change; a manual step on hosts with a strict filter.
    - Not taken: for QD-800, (2) SO_BINDTOIFINDEX, which needs CAP_NET_RAW as well and rebinds on every change of the physical interface, and (3) `exits` kept off `ur tun` on Linux; for QD-809, (2) wg-quick's whole recipe now, a firewall change and a global sysctl `ur` would own, and (3) rp_filter set to 2 while the exits are on, a host security setting changed without the person's say; for QD-810, (2) both sockets left outside the machinery.
    - Raised by the build (BRIDGE-M4-18 and the linux-egress-fwmark track; the build's ledger, QD-800, QD-809, QD-810).

480. **Under `exits`, each carved address the host reaches only by its default route keeps the host's own route, in both families, on every platform (the recommendations, 2026-11-14 to 2026-11-16).**
    - Question: §17.4 carves each local address the physical path needs (the host's interface addresses, gateways and system resolvers), so a captured resolver never black-holes DNS, and the bridge sends a carved address to the default route; under `exits` that route rides the tun, which can only drop and count such a packet, so a host whose resolver is off-link lost its own DNS, on Linux, on macOS and Windows, and for an IPv6 resolver once `ur tun` took IPv6's default route too.
    - Answer: on Linux `ur tun`'s exits table T gets a `throw` route for each carved IPv4 /32 beside its default route, kept in step at each sync from the addresses `ur tun` already reads and hands to `Bridge.SetLocalAddresses`, so a lookup of a carved address leaves T for the main table's default. On macOS and Windows, while `ur tun` carries IPv4's halves (0.0.0.0/1, 128.0.0.0/1) under `exits`, it adds a host route for each carved IPv4 address the host's own table reaches only by its default route, through that route's gateway and interface, as wg-quick on macOS and WireGuard for Windows route an endpoint's /32; an address an on-link or local route reaches takes none; the routes follow the carve and the default route, and come off with the halves and at close. Each IPv6 address of the carve that the host reaches only by its default route, an off-link IPv6 resolver above all, takes an IPv6 throw in T on Linux, and an IPv6 host route through the IPv6 default route's gateway on macOS and Windows, kept in step the same way. The routes follow the bridge's own carve and add nothing by the platform's addresses (owner decision 479); the host's own DNS to such a resolver leaves outside the exits, as the carve already accepts for apps (§17.4, §17.5's Classify row, §17.9's egress spec, §17.11).
    - Cost: route sets that follow the carve and the default route's gateway; the host's own DNS to a public resolver leaving outside the exits.
    - Not taken: for QD-808 and QD-869, (2) as built, a host packet to an off-link carved address dropped and counted under `exits`, so such hosts lose their own DNS, and (3) resolvers left out of the carve under `exits`, a change to §17.4's carve rule and DNS that depends on the exit link; for QD-971, (1) the throws and host routes IPv4's alone.
    - Raised by the build (the linux-egress-fwmark, linux-exits-carve-throw and tun-ipv6-exits-drop tracks; the build's ledger, QD-808, QD-869, QD-971).

481. **`ur tun` holds wintun.dll to an exact SHA-256 per architecture, which the owner records from a Windows run of fetch-deps.ps1 (option 1, the recommendation, 2026-11-14).**
    - Question: owner decision 222 has `ur tun` load a wintun.dll already on the host only once its SHA-256 and Authenticode signer match windows/app/tools/fetch-deps.ps1's pins, but that script pins the downloaded zip's hash, which a host holding only the extracted DLL cannot check, and no per-architecture DLL hash was written anywhere.
    - Answer: on Windows `ur tun` holds wintun.dll to an exact SHA-256 per architecture, beside WinVerifyTrust and fetch-deps.ps1's signer thumbprint. The owner, on a Windows machine, runs fetch-deps.ps1, which checks the zip's pin and the DLLs' signer, and records `Get-FileHash` of `bin\amd64\wintun.dll` and `bin\arm64\wintun.dll` beside the zip's pin as `$WintunDllSha256Amd64` and `$WintunDllSha256Arm64`; `ur tun` carries the same two values, with a test that they equal the script's. Until they are recorded the Windows tun refuses every DLL, the closed direction (§17.10's sidecar row).
    - Cost: one manual step on a Windows machine and a windows-repo change.
    - Not taken: (2) the signer alone with the file's version resource, which any wintun.dll its vendor signed at that version passes; (3) a hash the MSI or `ur` records at install, which an elevated loader should not take on faith.
    - Raised by the build (BRIDGE-M6-06; the build's ledger, QD-823).

482. **Under `exits`, `ur tun` takes the host's IPv6 default route too, and the bridge answers IPv6 there through the rejects row, so IPv6 reaches no exit (the recommendations, 2026-11-15 and 2026-11-16).**
    - Question: §17.9's egress spec sends a subject's default route to an approved exit or nowhere, but `ur tun` carried IPv4 alone under `exits`, so a dual-stack host's IPv6 left by its own route past the exits; and once IPv6's default route rode the tun, a silent drop there left each source to wait out a connect timeout before IPv4.
    - Answer: under `exits`, `ur tun` also takes the host's IPv6 default route: `::/0` goes through the tun in the exits table with IPv6 copies of the two rules on Linux, and the platforms' equivalents beside the IPv4 halves on macOS and Windows, since the overlay carries no IPv6 (§17.4). `device.Bridge` answers an IPv6 packet there through §17.5's rejects row (an ICMPv6 destination unreachable, administratively prohibited, or a TCP reset, within the per-source bucket, past which a packet is dropped and counted), with the per-family counter kept beside the rejects' own counts, so the source hears at once and falls back to IPv4. Nothing leaves either way, keeping "an approved exit, or nowhere" for a family the exits do not carry, and IPv6-only destinations are unreachable under `exits`. The daemon's own IPv6 sockets keep carrying the mark, so its traffic still leaves by the host's route. QD-970's reject governs QD-881's silent drop (§17.5's Rejects, §17.9's egress spec, §17.11).
    - Cost: IPv6-only destinations under `exits`; ICMPv6 errors and resets written into the tun within the bucket.
    - Not taken: for QD-881, (1) IPv4 alone, so a person's IPv6 leaves outside the exits they chose, and (3) IPv6 carried to the exits, through exit links and a NAT §17 does not specify; for QD-970, (1) a silent, counted drop.
    - Raised by the build (the linux-egress-fwmark and tun-ipv6-exits-drop tracks; the build's ledger, QD-881, QD-970).

483. **Rule 3's tag names the hosted address, so an old address's drain end closes only its own flows (option 2, the recommendation, 2026-11-15).**
    - Question: `RuleTag` hashed rules 3, 4 and 7 with one byte holding the rule number, so an old hosted address's tags were rule 3's for every subject, which were also the tags of every flow to the host's new address, and the old address's drain end reset live flows opened to the new one, against D-BRIDGE-7.
    - Answer: `RuleTag` is the first 8 bytes of SHA-256(subject_id || rule_id), a rule id being a map's or route's 32 bytes, for rules 4 and 7 one byte holding the rule's number, and for rule 3 that byte (0x03) followed by the hosted address's four bytes, so a subject's rule 3 flows carry one tag per hosted address, still enumerable from the state's addresses without tracking flows. At an old hosted address's drain end (§17.8's provider column, max(`drain_seconds`, 600 s)) the engine runs `CloseFlows` on that address's tags alone, so flows opened to the host's new address during the drain live on; a `GrantRevoke` still ends the subject's flows under every rule the grant reached. Nothing a server sees changes (§17.6's `RuleTag`, §17.8's disposition).
    - Cost: one byte string per hosted address in the tag derivation.
    - Not taken: (1) one reset of every rule 3 flow at each address drain's end; (3) an ended address's flows left to idle out, with no `CloseFlows`.
    - Raised by the build (BRIDGE-M4-11; the build's ledger, QD-931).

484. **An app device claims its overlay address only on demand, and forwarding's setup files none (option 1, the recommendation, 2026-11-15; Q-BRIDGE-8).**
    - Question: owner decision 197 names a device's overlay address among the forwarding its VPN component runs, always on, while §17.4 assigns an address on demand; should every app device hold one from its forwarding's setup?
    - Answer: Q-BRIDGE-8 is settled as §17.4 writes it: an app device claims its overlay address only on demand, when the app's UI asks, at the host's first map, or by an admin. Forwarding's setup files no `BridgeAddress` of its own; the address it names among the VPN component's forwarding is the one the device holds once it has claimed it. So no app device's loopback services become reachable to the network's untagged sidecars (§17.6's rule 3 with no ACL document) merely because its forwarding was set up. Hosting on app devices waits for C16 either way, and filing at setup can follow later with one statement at the next setup (§17.4, Q-BRIDGE-8).
    - Cost: none new; a device is addressable only once someone asks for it.
    - Not taken: (2) the sidecar role filing its own `BridgeAddress` at forwarding's setup, one statement per app device that opens each device's loopback to untagged sidecars while no ACL document exists.
    - Raised by the design of owner decision 197 (the design-od197 track; the build's ledger, QD-945).

485. **A VPN component's overlay of checked denials moves to its sealed store, and until then a posture whose overlay is full goes pending rather than silently stops applying them (option 3 in the review phase, with option 2 as built until it lands, the recommendation, 2026-11-11).**
    - Question: between compiles a VPN component's posture applies every revocation it can check by signature as an overlay entry, which only a state at or past it removes, and `PostureSettings.OverlayMaxCount` bounds the overlay at 4,096; under owner decision 216 a device that cannot compile keeps its last state indefinitely, so its overlay only grows and, within weeks, the revocations it was to keep applying at once would stop.
    - Answer: the posture's overlay moves to the component's sealed store in the review phase, streamed and indexed with only an index and a cache in RAM, as owner decision 366 directs for bulk state, so its only bound is storage's and the host's per-flow check reads the index. Until it lands, the interim as built stands: past `PostureSettings.OverlayMaxCount` (4,096) a denial the posture verified and cannot hold sets `PostureDecision.OverlayFullThroughN` (the posture manifest's field 23), the decision is pending until a state at or past it, the posture keeps following the chain, and it alarms malformed once a call. A posture that can no longer hold a denial it can check denies rather than silently stops applying them; owner decision 216's accepted cost covers only revocations the posture cannot check (§17.8's forwarding roles, §18.6's posture, §18.10's memory row).
    - Cost: review-phase work in `device/`; until then, on a device that never compiles, private reach and exits denied once its overlay fills, which costs availability there, never safety.
    - Not taken: (1) SDK-M4-01's bound kept as it was, so past 4,096 a never-compiling device stops applying checkable revocations and stops following, with only the malformed alarm to say so; (2) going pending as the lasting answer, rather than the interim.
    - Raised by the build (SDK-M8-17; the build's ledger, QD-676).

486. **The lb sends no X-Frame-Options for the key origin, a listed exact host, so its framing rests on its CSP's frame-ancestors (option 1, the recommendation, 2026-11-11).**
    - Question: once mmm's block makes `sidecar.ur.io` one of its names, the lb's `addSecurityHeaders` adds `X-Frame-Options: SAMEORIGIN` to its responses beside mmm's `frame-ancestors https://ur.io`, and SAMEORIGIN alone would forbid ur.io's frame, against the key origin's plan to send none.
    - Answer: warp's services.yml gains a list of exact hosts the lb sends no X-Frame-Options for, shaped like `no_access_log` and checked the same way (an exact host the lb serves), and warp/services/sidecar-blocks/mmm.yml adds `sidecar.ur.io` to it; warpctl's `addSecurityHeaders` skips the header for a listed host, and a warp config test asserts the key origin's server block has no X-Frame-Options while every other host keeps SAMEORIGIN. The key origin's framing then rests on mmm's CSP `frame-ancestors https://ur.io` alone, and ur.io keeps its own protection. The change lands before the owner applies mmm.yml (§18.8's key origin, §18.15's services row).
    - Cost: one small warp change, one fragment line and one more field the owner reviews.
