    - Answer: for mail to abuse@ and postmaster@ that §14.8's header-only parse cannot attribute, the platform listener answers at the end of DATA `550 5.7.1 Not read. Report abuse at https://ur.io/abuse` in place of 250 and drops the message unread; attributed mail keeps its 250 and sealed copy, and report-token mail is unchanged. The sender's own MTA returns that text to the person as a bounce, so the pointer arrives with no credential on the TCP 25 block, no backscatter from URnetwork, no third party and no stored address (§21.8, D-ABUSE-5, §15.4, §14.8).
    - Cost: role mail is refused rather than silently accepted, and 250 against 550 shows a sender whether attribution matched.
    - Not taken: (2) sending through a narrow SES credential in the platform block, a credential on the exposed block, reporters' addresses shown to a provider and backscatter on the account that sends sign-in mail; (3) a 250 carrying the link, which no person sees; (4) no auto-reply and the form published only in the RFC 2142 contact pages, so a reporter who mails abuse@ hears nothing.
    - Raised by the build (the ABUSE plan; the build's ledger, QD-142).

534. **A name sent to review is refused with a case, and a reviewer's allow lets the caller's retry pass (option 1, the recommendation, 2026-11-06).**
    - Question: §21.6 sends brand-plus-keyword names to review and §21.7 spoke of names held for review, yet §12's tables have no pending state.
    - Answer: review is a refusal with a case. The route answers `QuotaRefusal{limit_key: screen_review}` with the case id and stores nothing in §12's tables. A reviewer's allow is an `abuse_action` of kind `screen_allow`, naming the network and the name, which `ScreenFacts` reads, so the caller's retry passes. On probation a review verdict is refused with no case, and nothing reserves the name meanwhile (§21.6, §21.7, §12.8).
    - Cost: the user retries, and the name is not reserved while it waits.
    - Not taken: (2) a pending state in §12's record and label tables, through the journal and the restore step, which does nothing for published hosts; (3) refusing until the network verifies the brand's domain, so legitimate names such as `acme-support` lose.
    - Raised by the build (the ABUSE plan; the build's ledger, QD-235).

535. **Mailed records are HPKE-XWing sealed, their MAC inside, to an offline key that `sidecarctl abuse mailbox-keygen` makes (option 1 of each, the recommendations, 2026-11-15).**
    - Question: §21.10 sealed each record "to a vault-held HPKE key", and the accepted fix QD-078 gave it a MAC, but nothing said what bytes a record carries, where its keys live, what makes them, or in what form the private key is kept.
    - Answer: a record is sealed with HPKE-XWing as smtpedge's sealed report copies are (KEM 0x647a, HKDF-SHA256, AES-128-GCM, base mode), info "UR abuse 1.0 record", body u8(1) || enc (1,120 B) || ct, the plaintext the record's canonical JSON with its HMAC-SHA-256 MAC inside. Vault file `abuse-mailbox.yml` holds `abuse_mailbox_mac_key` (32 random bytes, base64) and `abuse_mailbox_seal_public_key` (1,216 B, base64), mounted on api and taskworker only; the private key is held offline beside §19.10's restore runbook and is reachable by no running service. The mail is a text/plain body of the base64, through api's and taskworker's SES path, and the legal-request mailbox takes the same seal and key file. `sidecarctl abuse mailbox-keygen -out <abuse-mailbox.yml> -private-out <file>` draws the pair with `hpke.MLKEM768X25519().GenerateKey()` and the 32 MAC bytes from crypto/rand and writes the vault file and, to the separate private file, `abuse_mailbox_seal_private_key: <base64 of the 32-byte seed>`, draft-connolly-cfrg-xwing's private key, which `hpke.MLKEM768X25519().NewPrivateKey(seed)` reads back, both 0600, printing neither key; the restore reads that private file. A rotation is the same command with a new pair; records sealed before it open only with the old seed, so §19.10's runbook keeps every seed until its last record's ttl ends. The owner runs it to create the vault file, an outward step, and keeps the private file offline (§21.10, §19.10, §2.8's api-14).
    - Cost: one vault file and one command; the owner creates the file and keeps the seed offline.
    - Not taken: for the seal, (2) DHKEM(X25519) with ChaCha20-Poly1305, a second suite to review and no post-quantum margin on records kept as long as the actions they describe; for the keys, (2) the owner writing the pair by hand with a short program, an unchecked manual step and a key form the restore would have to guess.
    - Raised by the build (ABUSE-M5-03 and the record seal's track; the build's ledger, QD-726, QD-832).

536. **`POST /abuse/report` counts reports per keyed address hash in Redis, which api-14 lists (option 1, the recommendation, 2026-11-15).**
    - Question: the report form's per-address limit counted inside each api process, so a caller's limit was N times its setting, and a shared count needs a Redis family §2.8's api-14 did not list.
    - Answer: api-14 gains "report attempts per caller address hash, for the window (POST /abuse/report's per-address limit, 30 an hour, keyed by the address's peppered /29 or /56 hash; no address held)", ttl the window, matching api-3's wording for the SSO discovery limit; the route counts each report through server.CheckIpRateLimitAttempt per keyed /29 or /56 hash (server.ClientIpHashForAddr, peppered) under `AbuseRouteSettings`' window and count, a refused report counting too and `WARP_LIMIT_EXCLUDE_SUBNETS`' infrastructure addresses counting nothing, so §21.16's limit holds across api processes. It keeps no more than the in-process count did, only shared (§2.8's api-14, §21.16).
    - Cost: a Redis call per report.
    - Not taken: (2) the in-process count, a caller's limit N times the setting.
    - Raised by the build (ABUSE-M5-11; the build's ledger, QD-895).

## The build's owner questions on testing, verification and phasing (2026-11-06 to 2026-11-16)

The design-records track for §21, §22 and §23 folded the answers below on 2026-11-16, each citing its ledger id: the owner-class items the owner accepted in bulk on 2026-11-06, the answers it decided one at a time from 2026-11-10 to 2026-11-15, and the recommendations of 2026-11-16 that the owner's instruction of that day accepted. A decision that groups several answers carries its latest answer's date. The sections' other items, the defect fixes the owner accepted on 2026-11-06 (§22's QD-200, QD-210 and QD-264, §23's QD-247) and the build's readings, fills and settled items, went into §22, §23 and the sections they name as text with no number.

537. **T3 attests each master of its private Redis Cluster by address (option 1, the recommendation, 2026-11-06).**
    - Question: §22.4 and Q-TEST-2 put T3 on a 3-master Redis Cluster, never one node, and §22.8's real rule needs server's real client, yet server/test-env.sh's preflight attested one Redis endpoint, so a MOVED to a second master was refused, and editing the file that protects production data needed the owner's word.
    - Answer: server/test-env.sh's portable mode takes a list of Redis authorities. Each must be loopback on a port above 50000, pass `test_env_refuse_loopback_tunnel_endpoint` and the probe, and be attested by address in `WARP_TEST_ENV_ATTESTED_ADDRS`, which has room for 8. test_env_dial_guard.go stays as strict, no other mode changes, and the list stays private to the sidecar stack (§22.4).
    - Cost: an edit to the file that protects production data.
    - Not taken: (2) T3 on one Redis node, relying on the CROSSSLOT lints, against Q-TEST-2; (3) T3's engines through sidecartest's local-stack client, so T3 no longer runs server's real Redis path.
    - Raised by the build (the TEST plan; the build's ledger, QD-028).

538. **§22.6's PROXY differential runs against a reader written from haproxy's specification (option 1, the recommendation, 2026-11-06).**
    - Question: §22.6 had pires/go-proxyproto agree with mailgun/proxyproto, which carries no license, so §4.7's refusal of unlicensed modules, which check/ applies to every leg, test legs included, kept the bullet from being built as written; and server's connect links that module in production, outside §4.7's reach.
    - Answer: §22.6's PROXY bullet names a second reader written from haproxy's proxy-protocol.txt, which is what landed, agreement running both ways; a one-off comparison against mailgun's parser, outside every repository, found it takes every header tlsedge takes, with the same address, port and length, and much that pires refuses. Separately the owner decides what to do about server/connect/transport_pp.go, which links the unlicensed module in production: a request to mailgun for a license, or a replacement, an owner's step outside this design. §4.7 is unchanged (§22.6).
    - Cost: no standing check against the parser server's connect runs, which tlsedge does not run.
    - Not taken: (2) the mailgun leg in server, a fuzz target outside sidecar waiting for server to require it; (3) admitting mailgun by exception, a §4.7 and check/ change putting an unlicensed module in a public repository's go.mod; (4) asking mailgun for a license first, an unknown wait.
    - Raised by the build (TLS-M5-07; the build's ledger, QD-135).

539. **The nightly form runs as one launchd job on a host the owner names (option 1, the recommendation, 2026-11-06).**
    - Question: §22.8's cadence runs T3 rows nightly, but there is no CI and no nightly job (§4.3), RUN-ALL runs under a suite lock agents may not take, and the build's only merge point is its landing tool.
    - Answer: a launchd job runs `sidecar/test.sh --cadence nightly` on a host the owner names, with T3's private stores on that host. During the build each gate sweep runs that form. It never takes tests/network-intensive-suite-lock.sh (§22.15, §22.8).
    - Cost: one host and one job; a race only T3 shows surfaces up to a day late.
    - Not taken: (2) T3's FS rows on every merge, hours a merge at 100 seeds; (3) no nightly, T3 rows only at gate sweeps, so a T3 regression surfaces a milestone late.
    - Raised by the build (the TEST plan; the build's ledger, QD-239).

540. **A rekey of a git-mode ref is a git-mode push with no commit that keeps the tip (option 1, the recommendation, 2026-11-10).**
    - Question: `ur evs rekey` re-checkpoints a repo's files under the current generation, but a git-mode ref takes writes only as git pushes through the local tier, so the job skipped git-mode refs and §22.7's row held for file-mode refs alone.
    - Answer: a rekey of a git-mode ref is a git-mode push with no commit, DESIGN-DETAILS.md 8.2's REKEY shape: it keeps the tip, and its changes only re-checkpoint files under the current generation, each new entry's sealed mode, oid and name equal to the standing one's. Any writer's device can send it, a head-mode one included. The rekey job's git-mode skip goes, after which §22.7's row holds for git-mode refs too; DESIGN-DETAILS.md section 8 stands as written (§22.7).
    - Cost: a rule in the git-mode push checks, and the readers' and export's handling of a push that seals no commit.
    - Not taken: (2) a rekey through the local tier's mount, re-pushing the tree with every blob forced to a checkpoint, which a head-mode device, a phone or a page Worker cannot do and which needs option 1's shape underneath; (3) no rekey of git-mode refs, so a removed reader keeps opening their untouched files until someone changes them.
    - Raised by the build (KEYS-M2-26; the build's ledger, QD-634).

541. **The soak counts orphan objects by orphan GC's own dry count (option 1, the recommendation, 2026-11-11).**
    - Question: §22.8's soak checks that orphan objects return to baseline, but evsservice had no count that deletes nothing, and orphan GC's definition lives in its own code.
    - Answer: evsservice/gc_orphans.go gains a dry form, `OrphanGc.CountRepo(ctx, repo *OrphanGcRepo) (*OrphanGcResult, error)`, which runs steps 0 to 4 and step 5's selection, issues no DELETE and reports `CandidateCount` and the count of those no pidx/ entry introduces, sharing orphan GC's one definition. The soak counts orphans through it per repo over the nightly pass's listings and compares what GC would judge orphan, past the horizon, at the baseline and at the end; until it lands the orphan signal reads open (§22.8's soak row).
    - Cost: about forty lines in the data lane's file.
    - Not taken: (2) counting the nightly pass's deletes, which measures what GC reclaimed, not what stands, and reads nothing in a soak under 49 h; (3) dropping the orphan signal until §22.8's full run exists.
    - Raised by the build (TEST-M2-03; the build's ledger, QD-677).

542. **The MLS subset stops handing its AEAD nonces to `testHookSecret` (option 1, the recommendation, 2026-11-12).**
    - Question: `mls_*` passed its 12-byte AEAD nonces to `testHookSecret`, and the privacy capture refuses a secret under 16 bytes as a finding, since it cannot search one reliably, so S3a and S4a failed on values that are no keys.
    - Answer: the subset's label expansion takes a hook flag, false for the three nonce derivations RFC 9420 makes (the ratchet nonce, the sender-data nonce and the Welcome nonce), and every secret a nonce is derived from stays hooked. The capture's rule stands: a secret under 16 bytes is refused as a finding, so no test passes on a value it could not search for. §22.4's "at every key creation, derivation and unwrap" reads keys, which a nonce is not (§22.4).
    - Cost: a small change in the MLS files.
    - Not taken: (2) the capture searching a 12- to 15-byte secret in its raw and hex forms alone, its base64 and base32 forms unsearched; (3) both.
    - Raised by the build (TEST-M2-11; the build's ledger, QD-755).

543. **FI-9's oracle keeps its own parse of git's object format, and go-git checks it from mlsinterop (option 1, the recommendation, 2026-11-15).**
    - Question: FI-9's oracle read exports with a parse of its own, while go-git, the reader the plan named, is linked only by mlsinterop.
    - Answer: FI-9's oracle keeps its own parse of git's object format in sidecartest/fault, independent of ur's evs_git_* and export code, as FI-9's trap asks, and a differential test in the nested mlsinterop module, where §4.4 already places go-git, holds the oracle's reading of exports and of stock git repositories equal to go-git's. No new root dependency; mlsinterop imports the fault package and carries its requires (§22.6).
    - Cost: one test file in mlsinterop and the fault package's requires in its go.mod.
    - Not taken: (2) moving the parse to go-git on the root module's test leg, a shared dependency change for every importer of the fault package; (3) the own parse alone, checked only against git's documented format and hand-written objects.
    - Raised by the build (TEST-M2-06; the build's ledger, QD-861).

544. **FS-114's restarted-host schedule stays on one process outside the settle rules, and a drive-level test with a held commit follows in the review phase (option 1 now, option 3 later, the recommendation, 2026-11-15).**
    - Question: the schedule held a commit in the old process of a one-process cluster, but owner decision 504 refuses to start a one-process cluster under the settle rules, so that shape cannot run under them in any build.
    - Answer: FS-114's restarted-host schedule stays as built on one process outside the settle rules: the new process is refused every drive while the old one lives and serves them once it exits. Owner decision 504's refusal gets no way around it in any build. In the review phase, the minio fork's cmd tests gain a drive-level test with a real held commit: a helper process holds a rename past its check and is killed while a second process opens the drive, showing a commit past its check dying with its process while a successor waits, the flock being per drive and per process (§22.8's FS-114).
    - Cost: until the review phase the held commit and the restart are shown only apart.
    - Not taken: (2) a settle_testhooks build starting one process under the settle rules on a test variable, a way around owner decision 504's refusal in the build T3 runs.
    - Raised by the build (the minio fork's settle track; the build's ledger, QD-891).

545. **blobsuite's late-request cases send a deadline late by the clock rule's margin (option 1, the recommendation, 2026-11-15).**
    - Question: blobsuite's two late-request cases sent `x-ur-deadline-ms` 1 ms late by the caller's clock, which a store host's clock, within §19.6's bound, may not yet have passed.
    - Answer: blobsuite's `Settle` gains `LateBy`, 200 ms by default, twice the 100 ms a settler waits past a deadline under §19.6's clock rule, which server's T3 suite passes through, and the two late-request cases send `x-ur-deadline-ms` as `Settle.Now()` minus `LateBy`. The rule under test is that a request late by the store's clock is refused, and a caller can know it is late only by the margin the clock rule gives; a store that takes a request 200 ms late still fails the case (§22.9's file-cluster suite, FS-114).
    - Cost: one settings field and the two cases.
    - Not taken: (2) keeping 1 ms with T3's suite reading the store's clock over NTP, a published port and an offset carried through the suite; (3) rerunning a failed case, so a real late take and a clock's noise read the same.
    - Raised by the build (the minio fork's settle track; the build's ledger, QD-892).

546. **The test leg moves to a Pebble release that finds its own certificates for replacement, or else the workaround moves into sidecartest's Pebble harness (option 3, else option 2, the recommendation, 2026-11-15).**
    - Question: Pebble v2.10.1 cannot replace about 1 in 255 of its own certificates, filing and looking up an issued certificate's order by two forms of its serial, and certs' tests worked around it by taking a certificate it can replace.
    - Answer: a track fetches github.com/letsencrypt/pebble/v2 v2.10.3 through the module proxy and reads its replacement-order check and its order lookup by issued serial: if they file and look up by one form of the serial, the test leg moves to v2.10.3, with its dependencies.yml row and the go.mod and go.sum of the modules that require it, and certsservice's test-side step goes. If no listed release fixes it, the workaround moves into sidecartest's Pebble harness, a method that issues a replaceable leaf or says whether it can replace one, so tlsedgeservice's, the local stack's and T3's tests share it. Reporting the bug upstream stays the owner's outward step (§22.9's ACME row).
    - Cost: a fetch and a dependency row, or a harness method.
    - Not taken: (1) the test-side step as landed, each later Pebble user having to know it; (4) a patched Pebble fork, against patching fetched modules. (5), the upstream report, stays the owner's to make beside either.
    - Raised by the build (the certs ARI track; the build's ledger, QD-903).

547. **FS-99's refusal on the push route comes at step 5c, as §11.12 says (option 1, the recommendation, 2026-11-16).**
    - Question: FS-99's row had a caller below admin refused "before any slot GET" on every route, while §11.12's directory row and I-EVS-16 refuse an ERASE, CUT or CLOSURE sent to `POST .../push` at step 5c, after step 4 may GET the slots it names.
    - Answer: §11.12's directory row and I-EVS-16 stand: a caller below admin that sends an ERASE, CUT or CLOSURE to `POST .../push` is refused 403 SCOPE at step 5c, before any commit or slot rewrite, every slot's ETag unchanged, though step 4 may first GET the slots it names; those reads are bounded by the refusal fence, at most one recorded refusal a second a ref, and their bytes never reach the caller. FS-99's row is reworded to match: its "before any slot GET" binds `POST .../slots/delete` and a `?closure=1` upload, which refuse a caller below admin at arrival. No engine or test changes: FS-99's evs clause already holds the push route that way (§22.8's FS-99).
    - Cost: a caller below admin can still make evs read the slots it names.
    - Not taken: (2) evs refusing such a statement before step 4 from the token's rights and the statement's flags alone, a change to the push path and an open question of whether that refusal is recorded.
    - Raised by the build (RECORD-M6-24; the build's ledger, QD-985).

548. **The gate sweeps name FS-5's and FS-73's T3 tests where they run, in server's evshost (option 1, the recommendation, 2026-11-16).**
    - Question: FS-5's and FS-73's T3 tests drive stock git and git-lfs through evsgit's world, which needs a testing.TB, so neither is a registered fault builder, and they run in server's evshost package, not in sidecart3, where the convention looks.
    - Answer: the gate sweeps name those tests where they run, as M1's sweep names FS-54's: M2's exit sweep names TestFs5OnT3 and M10's names TestFs73OnT3, each as {repo: server, package: ./evshost, tags: 'sidecar_testhooks,sidecar_pending,sidecar_localstack', stores: true}, and FS-72's rollback case, TestFs72RollbackOnT3, which also runs in ./evshost, takes the same entry at M10. No test moves, and gates.yml is regenerated. A gate keeps reading a row missing until a sweep names its test, so nothing passes early (§22.8, §22.9, §23.8).
    - Cost: three sweep entries.
    - Not taken: (2) moving both tests into server's sidecart3 package, a cross-area move; (3) making evsgit's world buildable from a fault.Net, a rework of the shared git world, whose rows would still need stock git and git-lfs on the host.
    - Raised by the build (EVS2-M10-09; the build's ledger, QD-995).

549. **A test that advances a fake clock past a timer the code arms after a step first waits for that timer to be armed (option 1, the recommendation, 2026-11-16).**
    - Question: TestTlsFrontKeepsItsRegistrationsListsInStep raced: the TLS front sends an update and then arms its next margin's timer, and an advance between the two left no timer to fire.
    - Answer: the test waits for the front's update loop to arm its margin timer before it advances the fake clock: the test clock counts its live timers and the test waits until one is due at the margin, as the receipt loop's clock tests wait for theirs. Production code is unchanged, since a real clock only delays the margin's update by the send's duration. Every test in that file that advances after an update takes the same wait, and §22.8's determinism rule states it for every fake-clock test (§22.8).
    - Cost: a timer-count helper on the test clock and a wait before each advance after an update.
    - Not taken: (2) arming the timer before the send, a production change for a test's race, which still races a test that advances before reading the update.
    - Raised by the build (the ACL atoms track; the build's ledger, QD-1002).

550. **The segment placement is a tracked item with a 26-week lead time, its design held by Q-OPS-11 (option 1, the recommendation, 2026-11-06).**
    - Question: §19.9 and D-OPS-17 say the segment placement and added HDD capacity land before year two's load, but §11.22 and §19.18 left them under Later, §23.4 omitted them and §23.7 gave neither due nor start, so no lead time could exist for undesigned work with no milestone.
    - Answer: a tracked item now, as the next metadata cluster has: §23.7 gives it (due: before the projected crossing; start: M3's exit, when the hdd run first gives an admitted throughput) and a lead time of 26 weeks, monitor's setting `hdd_trigger_lead_days` = 182. A new Q-OPS item, Q-OPS-11, holds the placement's design, due within 8 weeks of M3's exit; §23.4's Later list and §19.18 name it, and the rota owns the page (§23.7, §23.4, §19.14, §19.18, §11.22, Q-OPS-2's residual).
    - Cost: design time and HDD spend committed about two quarters ahead of a crossing that may come soon after M3.
    - Not taken: (2) deciding at M3's exit on the measured rate, which by the model fires and commits the schedule early; (3) operators' judgment, the first hard signal staying refused uploads.
    - Raised by the build (the G0 re-check of §19.9's hdd trigger; the build's ledger, QD-132).

551. **§23.8's density rule names the duplication the design keeps on purpose, and test lists move into §22 (option 2, the recommendation, 2026-11-06).**
    - Question: §23.8's "no content duplicated across sections" failed on the design as written: 4.1% of its words near-duplicate another section, some on purpose (§2.8's published list, §3.7's flows, Requirements rows), some harmfully (Interfaces subsections carrying test lists that repeat §22's rows) and some by accident (one rule stated in many places).
    - Answer: §23.8's clause reads "no content duplicated across sections (each item in its owning section, cited elsewhere), except §2.8's published list, §3.7's flows and Requirements rows". design-records moves the test lists out of the Interfaces subsections into §22 first, §7.17's, §9.15's, §11.20's and §17.16's, and turns accidental restatements into citations, before M2's Gn-in (§23.8, §22, §7.17, §9.15, §11.20, §17.16).
    - Cost: an edit pass, starting with the most dangerous copies.
    - Not taken: (1) the rule as written with all duplication removed, a large edit across nearly every section before M2 freezes text; (3) the rule and the text left alone, every re-check reporting the same failure.
    - Raised by the build (the G0 density review; the build's ledger, QD-274).

552. **The sweep that moves drafted code's constant tunables into settings objects runs in the review phase (option 1, the recommendation, 2026-11-11).**
    - Question: owner decision 297 puts every limit in a settings object, and on 2026-11-11 a scan found about 801 constants in 351 sidecar files that no remaining task names.
    - Answer: the sweep that moves the drafted code's constant tunables and limits into settings objects runs in the review phase, area by area as the review reads each area, not during the draft phase. Until then new code follows owner decision 297 and every track moves the constants in the files it touches; the remaining constants stay in drafted code, and the review phase also sweeps the build's code in server, connect and sdk (§6's bounds, §23.8).
    - Cost: drafted code keeps constants until its area's review.
    - Not taken: (2) sweep tracks beside features now, 25 to 80 lane-hours before the draft surface is done; (3) a sweep before features, pausing them about a day in four lanes.
    - Raised by the orchestrator (the build's ledger, QD-706).

## The build's owner questions on the published list and the build, second folds of §2 and §4 (2026-11-16)

The design-records track for §2, §3 and §4's second pass folded the answers below on 2026-11-17, each citing its ledger id: the answers queued for those sections after their first folds, each the recommendation that the owner's instruction of 2026-11-16 accepted. A decision that groups several answers carries its latest answer's date.

553. **api-7 says what a presence key registration's attestation statement shows api: the attestation object, with the authenticator's model and its vendor's attestation certificate chain (option 1, the recommendation, 2026-11-16).**
    - Question: QD-885's answer, which owner decision 455 records, ended the FIDO client's interim `fmt none` object with a zeroed aaguid, so a `packed` registration carries the authenticator's aaguid, its make and model, and the vendor's attestation certificate chain in `KeyRegister.attestation`, which is plain in the authorization log entry that api stores and reads; api-7 named the attestation's client data but not the attestation object, the model or the certificate, so the published promise of what servers see fell short of what api sees.
    - Answer: api-7 says what a presence key registration shows api: its attestation, which is the create ceremony's client data (its type, origin and challenge, owner decision 455's words) and the attestation object, with the authenticator's model (aaguid) and its vendor's attestation certificate chain where the authenticator gave a statement, a batch certificate shared by at least 100,000 devices under FIDO's rules that names the vendor and often the product line. The words go into api-7 beside owner decision 455's, `metadata_list.yml` following §2.8's block (list v44), before a release. The FIDO client keeps carrying real statements as owner decision 455 directs; no field, column or family is new, and the batch certificate identifies a model, not a device (§2.8's api-7).
    - Cost: none beyond the list's version; the list says what api already sees, and the network's members see the same bytes in the config log.
    - Not taken: (2) the client stripping the statement again until the list carries the words, which undoes owner decision 455's labels rising with no re-registration for every key registered meanwhile; (3) sealing the attestation to the network and giving api only its hash, a format change to `KeyRegister` and to §7.9's rule.
    - Raised by the build (the presence-attestation-labels track; the build's ledger, QD-983).

554. **Amending owner decisions 277 and 493: §4.7's legs hold each macOS slice in both configurations gomobile runs it in, its binding step as GOOS=ios with `macos` and its compile as GOOS=darwin with the bind's tags alone (option 1, the recommendation, 2026-11-16).**
    - Question: the pinned gomobile (golang.org/x/mobile, which sdk/build/go.mod pins) runs every Apple slice in two configurations, which agree for the iPhone and simulator slices and differ for the macOS ones: gobind's binding step type-checks the bound package and every dependency with GOOS=ios and the bind's tags plus `macos`, while the c-archive each slice links compiles with GOOS=darwin and the Makefile's tags alone, since a command-line `-tags` replaces the `-tags=macos` gomobile sets in GOFLAGS (cmd/gomobile's bind_iosapp.go and env.go). Owner decision 493 read the first as the slices' build and had the leg tables' macOS rows model it alone, which would leave no leg compiling sidecar as the macOS slices link it, and owner decision 277's `macos` sat on rows modeling a compile that has no such tag.
    - Answer: each macOS slice gets two rows in sdk's table (sdk/dependency_boundary_test.go's `messagingBoundaryLegs`), mirrored by sidecar's `check/` legs, for the main bind and the extension's: the binding step's, GOOS=ios with the bind's tags plus `macos`, and the compile's, GOOS=darwin with the bind's tags alone, dropping the `macos` owner decision 277 put on them; each row's name says which step it models, and owner decision 493's GOOS=ios rows go beside the darwin ones rather than replacing them. Owner decision 493's premise holds for the binding step only: the compiled archive already selects sdk's desktop dial and the root's daemon client, so the macOS app's remote mode works as linked; 493's tag changes make the binding step agree, and a later divergence between the two is caught by `make check` rather than at bind time (§4.7's legs, §5.10, §18.10).
    - Cost: four more legs, two per bind, each read by every rule and compiled, a few seconds warm.
    - Not taken: (2) the macOS rows modeling the binding step alone, as owner decision 493 had them, so no leg compiles the code the slices link and a break there surfaces at bind time; (3) the rows modeling the compile alone, 493's GOOS=ios rows dropped, so no leg reads what gobind type-checks beyond sdk's own `TestSdkCompilesForEveryBoundTarget`.
    - Raised by the build (the gomobile-bind-legs track; the build's ledger, QD-997, on QD-908's and QD-991's answers).

555. **The release keeps each path its rewrite should: the tests only the release layout broke are fixed in their owners' files, `check/` models run.sh's two path passes in run.sh's order and fails a line they leave unversioned, and sdk's release restores its nested modules' own paths (option 1 of each question, the recommendations, 2026-11-16).**
    - Question: with owner decision 278's fix landed, the real release fixture got through sidecar's release step and ran run.sh's `make check` in `sidecar/v2026` once both tags existed, where three tests failed that pass in the development layout. sidecartest/search_oracle_test.go's allow-list named the root beside a subpackage on one line, which run.sh's exact root pass versions and its guarded subpackage pass then skips, leaving the subpackage unversioned, while `check/`'s model of the rewrite (releaseRewritePath) applied the guarded pass alone; mcp/harness_cloud_test.go read cli/ under the module root, which is v2026/ on a version branch while cli/ stays at the repository's top; and sidecartest/barrier_test.go held one path in its goroutine dump and its want lines, which the rewrite reached in the first and not the second. Past `make check`, sdk's release stopped after sdk's tag was pushed: sdk/cgo, a nested module the release leaves unversioned and untagged, had gained a second package, sdk/cgo/abitest, whose import in its tests the root's subpackage pass rewrote to a package no module provides, the trap run.sh's comment names for sidecar's cli/ and mlsinterop/, which `check/` holds sidecar to and nothing held sdk to.
    - Answer: each of the three tests is fixed in its owner's file: the allow-list puts one path on a line, the cloud harness finds cli/ at the repository's top when the module root is a version directory, and the goroutine dump's test builds its dump and want lines from one path value, so the rewrite reaches both or neither. `check/`'s releaseRewritePath applies run.sh's exact pass before its guarded subpackage pass, in run.sh's order, with a twin input in release_step_test.go naming the root beside a subpackage, and a `check/` rule fails a .go or Makefile line that the modeled rewrite leaves holding an unversioned path of this repository, so that kind fails in development, where tip-check runs; run.sh's two passes keep their order in every repository. sdk's release region in run.sh restores each untagged nested module's own path after the root's rewrite: for build, cgo and js, `github.com/urnetwork/sdk/v2026/<module>/...` goes back to `github.com/urnetwork/sdk/<module>/...` in every .go file and Makefile, since those modules keep their unversioned paths by design, so sdk/cgo's tests keep importing `github.com/urnetwork/sdk/cgo/abitest` and any later subpackage of a nested module is covered. It sits in the region that already treats build, cgo and js apart (go_mod_fork's arguments, go_mod_fork_update), it does not constrain how sdk tests the C ABI, and a build/all test over a synthetic sdk tree and the release fixture's small sdk tree, which holds a nested module's subpackage, test it. Whoever runs the real release fixture gives it a TMPDIR of about 70 bytes or less, for the socket tests' sun_path limit (§4.7's hygiene row, §4.9's versions).
    - Cost: three small test fixes in three areas and one rule in `check/`, which catches the first kind only, the other two being one-off; a few lines in run.sh's sdk region and their tests.
    - Not taken: for QD-1003, (2) changing run.sh so the root's rewrite never skips such a line, the guarded pass first, which runs in every repository's release and needs each tree's own fixture run, and (3) running the real fixture without `make check` until the review phase, so the gate passes while `make check` at release still fails after both tags exist, burning the version; for QD-1004, (2) keeping each of sdk's nested modules one package, as sidecar's cli/ and mlsinterop/ are, abitest's cgo helpers moving into package main behind a tag only the tests set, and (3) leaving it until the review phase, so the next release stops at sdk/cgo's tidy after sdk's tag is pushed.
    - Raised by the build (the release-keep-third-party track; the build's ledger, QD-1003 and QD-1004).

## The build's owner questions on teams, access control, sockets and group keys, second folds of §7 to §10 (2026-11-16 to 2026-11-17)

The design-records track for §7, §8, §9 and §10's second pass folded the answers below on 2026-11-17, each citing its ledger id: the answers queued for those sections after their first folds, each the recommendation that the owner's instruction of 2026-11-16 accepted.

556. **A base carries the kind of each restored binding's admission it leaves in its tail: `ProjectionHeader.restored_cosigned_ids = 12` lists those filed with `self_admit` off (option 1, the recommendation, 2026-11-16).**
    - Question: owner decision 314 fixes a restored binding's admission's kind at its entry, `PROPOSAL_KIND_RESTORED` (3) when filed with `self_admit` on and `PROPOSAL_KIND_RESTORED_COSIGNED` (4) when filed with it off, so that a `self_admit` change between the proposal and its tick decides nothing. But `self_admit` changes only at a base, and every verifier rebuilds S(b) from the base's rows and re-applies the tail by its verdicts, opening a tail proposal's row with the base's `self_admit`. A base whose own head changes `self_admit` while keeping its fold, the path every head edit takes, therefore decided the kind of each restored binding's admission in its tail, alike at every verifier; and a new device or a rows-only gate that starts at that base cannot recover the setting at the entry, since the rows carry only the base's settings and `tail_verdicts` only "took effect" and "completed".
    - Answer: the base carries the kind. `ProjectionHeader` gains `repeated bytes restored_cosigned_ids = 12`: the ascending proposal ids, among the base's tail entries that took effect, of restored bindings' admissions filed with `self_admit` off, which the writer fills from the kinds it judged or took. A verifier present at the tail recomputes it and forks on a difference, and api answers 403; a verifier that starts at the base, a new device or a rows-only gate, takes it. Reapply opens those rows with kind 4 and every other restored binding's admission with kind 3, never by the base's own `self_admit`. This is DESIGN-DETAILS 5.2.1's principle, a verdict judged once, at the entry's position, by the verifiers present there, every later verifier taking it from the next base, applied to the one part of an entry's judgment the tail's verdict bits do not carry, so owner decision 314 holds at every base, one whose own head changes `self_admit` while keeping its fold included. Until the field lands the code follows option 3, which keeps every verifier agreeing, and §2.8 api-7's phrase names the field (§7.9's self_admit, base and verdicts rows and `ProjectionHeader`).
    - Cost: a header field with its api-7 phrase, a compare at each base, and the writer's fill of the field.
    - Not taken: (2) forbidding the case, a base whose rows' `self_admit` differs from the previous base's folding past every restored binding's admission open in its tail, so a writer whose fold the bridge ledger stops below such a proposal cannot change `self_admit` until the proposal closes or the ledger moves; (3) accepting it as built, the change deciding the kind of a proposal left in such a base's tail, so within the 72 h window an off-to-on change by a base that keeps its fold lets a restored admission filed with `self_admit` off activate with no admin's cosign.
    - Raised by the build (the proposal-kind-restored-cosigned track; the build's ledger, QD-1005). It follows owner decision 314.

557. **`AclRepo` carries `holders_shrunk_time_ms = 13`, the time of the entry at its `holders_shrunk_at`, so every verifier reads owner decision 315's t_s from the rows (option 1, the recommendation, 2026-11-17).**
    - Question: owner decision 315 has entry n, a base included, also shrink repo R when it drops a holder of KeyHolders(R, n-1, t_s) from KeyHolders(R, n, t_s), t_s being the time of the entry at R's `holders_shrunk_at`, or R's creation when that is 0, and every verifier must compute it alike, since `holders_shrunk_at` is compiled into the rows `rows_sha256` covers and a reader present at the previous entry recomputes a base's `shrunk_repo_ids`. The rows carried `holders_shrunk_at` as a position with no time, and a new device, a cold gate and api after a reload start at a base and hold entries only from the retention point, so nothing every verifier holds gave t_s for a repo whose last shrink or making is older. The build meanwhile kept t_s in memory where every verifier held it alike and closed the comparison elsewhere, exact for a repo whose holders do not change with time but over-counting for one holding a lapsed holder, which rotated with cause REMOVE once more each fold span.
    - Answer: `AclRepo` gains `int64 holders_shrunk_time_ms = 13`: the time_ms of the entry at `holders_shrunk_at`, or of the RepoCreate's entry while that is 0. ACL's fold sets it at the RepoCreate, and TEAM's `configSetShrunkAt` sets it with `holders_shrunk_at`; the config repo's own row, which no entry makes, reads the Genesis's `time_ms` until its first shrink writes both fields. Bases carry it as they carry `holders_shrunk_at`, and §2.8 api-7 gains "and its time" beside `holders_shrunk_at`, through an entry in `check/`'s additions naming the question (owner decision 252); it is a time api stamped itself, so the server learns nothing new. Every verifier, a new device, a cold gate and api after a reload included, then computes owner decision 315's second comparison exactly at t_s, the in-memory map and the closed reading go, and a repo holding a lapsed holder no longer rotates once more each fold span, as owner decisions 307 (`admitted_time_ms`) and 343 (`deleted_time_ms`) took the same step for the same reason (§7.9's shrink row, §8.8's `AclRows` and Repo rows, §2.8 api-7).
    - Cost: one field, about 8 B a repo in the rows, and one api-7 phrase.
    - Not taken: (2) keeping the built reading as the rule, with no format change, a repo holding a lapsed holder rotating once more each fold span, and owner decision 315's text saying the comparison is at t_s only where a verifier holds it; (3) every verifier and api keeping entries back to each repo's last shrink, unbounded retention against §7.9's retention row.
    - Raised by the build (the shrink-two-time-compare track; the build's ledger, QD-1015). It follows owner decision 315.

558. **At an app host's forwarding role, §8.9's targets bind revocations, and a grant, which only a compile applies, is held to the p99 at a quiet moment; the Forwarder keeps its 2 s and 10 s compile delays (option 1, the recommendation, 2026-11-16).**
    - Question: §8.9 sets the targets for rule changes applied on connected clients, revocations included, at p50 1 s and p99 5 s, and DESIGN-DETAILS 4.6 asks for a grant and a revocation within them at an app host with the app's sidecar stopped. The app host's Forwarder runs at DESIGN-DETAILS 4.2.5's `CompileDelay` (2 s) and `CompileMinInterval` (10 s): a revocation the posture can check applies as soon as its hint arrives, with no compile, but a grant is an opening, which only a compile applies, so a grant at a quiet moment applies 2 s after its hint, inside the p99 and outside the p50, and one within 10 s of the previous compile up to 10 s after it, outside both.
    - Answer: an app host's Forwarder keeps `CompileDelay` 2 s and `CompileMinInterval` 10 s on every host class; the battery and the memory cap (DESIGN-DETAILS 4.2.4) are why they exist. At an app host, §8.9's targets bind revocations, which apply on their hint with no compile and no time passing. A grant is an opening, which only a compile applies, and is bound by the p99 alone when no compile ran in the `CompileMinInterval` before it: at a quiet moment it applies `CompileDelay` after its hint (2 s), and within `CompileMinInterval` of the previous compile up to 10 s after it. DESIGN-DETAILS 4.6's row is reworded to say so ("a revocation within §8.9's targets, and a grant at a quiet moment within its p99"), and BRIDGE-M8-07's `FwdAppWithinTargets` keeps measuring a quiet grant against the p99 and the revocation with no compile, so a regression in either shows. No setting or code changes (§8.9's targets).
    - Cost: a grant on a phone can take up to 10 s to apply.
    - Not taken: (2) `CompileDelay` 500 ms and `CompileMinInterval` 4 s on every host class, up to 2.5 times as many compiles under a burst of openings, each up to about 200 ms on a phone at 5,000 devices; (3) the sidecar role's 100 ms and 1 s on the desktop class, whose cap is 64 MiB and which has no battery, grants on phones staying as in option 1.
    - Raised by the build (the bridge-m8-01 track's BRIDGE-M8-07 schedule; the build's ledger, QD-998).

559. **The prefix filer knows a group's atom by the head's group atom ids, whatever its row holds, so it refuses an empty group's atom (option 1, the recommendation, 2026-11-16).**
    - Question: since owner decision 353, compile writes an `AclAtom` row for every group the groups block defines. The prefix filer's check that each prefix rule's subject names someone (owner decision 345: a group's atom names nobody) told a tag's atom from a group's by what its row holds, a row with no member, client, network, role or principal taken for a tag's; a group with no members, an empty groups entry or one whose members have all left the log, holds only its atom id, as a tag with no owners and no sidecars does, so the filer filed a rule naming nobody instead of refusing it, now for every empty group the document defines. It stayed fail-safe: the filed rule granted nobody anything, and only the filing device runs the check.
    - Answer: the prefix filer refuses a group's atom by name. Its check also takes the head's group atom ids, the filing device's `AclLocalPolicy.Names` entries that read `group:`, or its groups table, so a group's atom names nobody whatever its row in `AclRows` holds, an empty group's included, and a tag's row is still taken as naming someone. Owner decision 345's rule then holds whether or not a rule names the group and whatever its row holds, now that owner decision 353 gives every group the document defines a row (§8.8's Atoms and RepoPrefixRules' subjects).
    - Cost: one argument on the filer's path and a test of an empty group's atom refused.
    - Not taken: (2) leaving it, a filer that files, for an empty group, a rule naming nobody, against owner decision 345 and its own doc comment.
    - Raised by the build (the acl-atoms-every-tag track; the build's ledger, QD-1001). It follows owner decisions 345 and 353.

560. **A `ur/` ticket names its dialer's sidecar in `SocketTicket.dialer_sidecar_id = 16`, signed at Find, and smtpedge judges `mail_send` for it (option 1, the recommendation, 2026-11-16).**
    - Question: §14.5 has smtpedge judge §8's `mail_send` for the stream's sidecar, which smtp-2 says it reads from the ticket, but `SocketTicket` carried only `dialer_network_id` and `dialer_client_id`, and nothing at the edge maps a client to its sidecar: `AclState` has no client index, and an api lookup is a read the preamble path may not wait on (I-12). The sockets service knows the caller's sidecar at Find, from the `sidecar_id` claim on its by_client_jwt (owner decision 497). SMTP-M9-13 built a seam answered from memory, a stream whose sidecar is unknown answering TRY_LATER, and no production source filled it.
    - Answer: smtpedge learns the dialing sidecar from the ticket. `SocketTicket` (sock-2) gains `bytes dialer_sidecar_id = 16`, set on `ur/` tickets alone and signed with the rest: the sockets service fills it at Find from the caller's `sidecar_id` claim, which it has already authenticated; `tls/`, `smtp/` and edge tickets leave it empty, edges naming no sidecar. smtpedge judges `mail_send` (`SmtpGate.MaySendMail` through `AclGateView.MaySendMail`) for the ticket's `dialer_sidecar_id`, read from the stream's admitted ticket, so the edge needs no client-to-sidecar lookup or cache and `SmtpDialerSource` gives way; a `ur/` stream whose ticket names no dialer sidecar still answers TRY_LATER, failing closed. Every `ur/` listener may read its subject the same way. The ticket grows about 18 B and stays within `StreamOpen.ticket`'s 512 B. The field joins `SocketTicket`'s fence through protocol's fence additions naming the question until design-records writes it (owner decision 273) (§9.6's ur tickets, §14.5's Authorization, §2.8 sock-2).
    - Cost: a ticket field of about 18 B, and sock-2's words.
    - Not taken: (2) a client-to-sidecar index per network in the gate's projection feed, cached at the edge beside the rows, a new server-visible read and a cache that can lag a binding change by its refresh; (3) `MaySendMail` taking a client subject that the state resolves, which holds no client index, so option 2 inside ACL.
    - Raised by the build (the smtp-m9-13 track; the build's ledger, QD-989).

561. **The revocation record keeps team memberships and their users' credential cutoffs, so sockets checks a member's token with no SQL (option 1, the recommendation, 2026-11-17).**
    - Question: owner decisions 347 and 497 have sockets check a Client-class token against its Redis revocation record alone, never SQL. A team member's credential, and every client credential derived from it, carries `member_id` (§7.5), and its state check reads `network_member` in place of the network's admin user, which in a team is a synthetic owner of record. The record kept no membership, its `n:user` being the network's admin user and `n:cred` that user's cutoff, so every member credential went to SQL, and the no-SQL verifier answered it unavailable for good: in a team network no member's sidecar could register a listener, Find or list at sockets.
    - Answer: the revocation record keeps membership, so sockets keeps reading the record alone and a team member's sidecar can use sockets with §19.10's outage behavior. Two kinds of fields join a network's record: `m:<member_id>` holds `a <user_id> <reauth_deadline_ms>` (0 for no deadline) for a joined, admitted membership, projected by the SQL fallback with the positive ttl as it projects `c:` and `r:`, never for a pending member, and a removal, and the end of a membership a re-join replaces, writes the tombstone `x` before it commits, as client removals do; `u:<user_id>` holds the member user's credential_change_time, written wherever `n:cred` is written (a reset, sign-out-all). For a token carrying `member_id`, the sockets verifier and api's own record path read `m:` and `u:` in place of `n:user` and `n:cred`, and refuse a tombstone, another user, a passed reauth deadline or a cutoff after the token's creation. TEAM's membership writers write the tombstone first; session's fallback and the record's parser change; §2.8's row for the record family gains member and user ids (I-18); an FS case covers a removal racing a projection. Credentials descending from an API key stay outside this answer (§9.8's client tokens, §7.5's members, §19.10's Postgres row).
    - Cost: two kinds of record fields, a tombstone before each membership removal commits, changes to session's fallback and the record's parser, and an FS case.
    - Not taken: (2) members unavailable at sockets until a later milestone, so a team's sidecars publish no `tls/` name and Find nothing, and M6's Team scope hosts fail for members; (3) sockets asking api for a member credential's state over its internal gate bearer, cached per member, a cold check reaching Postgres behind api, so §19.10's hour of Postgres loss would not hold for members.
    - Raised by the build (the sock-client-verifier track; the build's ledger, QD-1013). It follows owner decisions 347 and 497.

562. **A sockets ticket key settles `SettleTime` after the later of its creation and a block's first read of it, so a slow hand placement only delays the switch (option 1, the recommendation, 2026-11-17).**
    - Question: `SocketTicketKeySet` treated a key as settled `SettleTime` (10 min) after its `created_millis`, the moment `sidecarctl sockets ticket-keygen` ran, by when every live listener holds it from a registration answer or `GET /socket/keys`. Owner decision 354 has the operator place the file in vault by hand, and nothing tied that placement to the run's time: a file that reached the blocks more than about `SettleTime` after the run was already settled when a block first read it, so that block signed with it before any listener held it, and dials under it were refused for up to about 75 s a rotation. The command printed a placement deadline it could not enforce.
    - Answer: `SocketTicketKeySet` settles a key `SettleTime` after the later of its `created_millis` and the first read in which this process saw it, keeping a per-process map from key id to that first sight, so a slow hand placement only delays the switch, and the key set's invariant holds however long the hand step takes: no ticket names a key a listener has not been given. A restarted process sees every key as new, so for its first `SettleTime` the oldest live key that outlives `SignMargin` signs, a key every listener already holds; different blocks may sign under different keys for up to a reread interval, which every listener verifies. No format, wire or D- item changes; the command drops its placement deadline line, and the owner's monthly runbook stays "run the command, place the file" (§9.8).
    - Cost: a small per-process map and a change to the key set and its tests.
    - Not taken: (2) the command taking a not-before time, or setting `created_millis` ahead, a meaning change in the key file that leaves the operator to judge the delay; (3) keeping it as built, the command and the runbook telling the operator to place the file within minutes, a late placement costing up to about 75 s of refused dials a rotation.
    - Raised by the build (the sockets-ticket-keygen track; the build's ledger, QD-1016). It follows owner decision 354.

563. **gkm's guard honors a route's rights only whole, as evs's does, so a deleted repo's standing admins pass only gkm's admin routes (option 1, the recommendation, 2026-11-16).**
    - Question: owner decision 351 gives a repo whose newest RepoDelete stands unrestored one right, `admin`, held by its standing admins alone, and says gkm's guard reads the same rights and its own repo/ recheck refuses its writes. Each guard honored one right a request, the strongest the token holds of those the route takes, so a token minted with `admin`, which carries `rw` and `r`, was held to `admin` on every route that takes `r`, `rw` or `admin`, and passed. The build closed it at evs, whose guard now asks the state for the strongest right together with each weaker right of its role that the route takes; gkm stayed as it was, so a deleted repo's standing admin passed gkm's reads (GroupForms, a group, its epochs, Welcomes) for as long as the RepoDelete stood, and its writes until repo/ showed deleted.
    - Answer: gkm's guard takes evs's rule over the one rights table (D-ACL-21): it asks the state for the strongest right the token holds of those the route's scope row takes together with each weaker right of its role that the row takes, and the state honors that set only whole. A live repo's admin or writer is asked nothing more. A deleted repo's standing admins, who hold `admin` alone, pass only gkm's admin-scope routes (group create, re-form and the ladder), which repo/'s state closes outside a re-seed, and are refused gkm's reads (GroupForms, a group, its epochs, Welcomes) and its other writes (KeyPackage claim, Welcome write, generation claim, epoch commit, GroupInfo, wrap batches) from the moment the RepoDelete is in the gate's state, before repo/ shows deleted as after. §19.10's re-seed keeps the GroupForm and ladder it needs on a repo re-seeded as deleted. This meets §11.12's refused row at gkm for that admin's reads, which owner decision 351's wording left open, as well as its writes (§10.6's checks row, §8.8's Repo rows).
    - Cost: a change to gkm's guard, to its state's contract and to every fake of it; nothing for a live repo.
    - Not taken: (2) leaving gkm as owner decision 351 words it, its reads open to that admin while the RepoDelete stands and its writes until repo/ shows deleted, short of §11.12's refused row; (3) the deleted repo's admin right at the evs audience only, the exchange minting no gkm scope, which closes gkm whole but leaves §19.10's re-seed, whose GroupForm and ladder are admin routes, needing a rule of its own.
    - Raised by the build (the evs-deleted-repo-admin-right track; the build's ledger, QD-975). It follows owner decision 351.

## The build's owner questions on encrypted versioned storage and certificates, second folds of §11 and §13 (2026-11-16 to 2026-11-17)

The design-records track for §11, §12 and §13's second pass folded the answers below on 2026-11-17, each citing its ledger id: the answers queued for those sections after their first folds, each the recommendation that the owner's instruction of 2026-11-16 accepted. §12 had none. A decision that groups several answers carries its latest answer's date.

564. **A re-seeded push in parts' part and seal records die only by the fence, as a one-part reseed record does, and the reseed mode's seal refuses a part place older than a settings bound, 24 h by default (option 1, the recommendation, 2026-11-16).**
    - Question: owner decision 404 has evacuation resolve a one-part reseed record by its statement with the fence in place of the time clause, since the reseed mode skips the statement's time window and a re-seeded statement is as old as the mirror it came from. The two other records a re-seed writes, a re-seeded push's part (an EVS_BATCH_RESEED record holding a part's body) and its seal (the EVS_BATCH_SEAL record the reseed mode writes, owner decision 402), still resolved by the push's own rule, whose time clause calls an uncommitted one dead on any head holding a fence_ms at all; and the reseed mode puts no bound between a part call and its seal, so a seal arriving after evacuation had judged its parts dead, and before the run deleted their batch, could commit a version whose parts the run then deleted (I-EVS-2), and an older fence ended no seal attempt still in flight.
    - Answer: owner decision 404's rule extends to both records: a re-seeded part and a reseed seal resolve by their statement with the fence in place of the time clause, so either is dead only on a head whose fence_ms is at or past the time the evacuation run held the record, never because its statement's time_ms + 35 min has passed. The reseed mode's seal refuses 409 PARTS_MISSING, refusing and recording nothing, any part place whose batch minute is older than a new settings bound, 24 h by default (the push route's receipt figure, well under the 25 h after which evacuation can judge a part), so the device sends those parts again; no seal can then name a part old enough for evacuation to have judged it, and I-EVS-2 holds for re-seeded pushes in parts whatever the device's timing. Resolve tells a reseed seal from a pushed one by its parts' record type, or applies the fence rule to every seal, which only moves a pushed seal's drop from its time clause to a fence; FI-6's oracle takes the same rule (§11.8's parts, seal and Resolve rows, §11.13's resolve and re-seed rows).
    - Cost: one settings field and one check in the reseed seal's round, the Resolve rule's reseed clause widened, and FI-6's oracle.
    - Not taken: (2) keeping both records' rule and having evacuation resolve a re-seeded part again, on a head read from the cluster, just before it deletes the part's batch, which narrows the race and leaves the window between that read and the DELETE open; (3) accepting it as a device fault, the device re-seeding the version from its mirror on its next walk, so I-EVS-2 holds for re-seeded pushes in parts only by the device's timing.
    - Raised by the build (the reseed-record-resolve-fence track; the build's ledger, QD-974). It follows owner decisions 402 and 404.

565. **§11.12's re-seed exception holds under the real gate state: on PUT .../segments alone, evs's guard passes a deleted repo's standing admin holding `admin` alone, marked, while the host's reseed gate is on, and the upload takes the request only through the exception (option 1, the recommendation, 2026-11-16).**
    - Question: owner decision 351 gives a repo whose newest RepoDelete stands unrestored the evs `admin` right alone, held by its standing admins, and evs's guard asks the state for the strongest right a token holds together with each weaker right of its role that the route takes, honoring that set only whole, as gkm's does under owner decision 563. PUT .../segments takes rw, deposit or admin, so such an admin's upload was refused 403 SCOPE at the guard, before it reached §11.12's re-seed exception, a repo re-seeded as deleted taking its segments back, which was therefore unreachable under the real gate state: a recovery took a restorable deleted repo's pushes through POST .../reseed, an admin-only route, but not their segments. No test showed it, since the re-seed tests ran on a fake state that granted everything.
    - Answer: PUT .../segments, the one route that carries the exception, says so in its route entry (an `EvsRoute` field set on it alone). On that route, when the state refuses the whole right set the guard asks, the host's reseed gate is on and the state honors the bare admin right, the guard passes the request holding `admin` alone, marked as admitted by the exception; the upload then takes a marked request only through the exception (reseed on, repo/ deleted under the unrestored RepoDelete the projection ends in, inside the 7 d restore window, the request holding `admin`) and refuses it 403 SCOPE otherwise, as the guard did, in particular for a keying or active repo/ in the time between the RepoDelete reaching the gate's state and repo/ showing deleted. Every other route keeps the whole-set rule, so owner decision 351's "no read, write or deposit" holds, and a repo re-seeded as deleted takes its segments back in a recovery (§11.12's refused and re-seed rows, §19.10's repos row).
    - Cost: a route field, the guard's marked pass and the upload's check, with a test under the real gate state.
    - Not taken: (2) leaving it refused, so a restorable deleted repo's history does not come back whole in a recovery and §11.12's exception would need restating; (3) the guard honoring the one strongest right again, so a deleted repo's admin token passes every read and write route until repo/ shows deleted, against owner decision 351.
    - Raised by the build (the evs-deleted-repo-admin-right track; the build's ledger, QD-977). It follows owner decisions 351 and 563.

566. **A reader's walk agrees with a removal it holds: a served removal of a path with a later walked removal of a prefix holding it, and a plain absent reply with a walk whose newest change of the path, or of a prefix holding it, is a removal (option 1 of the first question and option 2 of the second, the recommendations, 2026-11-16).**
    - Question: two cases in which a reader's walk check refused an honest evs. evs answers a path that was already removed when a prefix holding it was removed with the path's own, older removal (§11.7's removal row: a marker stays as it is), and the reader's walk refused a walked removal of a prefix after a served removal as a later statement changing the path, raising the tamper alarm, in the inline form and the rooted one, so after a writer removed d/a.txt and then d, every read of d/a.txt alarmed, and a writer pinned before both removals could not re-create it. And owner decision 380 has evs serve a collected removal from the window to a reader whose walk shows it, which reaches only as far as the window: a removal leaves the window within about 64 to 127 versions of its commit on a busy ref, often before the nightly collection, after which evs has nothing that names it and serves the path plain absent, which a reader whose pin is older than the removal read as a path never written and refused with the alarm, a device then stuck, since its record's pin moves only at the read being refused.
    - Answer: the reader's rule changes, and evs and §11.7's removal row stay as they are. A removed path stays removed through a later removal of a prefix holding it: where evs serves a path's own removal at r, a walked removal of a prefix after r agrees with the reply, as it already did for a path served plain absent, and only a walked write of the path, or of a prefix that writes an entry over it, refutes the reply, in the inline form and the rooted one. Where evs serves a path plain absent, as it does once a collected removal has paged out of the window (owner decision 380's window case staying as built), the reader takes the reply as agreeing with its own verified walk when the newest walked change of the path, or of a prefix holding it, is a removal; only a walked write of the path with no walked removal of it or of a prefix after it refutes absent and raises the alarm. The proof of absence is then the walk's signed statements, not evs's word, so I-EVS-8 and the absence check lose nothing, and it holds at any distance behind the window with no new evs reads; a creation on such a reply takes max(b, m) + 1 with no m, b being past any collected removal (§11.8), so the planner is unchanged (§11.9's reply and reader rows).
    - Cost: changes to the reader's walk check, inline and rooted, and to its absence rule; nothing at evs.
    - Not taken: for a prefix's removal, (2) evs serving the newest removal that covers the path, the prefix's, which changes §11.7's removal row and what a marker means and carries a path version the path never took; for a paged-out removal, (1) evs following the removal below the window through the log pages, which needs a page bound past which a reader stays stuck, and (3) leaving a device pinned below a collected, paged-out removal stuck behind a tamper alarm on an honest evs.
    - Raised by the build (the evs-removal-from-window track; the build's ledger, QD-978, QD-979). It follows owner decision 380.

567. **Any config writer whose config admin evs's guard honors continues the config repo's standing placement; every other kind keeps 409 REPO_PLACED for all but place/'s creator (option 2, the recommendation, 2026-11-16).**
    - Question: owner decision 352 gives every config writer `admin` on the config repo at evs and gkm, so another config writer can re-form its group, post its ladder and activate or re-place it after a restore. §11.8's place row continued a standing place/ only for the same creator, any other getting 409 REPO_PLACED and picking another id (owner decision 411), but the Genesis fixes the config repo's id, so a config repo whose place/ names a bootstrap sidecar that is gone and whose repo/ was never written, its create stopped between place/ and repo/ or a restore having brought back the first metadata cluster's place/ but not the placed cluster's repo/, could be placed by no one, and the network had no config repo.
    - Answer: for the config kind alone, PUT /evs/v1/repos/<config_repo_id> continues a standing placement for any sidecar whose config admin evs's guard honored (every config writer, owner decision 352), not only for place/'s creator: the second step keeps the cluster place/ names and its creator as written, and the third writes repo/ If-None-Match as before, so racing creates still converge on one repo/ and place/ stays write-once. Every other kind keeps "any other gets 409" with REPO_PLACED, since its client can pick another id. So such a config repo is placed by another config writer, as owner decision 352's re-placing after a restore describes (§11.8's place row).
    - Cost: one clause in the placement's continuation and a test at the real gates.
    - Not taken: (1) the rule unchanged for every kind, such a config repo recovered only by §19.10's re-seed mode or an operator's hand on place/; (3) rewriting place/ for the config kind to name the new creator, which breaks "place/ never changes", which evs and gkm cache for the process's life.
    - Raised by the build (the config-writers-admin track; the build's ledger, QD-982). It follows owner decision 352.

568. **A read by number below a re-created path scans at most `EvsSettings.ReadVersionScanCount` versions below the window, to the newest cut-over below the creation, and answers 503 past the bound, both limits stated, and from the review phase mailcfg's readers replay the ref from genesis (option 1 now and option 3 for mailcfg in the review phase, the recommendation, 2026-11-16).**
    - Question: owner decision 380 has a read by number that the path holds no version at answer its newest version below it, so History walks into the removal before a creation and the life it ended, costed as a scan of the window statements a read already holds. That covers a creation still in the window, or whose removal is; it does not cover the rest, since a creation's fold replaces what the entry held, carrying nothing, and a collected marker leaves nothing, so once the creation is folded and its removal has paged out of the window the earlier life is only in the ref's log pages; and every History walk of a path never re-created ends with a read below its first creation, which evs could answer "nothing" honestly only by reading the log down to version 1. A scan below a creation also finds the path only in the epochs the request names it in.
    - Answer: now, as built: a read by number below a re-created path scans the ref's statements below the entry and the window, newest first through their log pages, in the epochs the request names the path in, at most `EvsSettings.ReadVersionScanCount` versions (16,384, about 256 log pages); past the bound it answers 503, unfinished, never "nothing", History ends with BelowUnknown, Lookup answers unknown, and mailcfg's reader keeps such a record unknown to Resolve, as it does one past its replay bound. The scan ends at the newest cut-over below the creation, since the request's epochs stay at or above the path's floor. Both limits are stated: §11.9 gains the bound and the cut-over, and §15.16's risks gain that fresh readers of a large or long-lived mailcfg ref do not honor a record past the bound, a member who can write mailcfg being able to push that many versions to make older records unknown to fresh readers, and that a fresh reader judges a re-creation across a cut-over on its new life alone. In the review phase mailcfg's readers replay the ref from genesis, as §11.7's refused row already describes them: a fresh reader reads every statement of the mailcfg ref through GET .../log and every record version by number and commit_version, judging each life from the statements themselves, so no evs answer below a creation decides a mail record, and History's bounded crossing stays for display and Lookup. That closes both limits for mail routing without a format change or trusting evs (§11.9's reply and rounds rows, §15.16).
    - Cost: until the review phase, fresh readers of a large or long-lived mailcfg ref lose records past the bound, and a re-creation across a cut-over stays open on them; the replay then reads a mailcfg ref's whole history once, its statements small and inline.
    - Not taken: (2) the fold keeping a creation's predecessor in a new EvsIndexEntry field that the path-epoch copy carries, so evs answers below a creation in one GET: a format field and changes to the fold, copy and collect rows, a marker collected before the re-creation still found only by a scan, and an earlier life across a cut-over still needing a token path the request does not name.
    - Raised by the build (the evs-read-earlier-lives track; the build's ledger, QD-1006). It follows owner decision 380.

569. **Amending owner decision 414: a path epoch's due class holds an earlier pin its reader no longer retains to its number, as §10.6's gate does, and needs only the states around each shrink after it (option 2, the recommendation, 2026-11-17).**
    - Question: owner decision 414 has evs and its clients class a path epoch's due crossing from the log's entries between the auth pins of the generation it crosses from and the one it crosses to, a party that cannot reach the earlier pin's state reading immediate. A reader's window runs from its previous base to the tip, about one to two days by §7.9's fold row, while a generation lives up to 30 days, so on a repo that last rotated before the reader's previous base an agent's ordinary revocation still read immediate, blocking creation on its general, config and mailcfg refs and forcing an immediate epoch, and the log-based class helped only repos rotated within the window. The walk does not need the earlier pin's state, only the states just before and at each shrink after the pin's number, and that number.
    - Answer: a path epoch's due class holds an earlier pin the reader no longer retains to its number, as §10.6's gate already does ("not judged and counts against no one ... its number still has to reach n_r"), since that pin's chain was checked when it was new, by members' rotation checks and by evs's gate on the pushes that declared it. The walk needs only S(s-1) and S(s) for each shrink s after the earlier pin's number, plus that number; it still reads immediate on a pin off the chain or ahead, on a pin above the later one, when any S(s-1), S(s) or kept entry the walk needs is outside the reader's window, or when any shrink classes immediate. §11.6's "a party that cannot reach that state" becomes "a party that cannot reach the states around a shrink in between". So an agent's ordinary removal reads batched on quiet repos whose generation was rotated before the reader's previous base, where creation blocks hurt most and what owner decision 414's log-based class was chosen for; every other fail-closed reading stays (§11.6's due row).
    - Cost: the class rests on the old GenRotate's pin number without a chain check, which the gate's coverage already trusts the same way; a small change to the span walk and its tests.
    - Not taken: (1) keeping the built reading, a crossing whose earlier pin lies before the reader's window reading immediate whenever an entry in between shrank the holders, so an agent's ordinary removal blocks creation and forces an immediate epoch on most quiet repos.
    - Raised by the build (the evs-due-class-from-log track; the build's ledger, QD-1018).

570. **Amending owner decision 512: `sidecarctl evs drop-reindex` takes a job's drops from its snap-0 when no list file reads whole and refuses named drops that differ from snap-0's, and the drop job keeps snap-0 for the repo's life (option 1, the recommendation, 2026-11-17).**
    - Question: owner decision 512 has `sidecarctl evs drop-reindex <repo_id> <job_id>` with no drops named take them from the first of the job's list files that reads whole. A job writes one list file per 131,072 listed versions, so most have one, and when drop_index_corrupt pages for it none reads whole: the command refused, and the operator had to name every drop the job covers by hand, from the job's snap-0 file or the drop table's entries, both binary with no tool that prints them and both gone 30 days after the job ends, after which nothing recorded which drops the job covered, so an older job's corrupt list file could not be rebuilt and evs answered the reads it could not clear 503 for the repo's life. Reindex also trusted named drops: a set that left one of the job's drops out resolved and was written, so once that drop's table entry had left, a version it removed, an inline record's bytes among them, would be served again.
    - Answer: the command reads the job's snap-0 through `evsservice.DropFiles.Read(DropFileSnap, 0)`: with no drops named and no list file reading whole, it passes snap-0's covered drops to Reindex and prints them, ids only; with drops named and snap-0 present, it refuses, writing nothing, unless the named set equals snap-0's. The drop job's cleanup keeps snap-0 for the repo's life, as it keeps the list files (84 B a drop), so an older job's corrupt list file can still be rebuilt; snap files of later rounds, cand, mark, del, held, ask and res still go 30 d after their job ends. §2.8's evs-28 says snap-0's place and seen_ms are kept as long as the list files (metadata list v47), and the file clusters' runbook row says the command finds the drops itself, naming them being checked against snap-0 (§11.8's dropscan/ row, §11.13's table row, §19.8's page row).
    - Cost: a small command change, one retention line in the drop job's cleanup and evs-28's words; 84 B a covered drop kept for the repo's life.
    - Not taken: (2) the command change alone, snap-0 keeping its 30 days, so a recent job recovers with no hand decoding and an older job's corrupt file stays 503 for the repo's life; (3) as built, the operator decoding snap-0 or the table by hand under a page, a set missing a drop re-exposing what it dropped.
    - Raised by the build (the sidecarctl-evs-drop-reindex track; the build's ledger, QD-1021). It follows owner decision 398.

571. **Amending owner decision 424: each custom-domain budget's replay starts full at its key's latest quiet window, else empty at the retention's start, and spends are kept 16 d (option 2 of each question, the recommendations, 2026-11-16).**
    - Question: owner decision 424 had each custom-domain budget's replay start empty two windows back, at now − 2 × cap × refill, with spends kept 15 d (QD-990). A replay is exact only when it reaches back to a moment the bucket was full, and under steady pacing after a burst a host's exact-set bucket is never full, so at a host's 10th device, 204 h after a burst of four, the replay started at −136 h, read one token under the truth and refused the claim with 34 h waits until the burst left it, at 340 h (14.2 d), where the design details' pacing table counts 8.5 d; any fixed window count has the same edge further out. A replay from the key's latest quiet window then needs a retention longer than the host's busy stretch by one window, which 15 d gave only through the 9th device.
    - Answer: each custom-domain budget's replay starts from its key's latest quiet window: the newest instant t at or before now with no spend of the key in (t − cap × refill, t], where the CA's bucket is full whatever came before. It starts full at t and replays the spends after it, and where no such instant lies within the retained spends it starts empty at the retention's start. Spends are kept 16 d (384 h, `CertsCustomSettings.SpendRetention`), not 15 d. The level is never above the truth, and it is exact for any number of a host's devices while the host's busy stretch is shorter than the retention less one window, 214 h at 16 d: each device after a burst of four is granted 34 h after the one before, the 10th at 8.5 d and the 11th after a window's pause, and a refusal's `retry_after_ms` is answered by a grant. There is no level counter; a grant reads all of a key's retained spends instead of two windows', at most a few dozen rows for the set and domain buckets, and cert-1's published words say spends 16 d and the last 16 days (metadata list v41) (§13.8's custom-domain orders, §2.8's cert-1).
    - Cost: one more day of spend rows, and a grant reading a key's whole retention rather than two windows.
