    - Not taken: for the start, (1) keeping two windows, the edge moving with the window count (three windows serve 14 devices, with spends kept 22 d), and (3) a level row per key updated in the grant's transaction, the counter the design details avoided and one a restore behind the truth no longer repairs; for the retention, (1) keeping 15 d, a host's 10th device at 14.2 d, and (3) 30 d, pacing through the 20-device row at the cost of a claim reading up to about 14,000 retained new-order rows at the account bucket's cap rate.
    - Raised by the build (the tls-m6-04 track; the build's ledger, QD-994, QD-996).

## The build's owner questions on records and search, subnet routes, the apps, server services, quotas and abuse, second folds of §14 to §23 (2026-11-16 to 2026-11-17)

The design-records track for §14 to §23's second pass folded the answers below on 2026-11-17, each citing its ledger id: the answers queued for those sections after their first folds, each the recommendation that the owner's instruction of 2026-11-16 accepted. §14, §15, §22 and §23 had none of their own; §22's QD-1008 went into §22.8 with sidecartest/fault's catalog before this pass, and §15 and §22 carry parts of the answers below.

572. **§16.10's cold-contract arrival bound bounds a shard's hits reaching the caller, not only the engine's search of the shard (option 1, the recommendation, 2026-11-16).**
    - Question: §16.10's cold contract reports each shard's hits within 100 ms of the shard landing, 300 ms on phones and page Workers. The engine searched each landed shard at once but queued and ranked its hits only when the page's download stopped, which ran shard after shard until the page's `PageCpu` (2 s) ended or every shard was fetched, so a shard's hits reached the caller up to `PageCpu` after it landed: 0.78 s and 1.55 s on FS-110's fake clock for the desktop and the phone. The drafted reading took the bound as one on the engine's search of the shard, which it met, while FS-110's row, "each shard's hits within the arrival bound after it lands, none before", reads as delivery.
    - Answer: the bound is on delivery, since it bounds what a person sees, and §22.8's first-hit figures are delivery times. The engine returns a page as soon as a landed shard queues a hit, or stops the download at each landed shard that queued one and resumes it through the cursor on the next page, so a match found early in a long download is not held until the page's `PageCpu` ends, and FS-110 fails a hit that arrives past the bound as well as one before its shard lands. The drafted reading, the engine's per-shard search, is superseded (§16.10's arrival row, §18.4's targets, §22.8's search row and FS-110).
    - Cost: a change to the engine's page loop, and FS-110's arrival clause failing a late hit.
    - Not taken: (2) the bound on the engine's search of the shard, delivery waiting up to `PageCpu`, which §16.10 would then say, a 2 s wait a page hiding a match found early in a long download.
    - Raised by the build (the search-m10-38 track; the build's ledger, QD-999).

573. **A phone leases each approval log whole to learn of requests, and should T4's wake counts show capture pushes spending the phones' wake budget, the review phase gives the vault a notice per pending request that phones lease alone (option 1 now and option 2 in the review phase if needed, the recommendation, 2026-11-16).**
    - Question: §16.6 sends an `ApprovalRequest` entry to the approver's log and on to the approver's device "by evs hint, else §18.7 wake", and a phone is woken for a repo only by a lease it holds there (§11.10), but the vault has no path that holds approval entries alone: every entry is a file in its writer's stream, captures, receipts and approvals alike. So a phone leases each approval log whole, and every push to the vault hints it or wakes it; a member's daemons push about 2,400 times a member-day, while §18.7 coalesces to one wake per client per 10 s and APNs takes about three background pushes an hour, so a request filed just after capture pushes spent the hour's budget can wait up to an hour for its wake, and each wake's resync reads the vault's new entries to find the requests.
    - Answer: phones keep leasing each approval log whole, as built, which is §16.6's own text and fails no rule. In the review phase, if §22.8's wake counts on T4's surrogates show capture pushes spending the phones' wake budget, the vault gains a notice per pending request, as the team directory has (D-SDK-28, §16.11's `q/` notices): the requester writes a small file under a reserved prefix, `a/<approval_id>`, beside its request entry, a decision or cancel removes or rewrites it, phones lease that prefix alone, and a repair rule like the directory's covers a crash between the entry and the notice (§16.6's flow, §16.8's approvals).
    - Cost: until then, the wake budget above; with the notices, a format addition to the vault's layout in RECORD.md and §16.8, a write per request and a repair rule.
    - Not taken: (2) the notices now; (3) the requesting daemon summoning the member's app hosts with `POST /sidecar/wake` when it files a request, which shares api's hourly limit per summoned device with HeadCheck's summonses, so a second request within the hour wakes nothing.
    - Raised by the build (the mcp-m8-01 track; the build's ledger, QD-1009).

574. **An `ApprovalRequest` names the uses its agent requests for a template, daemon-built and rendered, which the approver signs as `ApprovalDecision.uses`, so an app approves a template request as shown (option 2, the recommendation, 2026-11-16).**
    - Question: §16.6 lets a decision with uses above 1 bind a template, a `CanonicalAction` with `scope = true`, and the approver refuses uses above 1 for anything but a template; `ur approve --uses <n>` gave the count, since `ApprovalRequest` names none, but §18.5's `Decide(approvalId, decision, callback)` takes no count, so an app had no way to say how many uses it approves, and on an app a template request listed deny alone.
    - Answer: the count is part of what the person approves, so it belongs among the daemon-built fields every approver renders the same way. `ApprovalRequest` gains the uses the agent requests for a template, daemon-built, covered by `request_sha256` and rendered beside the action's other daemon-built fields, a request for anything but a template carrying 1; the approver signs that count as `ApprovalDecision.uses`, and §18.5's `Decide` approves the request as shown, on an app as on a daemon. `ur approve --uses` defaults to the requested count, and a different count is still refused or narrowed as before (uses above 1 only for a template, never more than requested). The record.proto field is a format change after M3's freeze and takes the version bump RECORD.md's rules give it; until it lands, an app lists deny alone for a template request (§16.6's multi-use row, §16.8's fence, §18.5's `Decide`).
    - Cost: a record.proto field and its version bump, a render line and §18.5's copy.
    - Not taken: (1) deny alone on apps, so a person whose only approval-capable device is a phone cannot approve an agent's template; (3) a count in §18.5's API, a `DecideUses` beside `Decide`, a bound method on gomobile, the C ABI and js, the app's UI asking the person.
    - Raised by the build (the mcp-m8-01 track; the build's ledger, QD-1010). It follows owner decision 457.

575. **A route rule that names no `ip:` destination and no `autogroup:internet` reaches, through each advertiser its via subjects hold for the source that is also among its subject destinations for that source, every prefix of that advertiser's routes in force, whole, so Team's grant 1 reaches the prefixes a member's own devices' approved routes serve (option 2, the recommendation, 2026-11-17).**
    - Question: owner decision 344 gave Team's grant 1 `"via": ["autogroup:self"]` beside `"dst": ["autogroup:self"]`, so a member's devices reach the prefixes their own devices' approved routes serve. At the ACL layer they did, but the bridge compile gave a host an entry for an advertiser's route in force only for a rule of that advertiser's via meeting its prefix by the sealed policy's CIDRs, and a subject's route rows only its CIDRs' parts of what the host serves; grant 1's only destination is a subject, which holds no CIDR, so its `route` reached no prefix on the data path. The filer named a rule in a route's `route_rule_ids`, and a standing grant covered a route, only by the rule's prefixes, and `AclEngine.AllowRoute` read a rule with no prefix as reaching the via's own addresses but checked no address, so `ur bridge`'s previews said allowed for any address through a member's own device.
    - Answer: such a rule reaches, through each advertiser that its via subjects hold for the source and that is also among the rule's subject destinations for that source, every prefix of that advertiser's subnet routes in force, whole, its exposed prefix included. Grant 1, whose destination and via are both `autogroup:self`, then reaches exactly the prefixes a member's own devices' approved routes serve, and a rule whose destinations name none of its vias opens nothing new: under `route` a subject destination's routes in force are what such a rule reaches, through the vias that hold their advertiser. The compile gives such a rule an entry and a route row for each served prefix whole; exits stay as they are, only an internet or /0 rule giving exit rows; an activated grant with no local detail stays closed (owner decision 220); the filer's `route_rule_ids` and a standing grant's cover name and cover by the same reading; and `AclEngine.AllowRoute` answers `ur bridge`'s previews by it instead of allowing any address (§8.4's readings, §17.7's advertising).
    - Cost: the compile reads a rule's destinations as well as its vias for route entries and rows, a second meaning for a subject destination under `route`.
    - Not taken: (1) a via alone standing for its routes, so a prefixless route rule with a via reaches every prefix its via serves, its destinations playing no part; (3) as built, grant 1's prefixes unreached on the data path until a Team names `ip:` prefixes, and previews saying allowed where no flow passes.
    - Raised by the build (the team-grant1-via-self track; the build's ledger, QD-1019). It follows owner decision 344.

576. **A wifi_charging phone keeps all only on an unmetered link while charging, and not in Low Power Mode, in an OS-granted window as in the foreground (option 1, the recommendation, 2026-11-16).**
    - Question: §18.4 had wifi_charging sync all "in an OS-granted window, or while a foreground app or an open tab is on an unmetered link and charging", which the policy read as any window keeping all whatever the link and power, while FS-77 says a phone on Wi-Fi while charging syncs only in a window and that an iOS BGProcessingTask window on cellular fetches no all; a BGProcessingTask can require power and a network but not Wi-Fi, so on iOS a window can open on cellular.
    - Answer: a wifi_charging phone keeps all only on an unmetered link while charging, and not in Low Power Mode or power saving, whether in an OS-granted window or foregrounded: the policy's windows rule is (in a window or foregrounded) and unmetered and charging and not paused. The core does not rely on each host to gate its windows, since a phone's metered data is the person's money (§16.10 debits every byte), so a window on cellular or unplugged fetches no all, and FS-77's window cases are built as its row says (§18.4's windows row).
    - Cost: one condition and its test row.
    - Not taken: (2) any window keeping all, as built, FS-77's case dropped and the hosts told to open windows only on Wi-Fi.
    - Raised by the build (the search-m10-38 track; the build's ledger, QD-1000). It follows owner decision 154.

577. **Amending owner decision 428: an edge client's slot is taken over the edge service's processes on a host, not one warp block's, one of 4 (option 1, the recommendation, 2026-11-17).**
    - Question: owner decision 428 keys an edge client by (environment, host, block, slot, index) and has warp hand each process a slot, 0 or 1, alternating per deploy, so a deploy's old and new process of a block on a host hold different clients, api refusing a slot at or past 2. But §19.4 runs every service as `beta 1 + g1 99` on every lb host, tlsedge and mxedge's router among them, so two processes of each run side by side outside any deploy, passing the same environment, host and block to the client source; warp alternated slots per warp block, so whenever both held slot 0 they asked api for the same K client ids and connected them from two processes. With two slots no assignment keeps the beta and g1 processes apart and a deploy's old and new process apart, since three processes of the block then run on the host.
    - Answer: warp gives each new process of an edge service a slot that no running process of that service on the host holds, listing them by the `warp.env` and `warp.service` labels, not the name prefix, which mxedge-platform's containers share, and the slot count becomes 4 in server's `EdgeClientKeySettings` and warp's `DeploySlotSettings`, api refusing a slot at or past 4, so the beta and g1 processes and a deploy's old and new process of each always hold different clients. Owner decision 428's derivation, its bounded stateless ids and the beta canary stay; its slot 0 or 1 and twice K become slots 0 to 3 and four times K clients a block and host in `urnetwork-edge`. smtpedge, g1 alone, is unaffected, and running the edge blocks as g1 alone stays the fallback should they ever need no canary (§13.6's edge clients, §19.4's blocks).
    - Cost: a settings field on both sides and the scope of warp's listing; up to four times K clients a block and host.
    - Not taken: (2) the warp block in the key, api admitting the warp blocks edge.yml lists for each block, another derivation change and an edge.yml key; (3) the edge blocks as g1 alone, losing the beta block's 1% canary, so a bad tlsedge or router build reaches each host's whole share at once.
    - Raised by the build (the edge-client-deploy-slot track; the build's ledger, QD-1011).

578. **`sidecarctl jwt export-jwks` writes the sockets block's `jwt-jwks.json` from `jwt.yml`, and every change that puts a key version under a path `jwt.yml` lists writes it in the same commit (option 1, the recommendation, 2026-11-17).**
    - Question: the sockets block verifies Client-class tokens with a verify-only keyring read from `jwt-jwks.json` in its `secret_files` (owner decision 347), which `session.ExportByJwtPublicJwks` builds from `jwt.yml`'s key paths, but no command wrote the file. The keys are TLS key versions: a certificate renewal adds one, the issuer starts signing with it 2 h after it reaches the vault, and a sockets block whose file lacks it answers each token signed with it unavailable, as an unknown kid, so every sidecar that refreshed after a rotation would lose sockets until someone updated the file.
    - Answer: one small operations command in server/sidecarctl: `sidecarctl jwt export-jwks -out <file>`, a thin wrapper over `session.ExportByJwtPublicJwks`, run where `jwt.yml` is (`WARP_VAULT_HOME` at the environment's vault), writes the public jwk set (each key's public part, kid and alg), refuses an empty one and prints only kids. Every change that puts a key version under a path `jwt.yml` lists writes the sockets block's `jwt-jwks.json` in the same commit: `sidecarctl certs renew`'s move out of `all/tls.pending` writes it, and xops/certs/renew.md's move by hand gains the export as a step, so the push that delivers a version also delivers its public key to every sockets block, 2 h before the version signs; a §19.10 runbook row says to run the export after any other change to `jwt.yml`. The file stays mounted from `secret_files` and the one binary holds the command (owner decisions 274, 347), and no automated rotation can outrun the block's file (§19.4's sockets row, §19.10's failure table, §19.11's M5 step).
    - Cost: the renew command reads `jwt.yml`'s key paths, which it can since it runs with the vault, and adds a second file to its commit; the move by hand gains one command; the owner runs the export at each environment's vault before the sockets block's first deploy.
    - Not taken: (2) the standalone command alone, run by the owner at each rotation, so an automated renewal every few weeks depends on a manual step, and a missed step breaks sockets for every refreshed sidecar 2 h after the version lands; (3) api serving the by_jwt public keys at an internal route that sockets rereads over its gate bearer, which changes the mount and adds an api dependency to the verifier.
    - Raised by the build (the sock-client-verifier track; the build's ledger, QD-1012). It follows owner decision 347.

579. **Monitor's filecluster-wear selects the storage hosts' SMART series by `job="node"`, the label the host's shared Fluent Bit input gives every series under owner decision 509 (option 1, the recommendation, 2026-11-17).**
    - Question: owner decision 509 has the storage hosts' hourly SMART log ride the host's shared Fluent Bit, whose one node_exporter_metrics input labels every series `job="node"`, but monitor's filecluster-wear still selected `urnetwork_filecluster_smart_*` by `job="file_cluster"`, the label of a second input the one-input rule refuses, so it found no SMART log for any metadata cluster and raised its unobservable alert, and its media-error, spare and rating alerts could not fire.
    - Answer: filecluster-wear selects the SMART series by `job="node"`, in place of `job="file_cluster"`, which no series carries any more: the selector stays as narrow as it is, and the series stay where owner decision 509 puts them. Until the change lands, the signal reads every metadata drive's SMART log as missing and raises its unobservable alert, failing visibly (§19.6's wear row).
    - Cost: one constant in server's monitor, its signal documentation and its probe's fixtures.
    - Not taken: (2) selecting by environment and metric name alone, which a later writer under another job would also feed; (3) the storage hosts keeping `job="file_cluster"` through a second remote-write path, which needs a second input the one-input rule refuses or owner decision 509's rejected option.
    - Raised by the build (the file-cluster-fluent-bit track; the build's ledger, QD-1017). It follows owner decision 509.

580. **Amending owner decision 520: a mail receiver's three session keys, `mail_inbound_message_bytes`, `mail_inbound_recipients` and `mail_inbound_repos_per_message`, hold whatever `QuotaLimits.enforced` says, as the closure floor and pacing do (option 1, the recommendation, 2026-11-17).**
    - Question: owner decision 520 has every local pre-check refuse on a `QuotaLimits` limit only while the answer's `enforced` is true. The mail receiver is a local check of that kind: EHLO advertises SIZE at `mail_inbound_message_bytes`, RCPT past `mail_inbound_recipients` answers `452 4.5.3`, and a recipient adding a repo past `mail_inbound_repos_per_message` answers `452 4.5.3`. But these three also size the session, and the receiver refuses the EHLO with `451 4.3.0` when any reads 0 or -1, never advertising an unbounded SIZE, so reading none while dark would refuse every EHLO, the opposite of dark.
    - Answer: the three keys hold whatever `enforced` says, since they size a session's resources rather than refuse a creation, and mxedge holds the same message cap whatever the switch. The receiver keeps advertising SIZE at `mail_inbound_message_bytes`, answering `452 4.5.3` past the recipient and repo bounds, and refusing the EHLO with `451 4.3.0` when a key reads 0 or -1, never an unbounded SIZE, while the switch is off too (§20.5's switch, §15.5's session).
    - Cost: a Community receiver keeps refusing a message past 25 MiB, and past 100 recipients or 8 repos, while the switch is off; with an absent pro.yml, which reads every key 0, inbound mail is refused at EHLO while dark, as it is when enforced.
    - Not taken: (2) while dark, positive values bounding the session and a key reading 0 or -1 bounding nothing, which leaves SIZE unbounded where a key is missing; (3) while dark every key reading none and the session bounding nothing, a direct-MX receiver's spool and deposits taking any size; (4) bounds of the session's own settings while dark, a second copy of pro.yml's numbers against R-QUOTA-5.
    - Raised by the build (the quota-limits-enforced track; the build's ledger, QD-1023).

581. **`QuotaRefusal` gains an optional `case_id`, set only for `screen_review`, naming the review case the refusal opened or joined (option 1, the recommendation, 2026-11-17).**
    - Question: owner decision 534 refuses a name `ScreenName` sends to review as `QuotaRefusal{limit_key: screen_review}` with the case id, but `QuotaRefusal`, operator-core's type (owner decision 251), its sidecar_http twin and bringyour.yml's schema held only `limit_key`, `limit`, `used`, `retry_after_ms` and `upgrade`, and a quota refusal's body is the whole `QuotaRefusal`, so nothing said where the case id goes: the network could not learn it from the refusal, and `GET /abuse/cases` lists a case only once a reviewer acts.
    - Answer: operator-core's `QuotaRefusal` gains `case_id`: optional, a uuid, omitted unless set, and set only for `limit_key` `screen_review`, with the id of the review case the refusal opened or joined. Its sidecar_http twin and bringyour.yml's `QuotaRefusal` schema gain the field, so record writes and alias claims refused for review answer 429 `{limit_key: screen_review, ..., case_id}`. The field is additive, so clients that do not know it ignore it, and the id is the network's own case, which §21.8 shows its Owners and Admins anyway; the rename routes' `{code, message}` results may keep naming it in their message text (§20.7's refusal body, §21.6's names, §21.8's notice).
    - Cost: a field in operator-core's type, its twin and the schema.
    - Not taken: (2) a body of its own for `screen_review`, a `ScreenReviewRefusal{limit_key, case_id}`, giving clients two shapes for a 429; (3) no case id, so the network learns of the case only when a reviewer acts and cannot name it if it writes in.
    - Raised by the build (the abuse-m5-17 track; the build's ledger, QD-1014). It follows owner decision 534.

582. **`POST /abuse/report`'s 30 an hour per caller address counts each report, each URL and each header block, and refuses a request whole when one would pass the limit (option 1, the recommendation, 2026-11-17).**
    - Question: a request to `POST /abuse/report` holds up to 16 URLs and 4 header blocks, each a report of its own that opens or joins its own case, but the per-address limit counted each request once, before its body was split, so one address could file 30 requests of 20 reports an hour, 600 reports, while §21.16, §21.8's pipeline and bringyour.yml all say reports.
    - Answer: the limit counts each report: a request of n reports, 1 to 20, records n attempts, and the whole request is refused with 429 and Retry-After, nothing taken, when an attempt would pass the limit; every attempt it recorded still counts, since a refused report counts too, and a body refused for its content still counts once before it is read. The route makes n Redis script calls, or uses a weighted variant of the shared attempt check that adds n members in one call, leaving the unweighted form other routes share unchanged. bringyour.yml, §21.16 and api-14 keep their words, and a trusted flagger is counted like any caller, bounded by its own daily kill cap (§21.16's report route).
    - Cost: a campaign of 40 URLs files 30 of them in an hour from one address and waits, or mails `abuse@<root>`; up to 20 Redis calls a request.
    - Not taken: (2) counting each request, as built, bringyour.yml and §21.16 then saying requests, up to 600 reports an hour per caller.
    - Raised by the build (the abuse-report-limit-redis track; the build's ledger, QD-1024). It follows owner decision 536.

583. **A sync round other than "search more"'s asks the windows rule again before each read of all, so a window or foreground round stops fetching all once its link turns metered, its charger is unplugged or Low Power Mode turns on (option 1, the recommendation, 2026-11-17).**
    - Question: owner decision 576 made the search policy's windows rule (in a window or foregrounded) and unmetered and charging and not paused, but the sync engine read the rule once a round, before its read of the all set, and the fetch loop checked only the round's context and the day's background budget before each read, so a phone that started a window or foreground round on Wi-Fi while charging and then left Wi-Fi, was unplugged or entered Low Power Mode read the rest of all under the new conditions until the round or the window's budget ended, up to its all cap, 256 MiB on phones; §18.7 has a window do §18.4's work "while the reported conditions allow it", and owner decision 576's reason, that a phone's metered data is the person's money, applies to the rest of a round as much as to its start.
    - Answer: before each read of the all set in a sync round that is not "search more"'s, the fetch loop asks the ref's rule again; where it no longer allows all, the round ends its fetch of all, what landed stays staged in the plan, and the next window or foreground round the rule allows resumes from there, as a window's end does. A "search more" keeps its own metered wait at the call and is unchanged. FS-77 gains a case: a window on Wi-Fi while charging whose link turns metered after its first shard reads no further shard of all, and the next allowed window resumes (§18.4's windows row, §18.7's window row, FS-77).
    - Cost: one policy read a read of all.
    - Not taken: (2) keeping the per-round reading, §18.4 and the policy saying that a round started under allowed conditions runs to its end, bounded by the cap and the day's background budget.
    - Raised by the build (the search-window-unmetered track; the build's ledger, QD-1026). It follows owner decision 576.

## The owner's answers on the complexity review of 2026-11-17

On 2026-11-17 a complexity review of the build put to the owner, one question at a time, whether each of its heaviest mechanisms still earns its cost. The answers that change a behavior or a rule are numbered as the design-records tracks fold them: the track that folded DESIGN-DETAILS.md sections 1, 11 and 15 numbered the first below, which bears on those sections, and the track that folded sections 8 and 9 the second, which bears on section 9. The track that wrote DESIGN-DEFER.md numbered the third, which moves the client-checked prefix write rules to Later, and the owner's instruction that came with the review, the fourth: every deferred decision is documented in that file.

584. **Every search-index entry is a full key set or a tombstone: the diff kind is reserved and refused, and kept slots, chains, resting diffs, the re-entry of composed edits and the erasure's CLOSURE order go with it (option 1, the recommendation, 2026-11-17).**
    - Question: an edit's index entry could be a diff resting on its writer's earlier full entry, which that writer kept (owner decision 385), and an erasure re-entered an edit resting on an erased version and ordered its CLOSUREs around it (owner decision 395). Diffs held entries in proportion to an edit, about 1.8 MiB of entries a day against about 6.6 MiB all full on a 20-writer git workload, and about 14 merges a day against 50, but they caused five of DESIGN-DETAILS.md section 1's ten findings and three of section 15's seven, FS-107, about 20 of the 54 IndexMerge and IndexErase configurations, the erasure's ordering rules and the remaining work of two tasks.
    - Answer: writers emit full entries and tombstones only, and a decoder refuses kind 2, whose number stays reserved in format 2; chains, kept slots, resting diffs, the re-entry of composed edits and the CLOSURE ordering go, and the re-entry push stays for run-card refolds alone, refused at the storage limit as builds are, with nothing of the erasure held back for it; FS-107 is rewritten for full entries, and the IndexMerge and IndexErase models drop their diff, base and re-entry configurations. It reverses owner decision 385's diff clause and its own-bases rule, whose head rule, merges that read no head and roll-up promotion stand, and owner decision 395, which extended diffs to erasure, its storage-limit hold-back and retry included (§11.13's limit row; §11.14's entry, codes, dependency, merges, reads, erase, limit and drop rows; §11.15, §11.16, D-EVS-46 and §11.21; §2.10's index writer row; §22.6, FI-18, FS-107, §22.8's model row and D-TEST-14 and D-TEST-15).
    - Cost: about 3.7 times the entry bytes and merges on heavily edited file-mode and git-mode refs, so phones following such repos fetch more slot bytes; mail and record entries never diffed, and no trust or privacy goal changes.
    - Not taken: (2) keeping diff entries as owner decisions 385 and 395 had them.
    - Raised by the complexity review of 2026-11-17 (the build's ledger, QD-1036).

585. **Every path-epoch due is batched: no key holder's removal makes a crossing immediate, no due holds a creation or a new name, and a mapper's 2-minute creation block stays, the one creation refusal, with 412 PATH_EPOCH_DUE (option 1 of each question, the recommendations, 2026-11-17).**
    - Question: after a member's or a member device's removal, or a compromise retirement, §11.6's due was immediate: the shared and owned refs of general, config and mailcfg repos refused every new path, 412 PATH_EPOCH_DUE, the crossing push's own creations included (owner decision 229), until a device holding every name uploaded a full map, about 280 MiB a million paths, and evs and its clients classed each crossing from the log between the two generations' pins (owner decisions 414 and 569). The complexity review counted about 10.1k lines and identifiers in 166 files, 15 decisions, 120 KB of DESIGN-DETAILS.md section 9 and a 24-configuration model. Once every due is batched, a mapper's block, which stops creation on a busy ref for at most 2 minutes so that its PATH_EPOCH commits, was the one creation refusal left, and the first answer did not name it.
    - Answer: every path-epoch due is batched. The first commit whose generation pin crosses a REMOVE generation records the due, `due_by_ms` 24 h on, and no due holds a creation or a new name, in the push that crosses it or after; the new epoch lands through the batched path within its window, so a removed holder can test guessed names, with the server's help, for up to 24 h after its removal, contents staying sealed throughout. The immediate and batched classing goes, with owner decisions 229, 414 and 569 and the blocking parts of the defect fixes that named the refs and kinds a due blocked (QD-091, QD-186), and `due_blocks_creation` stays in the format unused, written false and read past. Path epochs stay (owner decision 2's item 9, D-EVS-12): maps, tails, copies, floors and multi-epoch reads are unchanged; deposit and erasure refs stay at epoch 1 for life; and a new ref still starts in the newest epoch its creator holds (owner decision 232), its first names under no removed key, QD-632's question, a first push across an immediate removal, no longer arising, and with it an overlay's first push held while its base ref blocked creation. A mapper's creation block stays as landed: 10 min past `due_by_ms`, once its PATH_EPOCH has been refused for its tail twice, a mapper may stop creation on the ref for at most 2 min, at most once each 10 min, and a creation there meanwhile gets 412 PATH_EPOCH_DUE, which writers wait out; that is the refusal's one use. `K_sock` keeps its own rotation classes (§9.4), which named the path epochs' and no longer do (§11.5's leak bound; §11.6's due, block, commit, read and removal rows; §11.8's 5e; D-EVS-12; §11.21; §5.6's compromise reason, §7.9's recovery rows and §10.5's and §10.8's rotations; §9.4; §19.9's copies row; §22.7's revocation suite, FS-4 and FS-118).
    - Cost: a removed holder's chance to test guessed names lasts up to 24 h after any removal, where a member's or a member device's removal or a compromise had closed it to new names at once; no format change.
    - Not taken: (2) no path epochs, tokens keyed for each ref's life, so a removed holder tests names for good; (3) keeping the immediate due as planned; for the block, (2) removing it too, so a mapper's tail can lose without end on a file-mode ref with steady creation and its epoch miss its window, a removed holder's exposure unbounded there, and (3) answering it 429 WAIT unrecorded, retiring 412 PATH_EPOCH_DUE, a change to §11.8's refuse row and 5e for no gain.
    - Raised by the complexity review of 2026-11-17 (the build's ledger, QD-1046), and its block by the path-epoch-dues-batched track (QD-1057).

586. **The client-checked prefix write rules move to Later: launch uses a repo per prefix as the write boundary, and the drafted rules stay on the branches, dark behind one switch (option 1, the recommendation, 2026-11-17).**
    - Question: `RepoPrefixRules` let a writer be held to path prefixes inside one general repo, every reader checking and no server (Q-EVS-3's v1 half; owner decisions 345, 415 and 416; DESIGN-DETAILS.md section 16). They are advisory at evs: a breaching push commits, and readers ignore it and repair it, and one breaching push stops a shared git-mode ref on every mount until an admin deletes and re-pushes it (owner decision 415). The complexity review counted about 8.2k lines and rule hooks in 54 other files, 12 decisions, 158 KB of design, an 18-configuration TLA+ model, a clause in evs's gate and repair-push quotas.
    - Answer: the rules move to Later. Launch uses D-ACL-11's v0 answer in every network, a repo per prefix, as the write boundary, which servers enforce and which also limits reads; agents keep their owned branches and overlays. The drafted rules stay on the branches, dark behind one switch, `AclPrefixSettings.Enabled`, off: no sidecar files a `RepoPrefixRules`, `ur evs rules` and every tool that would file one refusing and naming the deferral; no reader judges a push, repairs or pin-pushes under rules; the statement keeps its number, SignedType 0x1111, and `AclRepo`'s `prefix_rules = 12`, reserved, and a log refuses one at its position as a type this release does not carry, so the log goes on; and evs's gate drops the clause for a push that creates or re-creates a shared ref, which owner decision 416 added. It defers owner decisions 345, 415 and 416 with 559's filer rule, whose text stays as the design the rules return with, and amends Q-EVS-3 and D-ACL-11; DESIGN-DETAILS.md section 16 is not folded. TEST-M6-03 is held and leaves the needs of TEST-M6-02 and TEST-M10-06 (§8.8, §8.12, D-ACL-11, §8.19; §11.8's refusals row, §11.11's prefix and new refs rows, D-EVS-18, Q-EVS-3, §11.22; §23.4; §5.4's alarm; DESIGN-DEFER.md's acl-prefix-write-rules entry).
    - Cost: no subtree write limit inside one repo or branch; a team that needs one splits the tree into repos and loses atomic commits across the split. What servers see is unchanged.
    - Not taken: (2) keeping the prefix rules as planned.
    - Raised by the complexity review of 2026-11-17 (the build's ledger, QD-1032).

587. **DESIGN-DEFER.md documents every deferred decision, one entry each, and every section a deferral touches points at its entry (option 1, the owner's instruction, 2026-11-17).**
    - Question: deferrals were scattered. Sections and phasing rows said "Later" here and there, §4.18's C4 and C17 and owner decision 246's ciphertext reclaim among them, and some deferrals lived only in the build's ledger, so nothing listed what launch goes without, what stands in its place or what returning it would take.
    - Answer: the owner's words: "All deferred decisions need to be documented in DESIGN-DEFER.md". DESIGN-DEFER.md, beside DESIGN.md, holds one entry for each feature, mechanism, row or task that the owner or the design moves to Later, past launch or to a later version. An entry gives what is deferred, with its ids (owner decisions, D-, Q- and R- items, ledger ids, tasks, FS rows); why; what launch does instead; what is lost; what stays dark in code and where; what is held, tasks and tracks; and what bringing it back would take. It collects every deferral already in DESIGN.md, DESIGN-AMENDMENTS.md, DESIGN-DETAILS.md and the ledger, and owner decision 586's prefix rules; each later deferral adds its entry when it is folded; and the sections a deferral touches point at its entry. The file is generated, as DESIGN.md is: its entries are in `design/source/defer/`, grouped by section, and the build refuses an entry that lacks a field, an entry no section points at and a pointer no entry answers (§4.11; §23.4; each section's phasing subsection).
    - Cost: a deferral is written twice, in its section and in its entry, and a fold that defers something without its entry fails the build.
    - Not taken: none; the owner's instruction was the one option.
    - Raised by the owner on 2026-11-17, with the complexity review (the build's ledger, QD-1033).

The review's other proposals, which the owner declined on 2026-11-17, change no behavior or rule, so they carry no number and change no section's text: outbound internet mail (M9) stays in the plan, its tasks and tracks going ahead and owner decisions 35, 125, 194, 266, 433 and 434 unchanged, both the deferral and the BDAT-only cut declined (QD-1030); evs's DROP of old history stays, owner decisions 173, 183 and 184 with their amendments (204, 396 to 398, 570) and D-EVS-62 to D-EVS-65 unchanged (QD-1031); forwarding stays in every app's VPN component, as owner decision 197 has it with its amendments 215, 216, 485 and 491, rather than moving into `ur daemon` on desktops with iOS forwarding deferred (QD-1037); Team custom-domain certificates stay ordered through a customer-held ACME account and ordering desktops under D-DNS-9's strong mode, owner decisions 39, 424 to 427 and 571 unchanged, and the one-key-per-host step is declined with URnetwork's ordering (QD-1047); `ur tun` keeps capturing the host's default routes under `exits`, as owner decisions 479, 480 and 482 have it, the split-tunnel proposal declined, until owner decision 606 made the capture the person's choice the same day (QD-1048); cross-network shares stay at M6, owner decisions 86, 350, 475 and 476 and D-BRIDGE-14, 15, 17 and 20 unchanged (QD-1049); the opt-in SCIM signer on an admin's indexer stays available at launch, as owner decisions 150 and 321 have it (QD-1050); and owner decision 297's settings rule stands, every limit a settings field and only identifiers constants, its sweep of drafted code staying in the review phase, the narrowing and the sweep-only cut both declined (QD-1051).

## The owner's answers folded last: the complexity review's other answers and the build's late questions (2026-11-17 and 2026-11-18)

The design-records track that swept up every answer still pending on 2026-11-18 numbered these, in the order it folded them. Some are the complexity review's (above), some are the owner's directions that followed it, and the rest are the build's questions, most of them accepted as recommended under the owner's standing acceptance of 2026-11-16. Each entry says which.

588. **sockets authenticates a sidecar with the 15-minute service token that evs, gkm and dns take, in place of a Postgres-free check of Client-class tokens (the owner's answer, 2026-11-17).**
    - Question: owner decisions 347 and 497 had sockets check Client-class tokens with no Postgres, so that it ran through about an hour of Postgres loss. That took a verify-only by_jwt keyring on a hostile-facing block with its own JWKS export (owner decision 578), a Redis revocation record extended with team memberships (owner decision 561), a `sidecar_id` claim re-minted at `POST /sidecar/bind` and adopted by every sidecar and sdk host (owner decision 286's adoption), a 120 s grace key, a rollout switch moved from vault to config, and two owner steps for each environment. The complexity review asked whether the hour was worth that chain.
    - Answer: sockets is a §7.8 service-token audience. An admitted sidecar's client exchanges for a 15-minute token of audience sockets with no scope, and sockets reads `ur_sidecar_id`, `ur_subject_client_id` and `member_id` from it and verifies it offline against the svc- JWKS it mounts, as evs, gkm and dns do. Revoked clients, sidecars and members are refused through the signed `rev/` objects and the removal and projection events §9.8 consumes, and a token's 15 minutes bound the rest. Find still makes no Postgres call. This reverses owner decisions 347 and 497 and the chain that served them: the bind's re-mint of a `sidecar_id` claim and its adoption, the Postgres-free verifier and its verify-only keyring, `revocation_record_enabled` as a step of sockets' rollout, `sidecarctl jwt export-jwks` (owner decision 578), and the revocation record's member fields (owner decision 561) with the §2.8 row a later answer had asked for them (the build's ledger, QD-1022), which is not added. The Redis revocation record stays as api's own paths use it (D-SOCK-10, §9.6's `ur/` tickets, §9.8, §9.15; §7.5's members, §7.8's exchange and `rev/` check; §5.6's enrollment and §5.15's bind; §14.5; §18.7; §19.4's sockets row, §19.10's failure table, §19.11; §23.4's M5 row; §2.8's sock-2, sock-5 and evs-18).
    - Cost: sockets stops taking sidecars' new registrations and Finds about 15 minutes into a Postgres outage, as evs and gkm stop new sessions, instead of about an hour; each sidecar makes one more exchange every 15 minutes; sockets reads `rev/` objects on the first metadata cluster.
    - Not taken: (2) keeping the Postgres-free check of Client-class tokens as owner decisions 347, 497, 561 and 578 had it.
    - Raised by the complexity review of 2026-11-17 (the build's ledger, QD-1029). It reverses the answers to QD-040, QD-797, QD-848, QD-993, QD-1012, QD-1013 and QD-1022.

589. **api's edge-client route answers each `urnetwork-edge` platform client a 15-minute service token of audience sockets beside its jwt (option 1, the recommendation, 2026-11-17).**
    - Question: once sockets took only service tokens of audience sockets (owner decision 588), urnetwork-edge's platform clients, tlsedge's, mxedge's router's and smtpedge's, could not reach it: no sidecar binds them, so the exchange grants them nothing, and they had presented their client jwts.
    - Answer: `POST /internal/gate/edge-client` answers, beside each platform client's jwt, a 15-minute service token of audience sockets naming that client in `urnetwork-edge`: `network_id` and `ur_client_id`, with no sidecar, no member and no scope, signed under the svc- set as the exchange signs. server's `PlatformClientSource` keeps it beside the jwt and renews it at its `RefreshLifeFraction`, and the edge hosts present it to sockets: tlsedge's and mxedge's router's Finds, and smtpedge's `ur/smtpedge` registrations. A removed edge client is refused at sockets by its client `rev/` object. The route's existing checks, the config's block, host, slot and index, still bound what a stolen edge bearer can mint; sockets stays on service tokens alone; no exchange path skips the projection; and there is no new owner step (§9.8's edge clients' tokens, §7.8's exchange, §13.6's edge clients, §14.11's shape, §19.10's failure table).
    - Cost: one more token a client from api's route, renewed every few minutes; since the route's mint reads Postgres, an edge's token is not renewed while Postgres is lost, and its Finds and registrations are refused once the held token ends.
    - Not taken: (2) a Client-class path at sockets for edge clients alone, which keeps a second verifier; (3) the exchange granting sockets to a client no sidecar binds, which skips the projection.
    - Raised by the build (the sockets-service-token track; the build's ledger, QD-1054). It follows owner decision 588.

590. **The sockets ticket keys' `RotationInterval` defaults to 365 days instead of 30 (the owner's answer, 2026-11-17).**
    - Question: owner decision 354 had the operator run `sidecarctl sockets ticket-keygen` every 30 days and place the file in vault by hand, an owner step twelve times a year for each environment.
    - Answer: `RotationInterval` defaults to 365 days. `KeyOverlap` stays as it is, and so does the rule that a key settles from the first read that saw it (owner decision 562). The owner runs the command once a year in each environment. It amends owner decisions 354 and 562 on the interval only (§9.8, §19.17).
    - Cost: a leaked key could forge tickets for up to a year, but a forged ticket alone admits nothing, since §9.6's steps 3 and 6 still check the session's source and the listener's reading of the config.
    - Not taken: (2) keeping 30 days.
    - Raised by the complexity review of 2026-11-17 (the build's ledger, QD-1039).

591. **taskworker's reap of idle top-level clients leaves `urnetwork-edge`'s clients alone (option 1, the recommendation, 2026-11-17).**
    - Question: `RemoveDisconnectedNetworkClients` marks a top-level client idle after 30 days without a connection and removes it, so an edge deploy slot unused for 30 days lost its derived clients, and api's edge-client mint, which now answers a client only once it finds it active (owner decision 589), refused the next deploy into that slot.
    - Answer: the reaper skips the clients of sidecar.yml's `urnetwork_edge` in its idle marking. An edge slot unused for 30 days can still be deployed into, and the mint finds its derived clients active. The set stays bounded by the config: the slot count times K, for each block and host. An operator's removal still ends a client, and a host dropped from the config keeps its clients until an operator removes them (§13.6's edge clients, §19.4).
    - Cost: clients of a host dropped from the config stay until an operator removes them.
    - Not taken: (2) the mint re-creating a reaped client under its derived id, which revives a removed client's id; (3) a heartbeat that keeps unused slots' clients connected.
    - Raised by the build (the edge-clients-sockets-token track; the build's ledger, QD-1059). It follows owner decision 589.

592. **A compromise cascade's void of a member's recovery binding leaves no binding and restores no earlier one, and the restored kinds leave the format before it freezes (option 1, the recommendation, 2026-11-17).**
    - Question: owner decision 149 had a void restore the member's newest earlier binding not void, whose scoped admissions activated through the 72 h recovery window with no counted cosign, and owner decisions 314 and 556 gave those admissions two proposal kinds of their own, `PROPOSAL_KIND_RESTORED` (3) and `PROPOSAL_KIND_RESTORED_COSIGNED` (4), which every base carried in `ProjectionHeader.restored_cosigned_ids`. That let a member whose code a thief re-bound still self-admit with the old code, a case that arises only when the stolen device held no live presence key, since otherwise owner decision 189's countersignature blocks the rebind. The complexity review counted two kinds every base carries, restored branches in three files, clauses in four fault schedules and two decisions.
    - Answer: restored bindings are removed before M2's format freeze. A void of a member's binding in effect leaves the member no binding, nothing earlier restored: no code of the member's admits a device or takes a wrap, and the member's devices re-seal `keychain.wrap` with no HPKE copy and alarm, as owner decision 149's re-seal has it. The member binds a fresh code from any of its devices outside the cascade (`ur keychain recovery-rotate`, a `RecoveryBind` whose `prev_sha256` is empty), or an admin admits a new device, which stays parked for owner-bound repos (owner decision 126); `ur login --recover` refuses a code with no binding in effect and says so. `ProposalKind` keeps authority (1) and recovery_scoped (2), its values 3 and 4 reserved and a row or cosign of either refused, so no base carries a restored kind, `restored_cosigned_ids` is never declared, and a base whose own head changes `self_admit` changes no kind in its tail, neither kind reading the setting once fixed. It reverses owner decision 149's restore clause, whose re-seal and backup rule stand, owner decision 314's restored kind, whose rows of open proposals and run-once activations stand, and owner decision 556; FS-16 and §22.7's revocation suite lose their restored-binding clauses (§7.9's scoped, commit, wrapped, self_admit, cascade, base, verdicts, Recovery, pending, activation, anchor and after rows and the `ProposalKind` and `ProjectionHeader` fences; §5.6's genesis, signers, alarm and reads rows, §5.9's keychain and recovery code; §10.5's wrapped row, KF2 and §10.8; §18.5's `GetPending`; §2.8's api-7; §22.7, FS-16).
    - Cost: a member whose code a thief re-bound from a device with no live presence key, and who has no other device outside the cascade, recovers only by an admin's admission, parked for owner-bound repos until a consented device confirms it, and in a network where that member is the only admin, not at all.
    - Not taken: (2) keeping restored bindings as owner decisions 149, 314 and 556 had them.
    - Raised by the complexity review of 2026-11-17 (the build's ledger, QD-1034). It reverses the answers to QD-229, QD-952 and QD-1005.

593. **operator-core holds one process-wide `Env`, which every service process server launches sets at startup and which code with no `Env` of its own reads (the owner's instruction, 2026-11-17).**
    - Question: owner decision 251 made operator-core's `Env` the host contract and had an extension hold only service instances an `Env` creates, never global service state, but server's taskworker and api router call their work and handlers with no `Env`, and no server process built one yet: the service mains built `sidecar.ServiceHost` through the host packages.
    - Answer: the owner's words: "Regarding the operator-core Env struct, there should be the ability to set the global env and a GlobalEnv(). Each service launched from server should set the SetGlobalEnv(...)." operator-core gains `SetGlobalEnv(env Env)` and `GlobalEnv() Env`, the package's one piece of process-wide state. `GlobalEnv` answers the `Env` last set; before any set it answers an `Env` whose every accessor refuses with an error wrapping `ErrNotGranted`, no global env having been set, never a nil, so the accessor contract holds, and `SetGlobalEnv(nil)` puts that refusing `Env` back, which tests use to restore. Every service process the server repo launches builds its `Env` at startup through server/core, `NewProcessEnv` under `ProcessGrants`, which names every such process, and sets it before it serves. Engines that already take an `Env` or a `ServiceHost` keep taking it; sidecar/work and sidecar/handler read the global `Env` and pass it to sidecar/model and sidecar/controller (owner decision 594). It amends owner decision 251's rule against global service state for this one `Env` (§4.4's operator-core tree, §4.5's host contract, D-REPO-3, §4.18's seam row).
    - Cost: one process-wide variable in operator-core, which a process that forgets to set it leaves refusing every service, and which a test that sets it restores.
    - Not taken: none; the owner's instruction was the one option.
    - Raised by the owner on 2026-11-17 (the build's ledger, QD-1027).

594. **sidecar holds its server-hosted code in sidecar/model, sidecar/controller, sidecar/work and sidecar/handler, mirroring server's packages: model's and controller's functions take the `Env` first, and work and handler read the global `Env` (the owner's instruction, 2026-11-17).**
    - Question: the sidecar branch's server-side code lived in server's own packages, server/model, server/controller, server/api's handlers and server/taskworker, about 600 new files beside the host packages, reaching Postgres and Redis through server's package-level functions, and §4.18 said only that the call sites and sidecar's SQL and migrations move from server into sidecar in the review phase, not where they land.
    - Answer: the owner's words: "we should use a similar sidecar/{model,controller,work,handler} directory pattern for model, controller, taskworker work, and api handlers", with model and controller "functions that take the env as the first arg" and work and handler using "the global env". sidecar/model holds tables and queries, sidecar/controller logic, sidecar/work the taskworker's work and sidecar/handler api's handlers. Every exported function in model and controller takes the `Env` (`operator.Env`) as its first argument and reaches Postgres, Redis, blobs and the other host services only through it, never through server's package-level functions; work and handler, which server's taskworker and api router call with no `Env` of their own, take `operator.GlobalEnv()` (owner decision 593) and pass it down. server keeps only the hosting: its service mains set the global `Env`, its router mounts sidecar/handler, its taskworker registers sidecar/work, and its migration runner applies sidecar's schema. This is the layout §4.18's review-phase move lands in. The move is planned now, as an inventory of the sidecar branch's server-side files with the package each goes to and the server imports each must drop, in ordered move tracks, and it runs as a barrier once the draft lanes drain, as the owner's next-phase instructions schedule it, after the owner's choices from the complexity review of 2026-11-17, so that code they drop is not moved (§4.4's tree, §4.5's split, D-REPO-3, §4.18).
    - Cost: the review phase moves several hundred files across two repositories in ordered tracks, each dropping the server imports its files used; until then the code stays where it is.
    - Not taken: none; the owner's instruction was the one option.
    - Raised by the owner on 2026-11-17 (the build's ledger, QD-1028). It follows owner decision 593 and places owner decision 251's review-phase move.

595. **The real release fixture's copies name their one commit main, so X9's rule compares connect's release edit against the commit it started from, and the real fixture runs in a go test of its own under a 3 h limit, in run.sh's BUILD_TEST and at sidecar-gate's item alike (the recommendations, 2026-11-17).**
    - Question: with owner decision 555's fixes, the real release fixture ran on through sidecar's whole `make check`, about an hour on this host and growing with the tree, past run.sh's BUILD_TEST limit of 60 minutes and sidecar-gate's 40-minute test limit at M1-out's release-fixture item, and its copy of connect held no main, so X9's rule, which compares connect's release edit against main, found nothing to compare against and failed, with a C18-before-C13 finding beside it.
    - Answer: the fixture's commitCopy also names its one commit main, as the host's clone has main below the version branch, so X9 compares connect's release edit against the commit it started from; REPO-M0-04's reading, that a checkout without main fails, stays, and the C18-before-C13 finding goes with it; one more real fixture run, about 70 minutes on this host, shows `TestX9` passing there (QD-1045). run.sh's BUILD_TEST runs the real fixture in its own go test under a 3 h limit and the small planted runs in another under the 60-minute limit, which keeps a tight hang guard for them, and build/README.md's BUILD_TEST line says so (QD-1056). sidecar-gate's gates.yml test entries take an optional limit, a `timeout:` field beside `tags:`, which the runner uses for both the `-timeout` argument and the command's context bound; the release-fixture item, `TestSidecarReleaseFixture` under `sidecar_release_fixture`, sets 3h, and every other entry keeps `gateSettings.TestTimeout`'s 40-minute default (QD-1058) (§4.9's fixture row; §22.4's release fixture).
    - Cost: a hung real fixture holds a release or a gate report for up to 3 h; two BUILD_TEST lines in place of one, and a field in gates.yml with its test.
    - Not taken: for QD-1045, (2) X9 skipping its main comparison on a release tree and reporting the skip, so connect's additive check at release rests on the branch builds alone; for QD-1056, (1) one 3 h limit for every BUILD_TEST run and (2) keeping 60 minutes until the release host is timed; for QD-1058, (2) a 3 h default for every gate test and (3) leaving the item failing on this host.
    - Raised by the build (the release-layout-test-fixes and build-test-fixture-limits tracks; the build's ledger, QD-1045, QD-1056 and QD-1058). It follows owner decision 555.

596. **The `ios_extension` leg's closure skips a short reviewed list of deliberate seams, each with its reason: the seven registration files that set a hook from `init` while tagged off the leg, `evs_read_provenance.go` first (option 1 of each question, the recommendations, 2026-11-18).**
    - Question: the leg's list, check/testdata/ios_extension_leg.txt, is the forwarding role's file closure, which also takes every file whose `init` writes a variable a closure file declares, and what that file needs. `evs_read_provenance.go`, which QD-851's seam keeps off the leg by tag so the forwarding role links no agent-subkey check, set such a hook, and the closure then named it though the leg compiles none of it; the closure's log then showed six more, `directory_card.go`, `directory_format2.go`, `directory_removal.go`, `directory_revoke.go`, `identity_cleanup.go` and `record_agent_body.go`, which pulled in 19 more files no extension leg links, 26 names in all, and before the index split one such chain had carried the index descriptor onto the leg unseen.
    - Answer: QD-851's seam stays: `evs_read_provenance.go` keeps `//go:build !ios_extension`, and on the leg a read gives no provenance. `check/`'s `closureRegistrations` skips a registration file named on a short reviewed list of deliberate seams, each with its reason, and all seven join it; the leg's list drops the 26 names and names again only what the leg links, and the rule's guard holds for every other `init`. The forwarding leg keeps the behavior it has: owner decision 197's forwarding role runs only IP and port forwarding, and none of the seven serves it (§4.4's leg files; D-SDK-20).
    - Cost: a reviewed list of seven seams to keep honest, with a planted twin in `check/`.
    - Not taken: (2) taking the tags off, so the leg links provenance reads, directory judging and repair, the format-2 writer, moderation alarms, erasure judging, the cleanup approval kind and agent-body sealing it never asks for, on a leg with about 1.17 MB of headroom; (3) leaving the list naming files the leg does not compile.
    - Raised by the build (the ios-leg-reader-budget and ios-leg-index-split tracks; the build's ledger, QD-1063 and QD-1065).

597. **smtpedge's image gives its binary `CAP_NET_ADMIN` as a file capability that a small static Go helper sets in a build stage, and a release check refuses an image whose binary lacks it (option 2, the recommendation, 2026-11-18).**
    - Question: smtpedge's block needs `SO_MARK`, so its binary needs `CAP_NET_ADMIN` while it runs as uid 65532 (D-OPS-1), and the image had no way to set it: the standard tool, setcap, is in no reviewed runtime package.
    - Answer: a small static Go program built in the server repo writes the binary's `security.capability` extended attribute in a build stage, running from a bind mount so it never ships. No new package is added and nothing is fetched, and D-OPS-1's uid and the reviewed runtime package list stay as they are. A test in server/cli/smtpedge reads the built attribute back as getcap would, and a release check refuses an smtpedge image whose binary lacks the capability, since the failure otherwise shows only at the first dial, as EPERM at `SO_MARK` (§19's smtpedge capability).
    - Cost: a few dozen lines that encode the kernel's capability attribute format, held to what getcap reads by the test.
    - Not taken: (1) `libcap2-bin` in smtpedge's build stage and setcap there, one more reviewed package; (3) running smtpedge as root with every capability but NET_ADMIN dropped, against D-OPS-1; (4) an ambient capability through a root entrypoint that drops to 65532.
    - Raised by the build (the ops-m9-01 track; the build's ledger, QD-1066).

598. **FS-86's card commits are judged on T3's 4-host file-cluster layout with nothing else running on the machine, over a whole peak hour at the Team limit's rate in `sidecar-t3.sh nightly`, and the local stack's run is the reduced-scale form (option 1, the recommendation, 2026-11-18).**
    - Question: FS-86's card-commit row, §16.11's cards ref commits, missed its p99 ceilings on the local stack, p99 413 ms at the peak and 1.30 s at 3x against 300 ms and 1 s, measured on the private Redis and the local stack's one MinIO while five lanes' builds held the machine at a load average of about 50, and nothing said which stores and conditions judge the row.
    - Answer: the row is judged on T3's 4-host file-cluster layout (server/local/file-cluster-t3) with nothing else running on the machine, the reading a whole peak hour at the Team limit's rate in `sidecar-t3.sh nightly`. The local stack's run stays as the reduced-scale form and is no gate reading: its Team-limit test logs a p99 miss and fails nothing, as the stress case already does. The numbers of 2026-11-18 are recorded as a loaded machine's; a sharded cards ref is not considered unless the quiet run misses; and the miss does not block M10's Gn-out until the quiet reading is taken (FS-86, §22.8's directory row, D-TEST-17).
    - Cost: the gate reading needs the quiet T3 run, which the review phase takes.
    - Not taken: (2) taking the local stack's numbers as the reading, blocking the gate and designing a cards ref sharded by sidecar_id now; (3) measuring p99 at evs instead of from the client's arrival to its write's return.
    - Raised by the build (the record-m10-04 track; the build's ledger, QD-1068).

599. **`DropCache` also resets the sync engine's loaded manifest, plan and version table for each ref it drops, so the next round fetches again and a search of that ref finds its hits (option 1, the recommendation, 2026-11-17).**
    - Question: `SearchEngine.DropCache` removed the cache's files and its ref records, but the sync engine kept each followed ref's manifest and plan in memory and counted every file they listed as held, so after a drop no round fetched anything again and every search of that ref found nothing, with no error, until a restart.
    - Answer: `DropCache` resets the sync engine's state for the dropped repos, every followed ref of a repo or every ref for nil: a `SearchSync` method takes each ref's round lock and clears what it loaded, the manifest, the plan and the version table, and `DropCache` calls it after the cache drop, so the next round reloads from the emptied cache and fetches the latest set, plus all where it is kept. In the review phase, `SearchCache.Evict`, the caps' eviction, and `SearchPolicy.EvictUnused` are checked for the same gap and, if they leave it, take option 2's check there (§5.4's `DropSearchCache`, FS-77).
    - Cost: a reset method and its call; the eviction paths' check waits for the review phase.
    - Not taken: (2) each round checking the cache's holdings at its start and treating as missing any file the manifest or plan names that the cache no longer holds, a listing on every round.
    - Raised by the build (the search-window-unmetered track; the build's ledger, QD-1025). It follows owner decision 583.

600. **On a gap head, a reader takes a recorded path that evs serves absent, or serves its removal, with no alarm and drops its record (option 1 of each question, the recommendations, 2026-11-17).**
    - Question: owner decision 566 has a reader take a plain absent reply where its walk's newest change of the path, or of a prefix holding it, is a removal, but a device behind the catch-up budget re-pins at the head and walks nothing before the re-pin, so a path it had read and recorded, then removed and its removal collected and paged out, met a record it could neither confirm nor drop: its next read was refused, and so was a read of a path served its own removal past the record's pin.
    - Answer: on a gap head, a re-pin past the budget, `finishRead` takes a recorded path that evs serves absent, or serves its removal, with no alarm, and drops its record. The gap already marks the versions before the re-pin as evs's word, so a path this device read and then lost track of reads as evs serves it, as a never-read path does (Q-EVS-6), and a later version evs serves of it is held to the walk alone. A version served below the record is still refused, gap or not (§11.9's reader row).
    - Cost: an evs that hides a standing path as absent, or as removed, from a device far enough behind to gap goes unalarmed there; reading a removed path costs at most one gap.
    - Not taken: for QD-1041, keeping the record and refusing the read, which leaves the device stuck on the path; for QD-1055, dropping only a path served absent, so a path served its removal past the record's pin stays refused.
    - Raised by the build (the absent-walked-removal-followups tracks; the build's ledger, QD-1041 and QD-1055). It follows owner decision 566.

601. **Beside a reply that serves a path no version, removal or base, evs sends, in each walked statement whose change list is under `changes_root`, the present of the path's own change and of a strict prefix's removal (option 1, the recommendation, 2026-11-17).**
    - Question: owner decision 566's reader takes a plain absent reply where its walk's newest change of the path, or of a prefix holding it, is a removal, and under `changes_root` it takes a present, an inclusion proof to the statement's root, of the path's own change or a strict prefix's removal; but evs sent, for each walked statement whose list is under `changes_root`, adjacent leaves where the statement left the path alone and a present only for a strict prefix's delta, so where the removal's statement had its list under `changes_root`, as a push past the inline bound does, the reader had no proof of it and refused the reply, with no alarm.
    - Answer: for a path it serves with no version, removal or base, evs sends, in each walked rooted statement's absence, the present of the path's own change, a write or a removal, and of a strict prefix's removal; protocol/EVS-FORMAT.md's present row widens to say so. It needs no new store read, since the push's record is read already for that statement's adjacent leaves, and adds one inclusion proof for each such statement to the reply. Owner decision 566's reading then holds for removals made under `changes_root` as for inline lists (§11.9's reply and reader rows).
    - Cost: one more inclusion proof a walked statement on such replies.
    - Not taken: (2) leaving evs as built, so a device pinned below a collected, paged-out removal made under `changes_root` cannot read or re-create the path.
    - Raised by the build (the evs-absent-walked-removal track; the build's ledger, QD-1043). It follows owner decision 566.

602. **While the reseed gate is on, evs's place route writes `repo/` deleted at creation for a repo re-seeded as deleted, and the device's groups step mends a `repo/` that stands keying by activate and then DELETE (option 1 of each question, the recommendations, 2026-11-17 and 2026-11-18).**
    - Question: owner decision 565 lets a re-seed's segment uploads into a repo re-seeded as deleted through only while `repo/` shows deleted, refusing them 403 SCOPE while it is keying or active, but evs's place route always wrote `repo/` keying, the reseed route moved an active one to deleted only at its first call, and the device's walk uploads each version's segments before that version's call, so the walk's first upload met `repo/` active and stopped, and where every ref's first version carried segments nothing of the repo came back. And a `repo/` left keying by a recovery that placed it before the fix had no edge to deleted.
    - Answer: while the reseed gate is on, evs's place route writes `repo/` deleted{deletion_id, its entry's time, signer} at creation for a repo whose projection ends in an unrestored, dated `RepoDelete` without `now` inside its 7 d restore window, as §19.10's groups row says, and refuses one past restoring with 409 SHREDDING; gkm already admits a re-seed's GroupForm and ladder for a deleted `repo/` while reseed is on, and the device does not activate such a repo, activate answering REPO_DELETED. The walk's order, segments then call, stays, and owner decision 565's refusal of a keying or active `repo/` never meets an honest walk (QD-1062). The device's groups step, for a repo its state shows deleted, also sends activate and takes 409 REPO_DELETED as the expected answer; a 200 says `repo/` stood keying and is now active, and the step then calls DELETE, the route alone with no new statement, which moves active to deleted{deletion_id, its entry's time, signer} under the projected `RepoDelete`, waiting out STATEMENT_MISSING while evs's projection trails, using only §11.12's existing transitions (QD-1064) (§11.12's creation, §19.10's groups row).
    - Cost: a change to evs's place route and the device's groups step, with a T2 test of the walk for a repo re-seeded as deleted; one more activate call for each deleted repo a recovery re-seeds.
    - Not taken: for QD-1062, (2) the walk sending each version's call before its segments, a refused call for every version with new segments, and (3) a new reseed call form that only applies §11.13's repos rule, a format change; for QD-1064, an edge from keying to deleted, a new transition.
    - Raised by the build (the reseed-segments-deleted-admin and place-deleted-at-creation tracks; the build's ledger, QD-1062 and QD-1064). It follows owner decision 565.

603. **Only a `versions` page judges a durable topic's fall: a reset that shows one below the version held applies its other topics and sends the client to `versions` once, whose page alarms if the fall is real (option 3, the recommendation, 2026-11-17).**
    - Question: owner decision 299 judges a `versions` page by the inputs the machine took after its refetch began, so a page that is only late raises no `head_alarm`, but the mirror case was not covered: a resident's reset or a poll's that applied after a page could show a durable topic (`team`, `dns`, `gkm:<group_id>`) below what the page raised, since nothing orders api's read of a page against a resident's read of `{scp_<C>}ver`, and the sync routes read `team` and `dns` from stores the resident's reset does not, so an honest race raised `head_alarm` of kind `rollback` and closed §8's inbound access until reconciled.
    - Answer: a reset, a resident's or a poll's, that shows a durable topic below the version held applies its other topics and sends the client to `versions` once; that page, read from the stores that never fall, alarms if the fall is real, by the rules owner decision 299 left in place. §6.8's "a lower or conflicting one alarms" becomes "sends the client to versions, whose page alarms" (§6.8's state rules).
    - Cost: one more refetch for each such reset, and a rollback alarms one refetch later; a lower durable version that a resident presents and api does not is no longer alarmed, though the client never applies it either way.
    - Not taken: (1) alarming on every such reset, as built, so honest races alarm; (2) ordering a page against the resident's read, which nothing in the push path can.
    - Raised by the build (the push-late-page-judge track; the build's ledger, QD-1044). It follows owner decisions 299 and 300.

604. **A copy that no longer opens an erasure request's own marker entry, while a later marker of the repo stands, takes that request as superseded (option 3, the recommendation, 2026-11-17).**
    - Question: owner decision 472 keeps a directory erasure request lapsed once a full item of its repo stands above its marker, derived from the log, but after a revoke, an announce again and a second revoke, the second request's ERASEs take every copy below its marker, the announcement included, so a device that never opened that announcement, a directory admin device admitted after the erasure, one whose read was lost or an offline revoker, read the first request as standing, raised a false `directory_erasure` past 24 h and could credit a later sweep ERASE to it.
    - Answer: a local superseded rule, derived from what every copy holds: an erasure request whose own marker entry this copy no longer opens, while a later marker of the repo stands, is superseded. The runner does not work it, does not show it as pending and raises no `directory_erasure` for it; later copies go under the newer request, whose ERASEs cover every copy the older one could name, and its revoker reads it as lapsed. This also covers a request whose marker a later request erased before the older one was worked. There is no format change, and owner decision 472's outcome holds on every device (§16.11's revoke row).
    - Cost: one rule in the erasure runner's check and in the revoker's state.
    - Not taken: (1) accepting the false alarm and documenting it; (2) carrying the lapse in the log, an `ErasureBody` field naming the lapsed requests, a record.proto format change for an edge.
    - Raised by the build (the directory-erasure-lapse-stays track; the build's ledger, QD-1061). It follows owner decision 472.

605. **A directory announcement's index row keeps the times its content-changing version held, and a check by rebuild of the cards ref's index compares an announcement row less its `expires` (option 1, the recommendation, 2026-11-18).**
    - Question: §16.11 puts an announcement's `expires` in its index row and has a renewal write no index entry, so an hour after a run's last content change its row's expiry trails the item's, and a rebuild from the card as it stands, which holds the renewed expiry, would not equal the incremental index, so §11.14's check by rebuild would read every renewed item as a forged entry.
    - Answer: the row keeps the times the content-changing version held; a renewal keeps it and writes no index entry, as §16.11's versions row says. A check by rebuild of the cards ref's index compares an announcement row less its `expires`: the row's other bytes, its keys and its version must match. `search_agents` keeps reading the fold for the current expiry. There is no format change, and FS-86's write rate on the cards ref is unchanged (§16.11's versions row, §11.14's determinism row).
    - Cost: a named exception in the rebuild comparison for one field of one document class; the check by rebuild itself is review-phase work.
    - Not taken: (2) writing 0 in an announcement row's `expires`, so a remote or bucket-only search says nothing about activity without the cards; (3) an index entry at every renewal, about 24 times a run's index writes on the ref FS-86 holds to a ceiling.
    - Raised by the build (the record-m10-04 track; the build's ledger, QD-1067).

606. **`ur tun`'s capture of the host's default routes is the person's choice: two per-network settings, default egress and route local traffic, decide it, and the tun takes the default routes only with default egress on and an egress destination (the owner's instruction, 2026-11-17).**
    - Question: owner decisions 479, 480 and 482 had `ur tun` take the host's IPv4 and IPv6 default routes under `exits` unconditionally, by fwmark rules with connect's `SO_MARK` on Linux, the /1 halves on macOS and Windows, throw or host routes for carved addresses and IPv6 refused and counted, which the owner had kept the same day (QD-1048, declined with the complexity review's other proposals), while the sdk's apps already have a per-device `routeLocal` for traffic with no destination and the sidecar client had no counterpart.
    - Answer: the owner's words: "`ur tun` routing the default routes should be optional. If the sidecar client does not have a destination and route local traffic is enabled, default traffic should not use the tun. The user should have the option to turn on and off the default egress destination." The sidecar client gains two settings that `ur tun` sets and shows, kept for each network: default egress, on unless the person turns it off, whether the host's default traffic goes to the egress destination through the tun; and route local traffic, on by default as the sdk's `DefaultRouteLocal` is, the client's counterpart of the sdk's `routeLocal`. `ur tun` takes the default routes, both families, with owner decisions 479, 480 and 482's rules, only when default egress is on and the client has an egress destination: under `exits` an exit of the network it can use, under `auto` the connect client's destination. With no destination and route local traffic on, default traffic does not use the tun: the host keeps its own default routes and the tun carries only the block, the granted prefixes and private names. With default egress off, default traffic stays on the host's routes in every mode. The orchestrator's reading of the case the instruction leaves open: with no destination and route local traffic off, default traffic is captured and refused as before, failing closed. A change of either setting, or a destination that comes or goes, re-applies the routes without restarting the tun. The network's egress mode stays the admin's policy for traffic that enters URnetwork, §8.10's gate unchanged. It amends owner decisions 479, 480 and 482, under which the capture was unconditional under `exits` (§17.11's `ur tun` row and its default routes; §8.10).
    - Cost: two settings a network and their status lines; a host whose person turns default egress off sends its default traffic outside URnetwork, which owner decision 607 lets a network forbid.
    - Not taken: none; the owner's instruction was the one option.
    - Raised by the owner on 2026-11-17 (the build's ledger, QD-1052).

607. **Under `exits`, a network's admin, owner or sponsor may require default egress on, in a field of the `BridgeEgress` statement's sealed exits body, and an `egress_change` action carries that requirement in a field of its own, so its approval shows and binds it (option 1 of each question, the recommendations, 2026-11-17).**
    - Question: with default egress a device's choice (owner decision 606), a member could turn it off on a network whose admin set `exits` so that members' internet traffic leaves through the network's exits (R-BRIDGE-7); and an `egress_change` action carried only owner decision 462's fields, its subject (29), `egress_mode` (39) and its exits in `grant_dst`, so an approver would not see such a requirement, and a filing that differed from another only by it would reuse the other's decision.
    - Answer: under `exits`, a network's admin, owner or sponsor can require default egress on, as `BridgeEgressBody.require_default_egress = 4`, sealed with the rest of the exits body, so servers learn nothing new and §2.8's api-7 row is unchanged. A device whose network requires it keeps default egress on for that network; `ur tun` shows the lock and refuses to turn default egress off, naming the network's requirement.
